# TO THE MOST ILLUSTRIOUS

## AND MOST POWERFUL PRINCE, LORD CHARLES OF LORRAINE, DUKE OF ELBEUF,
Peer of France, Knight of both Orders <note>The elite Order of Saint Michael and the Order of the Holy Spirit</note>, Counselor of the King’s Council in both public and private affairs, Count of Harcourt and Lillebonne, Lord of Rieux, Rochefort, etc. Also Commander of a hundred <term>Cataphracts <note>original: "Cataphractorum," referring to heavy armored cavalrymen, a term Morestel uses to evoke classical Roman military prestige</note></term>, Pierre Morestel <note>Petrus Morestellus</note>, Doctor of Theology, sends his most humble greetings.

No passage of time (most powerful Prince) will ever erase from memory the twenty-eighth of June <note>original: "quartum Kal. Iulij," the fourth day before the Kalends of July</note>, which committed your name to immortality at the siege of Saint-Jean-d'Angély <note>original: "obsidione Angeliana," a key 1621 military action during the Huguenot rebellions where the Duke of Elbeuf served under King Louis XIII</note>. For you kindled the spirits of the soldiers by fighting yourself, rather than merely by encouraging them. The cavalry and infantry forces know this; indeed, the Royal Majesty himself knows what great-heartedness—

<summary>This page contains a formal dedication by the theologian Pierre Morestel to Charles of Lorraine, Duke of Elbeuf, praising the Duke's military bravery at the 1621 Siege of Saint-Jean-d'Angély.</summary>
<keywords>Charles of Lorraine, Duke of Elbeuf, Pierre Morestel, Siege of Saint-Jean-d'Angély, 17th-century dedication, French nobility, military history</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication by Pierre Morestel to Charles of Lorraine, Duke of Elbeuf. The previous page ended mid-word, praising the Duke's bravery at the 1621 Siege of Saint-Jean-d'Angély, where he was wounded while leading his troops. This page transitions from praising his military valor to recommending the comfort of literature and study during his recovery.</meta>

# EPISTLE

—your great-heartedness <note>original: "magnanimitatem," continuing from the previous page</note>; than which nothing is more noble, he [the King] has recognized. A notable sign of your excellent spirit is the wound inflicted upon you at the very front, from which we hope (with God’s help) you will shortly recover. 

But meanwhile, so that the force of your pain may fade, what is to be done? One must take refuge in literature, from which delight is always sought. "Other pursuits," says the Orator <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero, in his famous speech *Pro Archia Poeta*</note>, "do not belong to all times, nor all ages, nor all places. These studies nourish youth, delight old age, adorn prosperity, and provide a refuge and solace in adversity; they delight us at home, do not hinder us abroad, stay with us through the night, travel with us, and go with us to the countryside." 

Have not many, when they were in the power of enemies and tyrants, in custody or in exile, eased their pain by satisfying their innate desire for learning through the study of doctrine? Innate, I say; for among such a great variety of living creatures, the desire for cognition and knowledge is implanted in man alone. For this reason, the Greeks rightly used the term *lights* <note>original: "φῶτα" (*phōta*). This is a classical Greek pun: *phōs* means "man," while *phōs* (plural *phōta*) means "light." Morestel suggests that humans are defined by the light of their intellect</note> to describe them; for it consumes the darkness of ignorance, to know and to [recognize...] <note>The text breaks off mid-word: "no-" likely intended for *noscere* (to know/recognize)</note>

<summary>Morestel comforts the wounded Duke by citing Cicero on the value of literature and study as a refuge during recovery. He argues that the pursuit of knowledge is the defining "light" of humanity that dispels the darkness of ignorance.</summary>

<keywords>Cicero, Pro Archia, humanism, pursuit of knowledge, healing, Siege of Saint-Jean-d'Angély, Charles of Lorraine, enlightenment</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication by Pierre Morestel to Charles of Lorraine, Duke of Elbeuf. The previous page discussed the innate human desire for knowledge and the "light" of the intellect. This page transitions into the philosophical necessity of self-knowledge as the highest form of learning, specifically focusing on the famous Delphic maxim "Know Thyself."</meta>

# DEDICATION
<sig>a iij</sig>

—to become known <note>original: "...tescere," likely from the verb "agnoscere" (to recognize) or "notescere" (to become known) starting on the previous page</note>, **to know and to be known**: however, one knowledge is necessary to him—the knowledge of himself. For unless he knows this, what does it profit

*To have attempted the airy homes, and in mind*
*To have traversed the deep pole?* <note>Morestel quotes a classical poet to emphasize that studying the heavens or the physical world is useless if one does not understand their own nature.</note>

This is commanded by the voice ascribed to the **Pythian Oracles** <note>The prophecies delivered by the priestess at Delphi</note>: **KNOW THYSELF** <note>original: "NOSCE TE." This is the Latin translation of the Greek "Gnothi Seauton," famously inscribed on the Temple of Apollo</note>. It is a brief saying indeed, but *a matter so great as only Jupiter may know*, as the **ancient poet** <note>This refers to the Roman satirist Juvenal, who claimed this maxim was so profound it must have descended from heaven</note> says. Yet man, according to the measure of his own intellect, can grasp and know himself, as the **Oracle of Apollo** intends. For there is a threefold ignorance of oneself: in **Wealth**, in the **Body**, and in the **Soul**. Consequently, there is a threefold knowledge in those same areas.

For does a man look upon his wealth? Let him know that he does not truly see his own property; riches depart, they come, and they are the plaything of **Fortune**. Only wealth? Honors also. Therefore, let him recognize these things and what follows: not to puff himself up because of them.

The second branch of knowledge regards that part of himself which is the **Body**: a shattered vessel, a lamp placed in the wind; it needs only a nudge, not a storm, to be destroyed.

The third and most principal knowledge touches the soul: **KNOW THYSELF**, surely from the most beautiful...

<summary>Morestel argues that self-knowledge is the most vital pursuit, far outweighing external scientific study or the accumulation of wealth and honors. He categorizes self-knowledge into three areas: understanding the fleeting nature of wealth, the fragility of the body, and the primacy of the soul.</summary>
<keywords>Know Thyself, self-knowledge, Delphic Oracle, Apollo, Fortune, fragility of life, soul vs body, Pierre Morestel, philosophy of mind</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication by Pierre Morestel to Charles of Lorraine. It follows the discussion of self-knowledge as a spiritual exercise, moving from the physical and external to the internal nature of the human soul.</meta>

# EPISTLE

...and in the divine part of you, the mind. For man within us is twofold: the **Material** and the **Essential**. And this latter is the true man, the prince, leader, and ruler of the other. The other part is a slave <note>original: "mancipium"</note>—though it, too, is called "man" if someone worships it. It is **Reason** which stands out, and which alone distinguishes us from the living creatures and the beasts.

For is man strong? So are the lions. Is he beautiful? So are the peacocks. Is he swift? So are the horses—to say nothing of the fact that in all these things, man is surpassed. Therefore, there is only one good proper to man: **Reason**, which must be known and cultivated as his divine part. From this knowledge follows a contempt for other things and an appreciation for **Virtue** alone, through which he will judge himself to be truly happy.

For (as **Agapetus** <note>Agapetus the Deacon, a 6th-century author known for his "Exposition of Advice" to the Emperor Justinian on the duties of a Christian ruler</note> says to **Justinian**): *He who knows himself, knows God; and knowing God, he will be made like God.*

Therefore, you are being made like God, **Most Illustrious Prince**. For from your earliest times, having been steeped in my teachings, you have joined yourself to the study of that knowledge <note>The "studium scientiae" or pursuit of wisdom</note> from which the moderation of life is born and nourished, and from which the right way of living and all order flows. You have, I say, dedicated your labor to **Philosophy**, at the urging of **Plato**...

<summary>Morestel defines the "true man" as the rational soul rather than the physical body, arguing that Reason is the only quality that truly distinguishes humans from animals. He praises the Duke of Elbeuf for pursuing Philosophy, which leads to a God-like moderation and virtue.</summary>

<keywords>Reason, Essential Man, Agapetus, Justinian, Plato, Virtue, Self-Knowledge, Divinity, Moderation</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the formal dedication, likely addressed to a patron or ruler, emphasizing the necessity of philosophical wisdom in governance.</meta>

# DEDICATION

<note>The author begins by referencing a famous Platonic ideal regarding philosopher-kings.</note> You shall say that Republics <note>original: "Respublicas," referring to the state or commonwealth</note> would be blessed if either learned or wise men began to rule them, or if those who rule were to place all their zeal into learning and wisdom. *For Philosophy heals the mind, frees it from desires, pulls out the root of all evils by the base, and contains the virtues; she is the inventor of Laws, the mistress of morals and discipline,* the eye of the soul <note>original Greek: "ὄμμα τῆς ψυχῆς," followed by the Latin "oculus animæ"</note>, *and without her it is truly impossible for the mind to be healthy. Do you wish to see the excellence and almost divine nature of the wise mind? It pervades all things in a moment; it is swifter than the stars, than thought, and than time: it is almost everywhere. Let the Wise man tell his soul to go there, and it will be there faster than the word is spoken: let him command it to swim across the Ocean, and it will be there most swiftly, not as if carried from place to place, but as if already existing there: let him bid it fly up into heaven, it will not need wings, no obstacle will stop it—not the heat of the Sun, nor the ether, nor the rotation of the Heavens, nor the bodies of the Stars—but rather, pervading all things, it flies to the furthest reaches of nature, and indeed beyond them, if it be permitted to gaze upon anything beyond.*

<summary>This dedication argues that the stability of a state depends on the wisdom of its rulers, describing the soul of a philosopher as a divine, boundless force capable of transcending physical limits.</summary>
<keywords>philosopher-king, virtue, governance, nature of the soul, Platonic thought, wisdom</keywords>

# EPISTLE.

How great, therefore, is the splendor of your name, (most noble Prince) by whose wisdom and virtues other Princes are enlightened, just as all things are clothed by the light of the Sun. For this reason, you have most deservedly taken a most illustrious Wife, who is both held to be and truly is the wisest of women, and a sharer in all virtues, of whom:

> Would that Art could portray her character and mind,
> ->No more beautiful painting would exist on earth.<-
<note>This couplet is adapted from Martial’s Epigrams, often used in the Renaissance to praise the internal beauty of a subject.</note>

IT IS A BEAUTIFUL THING TO STAND OUT AMONG ILLUSTRIOUS MEN: You certainly stand out through your supreme learning in this <term>Kabbalistic Art</term> <note>A mystical Jewish tradition involving the symbolic interpretation of scripture, which became popular among Christian scholars in the Renaissance</note>, in which you have employed me as your Preceptor <note>Teacher or private instructor</note>. This is the cause that impels me to publish this Academy of Divine Wisdom—which I hear is greatly desired—under the Shield of your name. Will you accept this gift with a calm mind? Surely you will accept it, on account of your most sweet and well-disposed benevolence toward me; this binds me closely to you and compels me to always celebrate your admirable fortitude, and that of your most dear little Son (whom since

# DEDICATION

->though all may love you for your exceptional grace, I yet surpass them all in my affection<- and I desire all things to be favorable for you. Farewell, <term>most powerful Prince</term><note>Latin: Princeps potentissime; a formal address to a high-ranking member of the nobility or royalty</note>, and recognize the innermost feelings of my soul: *it is the greatest virtue of a Prince to know his own people.* Again, farewell and greetings; may you and your most loving brother bring back victory over the King's enemies and the trophies of that victory, returning safely—O <term>Martial Hearts</term><note>Latin: Martia Corda; a poetic reference to those with the courage of Mars, the god of war</note>—together with the truly <term>Most Christian King</term><note>Latin: Ludouico Iusto; refers to King Louis XIII of France, who held the traditional title "Most Christian King"</note>, Louis the Just.

Paris. On the <term>Nones of June</term><note>June 5th</note>, the eighth day after you were wounded.

# TO THE SAME LORD
->Your most obedient servant, Jean-<-
->François de Chanlecy.<- <note>Jean-François de Chanlecy was likely a scholar or minor noble associated with the author or recipient.</note>

### EPIGRAM. <note>original: "EPIGRAMMA" — a short, pithy poem often used in dedications to praise a patron.</note>

As often as this lamp of Wisdom <note>original: "Sophia" — the Greek personification of Wisdom.</note> turns before my shining eyes,
->I am mindful of you.<-
Though you have drained immense waters with your mouth,
->Yet no liquid was able to relieve your thirst.<- <note>A metaphor for an insatiable desire for knowledge; the more one learns, the more one realizes there is to know.</note>
From this it is clear that you once penetrated the secrets of the Gods,
->Since you are able to perform the sacred duty of Wisdom.<-
She is open to all, yet she is known to only a few;
->But you are known to all, and your fame extends everywhere.<-

<vocab>Jean-François de Chanlecy, Epigram, Wisdom, secrets of the Gods</vocab>

<summary>A dedicatory epigram by Jean-François de Chanlecy praising a patron's insatiable thirst for knowledge and their mastery of divine secrets. The poem uses the metaphor of a lamp and unquenchable thirst to illustrate the pursuit of wisdom.</summary>
<keywords>dedication, epigram, Wisdom, Jean-François de Chanlecy, scholarship, intellectual thirst, divine secrets</keywords>

<meta>This page serves as a formal dedication and celebratory poem (epigram) addressed to the Duke of Elbeuf, likely following a military engagement where he was wounded.</meta>

# TO THE MOST ILLUSTRIOUS
## and Most Invincible Prince
## THE LORD DUKE D'ELBEUF <note>Charles de Lorraine (1556–1605), a prominent French nobleman and military leader during the Wars of Religion.</note>.

->E P I G R A M.<-

That day must be marked, *Prince*, with a better stone <note>original: "meliore lapillo" – a Roman idiom for marking a lucky or auspicious day with a white pebble.</note>,
On which you are the first to receive wounds for the sake of Christendom.
O, how welcome they are to you: with how joyful a mind
You suffer these wounds: But what pleasing rewards await you?
You are the first to go forth against the enemy troops with victorious arms,
And you stand brave even at the first strike.
Like a brave Hercules <note>original: "Alcides" – a common classical name for Hercules, referring to his lineage.</note>, wounded by the divine sting of love,
You yourself destroy these loathsome men.
You do not abandon your arms sooner because of a piercing wound,
Nor are you stripped of the garments of Mars <note>The Roman god of war; here referring to his armor or military status.</note> any sooner:
Than, O Prince, that numerous Phalanx of Sisters <note>The Muses, the nine goddesses of the arts and sciences.</note>
Places pleasing leisure beneath your feet through these books.
Thus Pallas <note>Pallas Athena, the goddess of wisdom and strategic warfare.</note> loves you: thus you are held worthy of Pallas;
Thus learned Minerva <note>The Roman equivalent of Athena; the author emphasizes that the Prince excels in both war and intellect.</note> flourishes in both peace and war.
Thus you are called the Apollo <note>original: "Phœbus" – the god of light, music, and prophecy.</note> and the pillar of the Muses,
And thus the sacred troop consoles its own Apollo.

->*Vowing his service to you from the heart, he dedicated this*<-
->**Pierre Langlois** *of Beauvais* <note>original: "Petrvs Langlosivs Bellouacensis" – the Latinized name of the author, a scholar known for works on hieroglyphics and symbols.</note>.<-

<summary>A laudatory Latin epigram dedicated to the Duke of Elbeuf, praising his bravery in battle and his patronage of the arts and sciences despite his military wounds.</summary>
<keywords>Duke of Elbeuf, Pierre Langlois, Hercules, Muses, Minerva, military valor, patronage, Neo-Latin poetry</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
# TO THE MOST ILLUSTRIOUS
### and Most Potent Prince,
### Lord Duke D’ELBEUF. <note>Charles de Lorraine (1596–1657), the Duke of Elbeuf and a prominent French military leader.</note>

---

->*On the wound inflicted upon him at the Siege of Saint-Jean-d'Angély.*<- <note>original: "Obſidione Angeliana." This refers to the 1621 siege during the Huguenot rebellions in France.</note>

### A HEROIC POEM.

It was spring, and the gardens were bright with snow-white flowers,
And the earth was spreading forth the first ears of the grain,
And the East Wind was breathing sweet breezes across the wide fields,
Where, amidst the war, we behold ELBEUF as one man out of many thousands
Carrying back grand spoils through the midst of the enemies
Of the Christians, though his own leg was marked by a wide wound.
Orthodox Religion was being brought low by wicked dangers,
And a poison was penetrating sluggish minds:
But like one who would condemn the unjust Python <note>The monstrous serpent slain by Apollo.</note> to death,
We behold ELBEUF, who performs the duties of bright Apollo,
Choosing rather to die in the midst of arms.
The fates had bound Andromeda to the sea-rocks:
But here is one who seeks to free the daughter of Cepheus from danger;
A second Perseus stands ready as a conqueror, and having taken up
His arms, he dares to go through the infectious enemies.
He who was born of Alcmena <note>Hercules.</note>, renowned for his virtues,
Strives to overcome the massive serpents with his strong arms,
And to tame the reborn Hydras, the malignant Cacus,
The Antaei, the Lapiths, and other rebels.

<summary>A Latin panegyric in heroic verse honoring the Duke of Elbeuf for his bravery and injury sustained during the Siege of Saint-Jean-d'Angély, comparing him to mythological heroes like Apollo, Perseus, and Hercules.</summary>
<keywords>Duke of Elbeuf, Siege of Saint-Jean-d'Angély, military bravery, Counter-Reformation, Apollo, Perseus, Hercules, Neo-Latin poetry</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the poetic eulogy or panegyric for a noble military leader, likely following a description of a specific battle or siege.</meta>

Why do you—oh pestilential minds—stir up these empty
battles? Do you wish to dictate the law to Christ?
In the manner of the Giants <note>In Greek mythology, the Giants attempted to overthrow the gods by stacking mountains (Ossa and Pelion) to reach heaven.</note>, do you truly wish to cast the mountain peaks
beneath other mountains, and wage war against the faithful gods?
The higher you wish to lift up your minds;
the deeper the fierce flames will overturn your bodies:
The high Ruler of Olympus <note>original: "Olympi Rector" – a classical reference to God, blending Christian and Roman imagery.</note> will hurl
flame-spewing fires; He who protects the supreme King,
mighty in war, and protects **Elbeuf** <note>original: "Elbovium" – likely referring to a member of the House of Guise-Elbeuf, a prominent French ducal family.</note>, who was wounded by the first
injury to his leg, and deserved to carry back the first garlands of victory.
Be blessed in your courage, **Prince**; drive out the harmful Hydras <note>A multi-headed monster from myth, here used as a metaphor for multifaceted enemies or heresies.</note>;
drive them out, and may you prevail in waging war with every kind of triumph.
There lies certain salvation; there are the rewards of the gods;
there stands the peace of the Kingdom, destined to endure forever.

->**The same author.**<-

<note>Vocabulary used in this passage: Elbeuf (the subject), Prince, Giant-like, Ruler of Olympus (God), Hydras (monstrous enemies).</note>

<summary>A Latin poem addressed to the enemies of a Prince (likely of the Elbeuf family), warning them of divine retribution and praising the Prince's bravery after being wounded in the leg.</summary>
<keywords>House of Elbeuf, military panegyric, divine protection, Giants, Hydra, Counter-Reformation imagery</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page serves as a formal dedication and series of commendatory poems for the author, Pierre Morestel. It establishes his authority through classical and mystical imagery, typical of 17th-century scholarly works.</meta>

# ON THE AUTHOR OF THE ART
->Of the Kabbalah<- <note>original: "Kabbal." refers to the Kabbalah, a system of Jewish mysticism often adapted by Christian scholars in the Renaissance to explore the secrets of the divine and nature.</note>

### EPIGRAM.

Who does not know that the Eagle, with fixed eyes, discerns the Sun? <note>original: "Phœbus," the Greek god of the sun, often used to represent enlightenment or the source of truth.</note> Who does not know that she is carried above the stars?
Not only were you immersed in the Fountain of the Muses <note>original: "Fonte Caballino," literally the "Horse's Fountain" or Hippocrene, created by the hoof of Pegasus; it represents poetic inspiration.</note> as an infant, but as a man, you likewise wash your face in the divine.
Supported by Wisdom, <note>original: "Sophia," the personification of Wisdom.</note> how well you know the hidden secrets of Nature; you possess whatever the world contains.
On the wings of Wisdom, you see the Sun and the heavens lie beneath your feet; you see all things as subject to you.
What is the cause? It was that work of yours: Labor teaches the arts; that relentless Labor has borne you above the stars.
You are the fountain, you are the stream, you gather the rivers into great floods; you, like the Sun, shine down upon all.
Turn your eyes toward the fountain, young men; there the example stands forth, and there the labor is found.

---

### TO THE SAME.
->Anagram<-

->PIERRE MORESTEL<- <note>original: "Petrus Morestellus."</note>
->YOU ARE A HARBOR OF THE STARS<- <note>original: "Stellarum Portus Es," an anagram of the author's Latinized name.</note>

Through the stars, while the dark night spreads its veils across the world far from the Sun, there is an entrance or a certain path to the heavens.
You stand as a HARBOR OF THE STARS; you are a Star present during the perils of the voyage, and you are the North Star <note>original: "Cynosura," the constellation of the Little Bear used by sailors for navigation.</note> as well.

->Offered from the heart by P. LANGLOIS<-
->of Beauvais.<-

## Extract of the Privilege.

By the grace and Privilege of the King, Melchior Mondiere, Merchant Bookseller in Paris, is permitted to print or have printed two books: one entitled, *The Funeral Pomp or Just Funeral Rites of Pierre Morestel of Tournus, Master of Liberal Arts, Doctor of Theology, and Rector of Saint-Nicolas de la Taille* <note>original: "Pompa Feralis siue Iusta funebria Petri Morestelli..."</note>; the other entitled *The Kabbalistic Art or the Divine Wisdom of the Academy, arranged in nine classes with most friendly clarity, by Pierre Morestel, Master of Liberal Arts and Doctor of Theology* <note>original: "Artis Kabbalisticæ siue Sapientiæ diuinæ..."</note>. All printers, booksellers, and other persons of whatever quality and condition they may be, are forbidden from printing or having printed the said books, or from selling or distributing any others than those which the said Mondiere shall have printed or caused to be printed, for the duration and term of six completed and accomplished years. This is under penalty of a fine of one thousand pounds <note>The "livre" was the currency of France at the time.</note>, one third of which shall be applicable to us <note>The Crown</note>, one third to the informer, and the other third to the said petitioner, as is more fully contained in the letters of Privilege given at *Paris* on the fifth of June, in the year of grace one thousand six hundred and twenty-one, and the twelfth of our reign.

->Signed. By the Council,<-
->**Hardy.**<-

<summary>This page contains the official royal printing privilege granted to the bookseller Melchior Mondiere in 1621, protecting the copyright of two works by the theologian Pierre Morestel for a period of six years.</summary>

## APPROVAL.

I, the undersigned, certify to whomsoever shall have the power to approve books, that I have read these two: the one entitled *The Funeral Procession, etc.* <note>original: "Pompa Feralis"</note>, and the other *The Academy of the Kabbalistic Art, or of Divine Wisdom* <note>original: "Artis Kabbalisticæ siue Sapientiæ diuinæ Academia"</note>, in which I have found nothing contrary to the faith, nor against good morals. This 7th of June, 1621.

->**MEURISSE.**<-
->**Doctor of Theology.**<- <note>original: "T. D." (Theologiae Doctor)</note>

<meta>This is the opening page of the work, establishing the dialogue between the author, Morestellus, and his friend Olympius regarding the Kabbalistic arts and the works of Ramon Llull.</meta>

# THE ART
## OF KABBALAH
### OR,
### THE ACADEMY OF DIVINE WISDOM.

*A Preface containing: 1. The occasion and origin of this Academy. 2. A commendation of the Lullian Art. 3. The subject matter. 4. The order of the work. 5. A short prayer.*

<margin>1.</margin>
The sweetness of the springtime was at hand when my close friend *Olympius*—a man most dear to those who engage in the exchange of letters <note>The "exchange of letters" refers to the "Republic of Letters," the international community of scholars and intellectuals.</note>—came to visit me. After we had greeted one another, as he held the works of Llull <note>original: "Opera Lullij." Refers to Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1315), a Majorcan philosopher and logician known for his "Great Art" of combining concepts.</note> concerning the Universal Art in his hands, he passed them into mine. I said to him: "You are always buried in books, my dear Olympius." 

Olympius replied: "I wish, my dear *Morestellus* <note>Pierre Morestel (1574–1658), the author of this work.</note>, that I had not spent my time in those books which exhaust the youth at great expense—following the advice of mere pretenders to knowledge—but rather that I had been immersed in this book (which is yet obscure to me) since my childhood."

*Morestellus.*

<summary>This title page and preface introduction establish a dialogue between Morestellus and Olympius, introducing the "Art of Kabbalah" and the philosophical system of Ramon Llull as the foundation for divine wisdom.</summary>
<keywords>Kabbalah, Divine Wisdom, Ramon Llull, Pierre Morestel, Lullian Art, Scholarly Dialogue</keywords>

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory preface, transitioning from a general greeting to a specific defense of the Lullian Art as the foundation of all knowledge.</meta>

# PREFACE TO THE ACADEMY OF THE KABBALISTIC ART

<margin>2.</margin> My dear Olympus, I hold you in great affection because you cherish a great man among the great, through whose thoroughly examined and clearly understood art we are able to discourse accurately and subtly on any proposed question. What more can I say? Believe me, such is the power of the <term>Lullian Art</term><note>The system of logic and philosophy developed by Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1315), using combinatorial diagrams to find universal truths.</note> that one equipped with this alone can compare all other things with no trouble. Concerning this discipline, the commentator on the *Short Art* of Llull wrote the following to <term>John Laurencinus</term><note>A 16th-century patron of Lullist studies.</note>: *We wish to gift you with an Art, and one that is not common, nor trivial, nor occupied with only one subject, but the Queen of all Arts. With this as your guide, you will easily ascend to all other sciences and arts, so that you may reasonably profess not only a mediocre discipline in them, but even distinguished learning. This is the **Inventive Art of Ramon Llull**, whose dignity and excellence, whose generality and certainty are such that it is sufficient by itself. It presupposes no other art and requires no outside help; it allows us to find truth and knowledge about every knowable thing without difficulty or labor, infallibly and with all security and certainty, all error having been removed. Secondly, it embraces all other sciences within itself and directs them toward the truth; it solves all questions and objections that can be raised concerning anything knowable, nor is there any knowable thing that escapes it. Furthermore, it reasons through infallible, most certain, and most well-known arguments which no one can contradict. Therefore, it does not need the testimony of other sciences or writings, but it proves the authority of all other writings and sciences itself; it even arranges and orders Holy Scripture itself. For it possesses universal principles, most general and most well-known, held in a certain mutual relationship...*

<summary>The author praises the "Lullian Art" as a supreme, universal method of logic that serves as the foundation for all other sciences and even validates Holy Scripture. It is presented as an infallible tool for discovering truth across any subject without the need for outside assistance.</summary>
<keywords>Ramon Llull, Lullian Art, Inventive Art, John Laurencinus, Queen of Arts, universal logic, methodology, epistemology</keywords>

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface regarding the Lullian Art, establishing its divine origins and pedagogical efficiency.</meta>

# PREFACE TO THE ACADEMY OF THE KABBALISTIC ART

and an ingenious method of reasoning, in which the principles and discourses of all other sciences shine forth as particulars within their own universal framework. Furthermore, such is the readiness and ease of this science that even young boys, relying on this Art, are able to discourse learnedly in almost every faculty. Many also who turned themselves to letters in extreme old age have, through this Art, emerged as learned men within a few months; thus this art overcomes all lack and scarcity of time. Shall I add the golden words of the golden work of Olympus? <note>Olympus is likely a character in a dialogue or a pseudonym for a specific commentator on Lull.</note> *It is that Art whose invention our fellow Jews ascribe to Solomon, the great Philosopher-King, and which the wise conclude returned to Lullus by a sort of divine suggestion, as if by a right of recovery.* <note>original: "postliminii" — a legal term referring to the restoration of rights to a person returning from captivity.</note> OLYMPUS: O happy am I, who have today encountered a Lullist and a man learned in the discipline of Raymond <note>Raymond Lull (c. 1232–1315), a Majorcan philosopher and logician known for his "Great Art."</note>, and whom I see I shall have as a teacher of this craftsmanship. *Morestus:* One must learn before one teaches, Olympus; truly, in this Lullian art I have used no master except for the commentators, who, although they bring light to many, nevertheless pour darkness over even more.

*Olympus:* You speak the truth, for the commentators are shadows and explain obscure things through equally obscure means; nevertheless, frequent use and the comparison of authors—which is familiar to you—brings forth perfection. <margin>3.</margin> *Morestus:* I wish it had brought it forth, Olympus; I would certainly see to it that your wish was granted. If, however, I possess any practice in this Queen of Arts (in which I do not deny I am moderately versed), I shall carry the torch before you by compressing many things into a few, illustrating the obscure with the clear, the difficult with the easy, and the confused with the distinct—

<summary>This page introduces the "Lullian Art" as a universal method of reasoning that allows even the young or elderly to master all sciences quickly. It links the Art's origins to King Solomon and discusses the difficulty of finding clear teachers among obscure commentators.</summary>

IIII. It will explain the Second Figure concerning absolute Predicates.

V. It will examine relative Predicates.

VI. It will treat the Questions, their rules, and their types.

VII. # Preface to the Academy of the Kabbalistic Art

the application of the Art will provide you with both precepts and examples.

**Olympius.** This method of yours entices and holds the mind: this order is fitting, as is the arrangement of parts, which will make things easy to understand and will aid the memory. For those things that are organized are understood far more easily than those which are disordered and mixed together; furthermore, those things learned in an orderly fashion cling more firmly to the soul. O, most excellent art! *This is the rest from my cares, this my sacred pleasure, and the one peace for my soul.* <note>original: "Hæc mihi curarum requies, hæc sacra voluptas, Vnaque pax animo." A poetic expression of devotion to study.</note>

I shall not let go of this art until life lets go of me: continue, Morestellus, to set me ablaze with your fire and fulfill your promises. **Morestellus.** They must certainly be fulfilled, but because the tongue of the Teacher labors in vain *unless the Holy Spirit is present to teach within*, I pray to that heavenly <term>Paraclete<note>original: παράκλητος (paraklētos). A Greek term for the Holy Spirit, meaning 'advocate' or 'helper'.</note></term>, the author of knowledge and speech, so that things may turn out favorably, happily, and prosperously for me. 

Let it be, therefore.

# FIRST CLASS.

## CONCERNING THE NAME OF KABBALAH, the subject of the entire Art, the meanings of its Alphabet, and finally the figures of the Art in general.

### LECTURE I.

->1. *What Kabbalah is.* 2. *Its etymology <note>original: "ἔτυμον" (etymon) - the origin or derivation of a word.</note>.*<-

<margin>1</margin>According to the Hebrews, Kabbalah *is the reception of the truth of any matter divinely revealed to the rational soul*. However, according to more recent Kabbalists, it is *Divine Wisdom*: for they say that Kabbalah is a name composed of two Arabic words, namely *Abba* and *Ala*. Now, *Abba* in Arabic is the same as *Father* in English, and *Ala* is the same as *My God*. And since *My God* declares nothing other than the Son of God—who is truly the Divine Wisdom—therefore this word "Kabbalah" signifies divine wisdom or science. <note>The author is using a creative, though linguistically questionable, etymology to link the Hebrew term 'Kabbalah' (meaning 'tradition' or 'reception') to Christian concepts of the Trinity via Arabic roots.</note>

# Art of the Academic Kabbalah, First Class

Each of these figures possesses nine terms, marked by the nine aforementioned letters of the Alphabet: B, C, D, and so forth. However, three other general principles have been added to the nine principles placed by Raymond <note>original: "Raymundum"; referring to Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1315), the Majorcan philosopher who invented this system of logic.</note> in the second figure, which are: *Essence, Unity, and Perfection.*

Furthermore, these figures are Circular: first, because the Circle is the most capable and most perfect of all shapes. Second, because a spherical or round shape is the most suited for motion, and discourse <note>The process of reasoning from one thought to another.</note> is indeed a certain kind of motion.

<summary>This page explains the symbolic and functional use of circles in Ramon Llull's mnemonic system, noting the addition of three new principles—Essence, Unity, and Perfection—to the standard nine.</summary>
<keywords>Ramon Llull, sacred geometry, circular figures, Essence, Unity, Perfection, logic as motion, mnemonic alphabet</keywords>

# SECOND CLASS.

## CONCERNING THE FIRST FIGURE SPECIFICALLY:

### OR,

### Concerning Universal Subjects.

#### LECTURE I.

*1. What is understood by the name of "subjects." 2. How many Lullian subjects there are. 3. Why they are called universal. 4. Their definitions, differences, and conditions must be learned.*

<margin>1</margin>Here, by the name of **Subjects**, understand both those things about which something is primarily affirmed or denied—said truly or falsely—and also those things which principally possess being <note>original: "entitatem"</note> and substance. For in these subjects, all things that are substance are signified in the manner of substance; they are identified with them, reduced to them, and understood to be comprised within them. Furthermore, all things are either species of these, or differences, or parts, or principles, or properties, or adjuncts, or primary or secondary intentions <note>In logic, a "first intention" refers to the mental grasp of a real object, while a "second intention" refers to the mind's own concepts, like "genus" or "species."</note>.

<summary>This page introduces the "First Figure" of Ramon Lull's art, defining "Subjects" as the fundamental substances and logical categories upon which all affirmations and denials are built. It establishes the metaphysical framework where all properties and concepts are reduced to these primary universal subjects.</summary>

<keywords>First Figure, Universal Subjects, Lullian Art, substance, being, logic, primary intentions, secondary intentions</keywords>

# ACADEMIC ART CABINET: SECOND CLASS

<margin>3</margin>
Raymond <note>original: "Raymundus"; referring to Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1316), the Majorcan philosopher and mystic who created this system of logic</note> takes these principles so generally that nothing can ever be expressed without drawing the subject of the proposition from them. For this reason, he also calls them "universal subjects." One must learn their definitions precisely, as well as their differences from one another and their specific conditions. This is necessary so that the method of assigning attributes in the following figures <note>referring to the "Figures" or diagrams used in the Lullian Art to combine concepts</note> may be understood later. For not all predicates <note>the part of a sentence or statement that modifies the subject</note> apply to all subjects in the same way. Those things said of God are expressed in a different manner than those said of created things; and those things attributed to man according to reason must not be present in a brute beast. Likewise, those things said of a just man are expressed differently than those said of an unjust man. Now, therefore, let us come to the definitions, differences, and conditions of the individual Subjects.

The diagram appears to be a bleed-through from the reverse side of the leaf or a very light printing of a combinatorial wheel typical of Lullian art.</image-desc>

# ACADEMIC CABINET OF THE ART. SECOND CLASS.
B

### LECTURE II.

### Concerning Subject B, or God.

1. *That God is a most simple nature.* 2. *That a plurality of persons must nevertheless be admitted in Him.* 3. *That He possesses in Himself every fulfillment.* 4. *Four conditions to be considered in this subject.* 5. *External terms, yet related to each subject, are to be subjoined, as well as the species and their powers when they occur.*

<margin>1</margin> God is that being in whom goodness, greatness, duration, and the other absolute predicates placed in Figure or Circle A <note>In the Lullian system, "Figure A" represents the divine attributes or "Dignities" arranged in a circle to show their interconnectedness.</note> are one and the same in number. For God is a most simple nature or a most simple being, or (if you prefer) *Pure Act* <note>original: "Purissimus actus"; a Scholastic term meaning a being with no unrealized potential or changeability</note> admitting no distinction of perfections, although in a certain most eminent way He contains infinite perfections, which are called His attributes. Thus, light is a single simple quality receiving no distinction of nature within the category of quality, even though it eminently includes other elementary qualities such as heat and dryness.

<margin>2</margin> But if it be said that there is therefore no plurality in God: the answer is that this is true when considering His most pure essence; but it is false when looking at the intrinsic Correlatives. For in God there is the *Bonifier* <note>original: "Bonificatiuum"; the active principle of goodness</note> which is the same as the Father; the *Bonified* <note>original: "Bonificatum"; the object or recipient of goodness</note> which is the same as the Son; and the *Bonifying* <note>original: "Bonificatio"; the act of making good or the bond of goodness</note> which is the Holy Spirit; and thus in God there is plu-

<summary>This page introduces the first subject of the Lullian Art, God (Subject B), arguing that while God is a perfectly simple being, He contains a plurality of persons through internal "correlatives" of His attributes.</summary>
<keywords>God, Divine Attributes, Simplicity of God, Pure Act, Trinity, Bonifier, Bonified, Ramon Llull, Scholasticism</keywords>

# Art. Kab. Acad. II. Classis.

The plurality of persons is like the plurality of essential correlatives <note>In Lullian philosophy, correlatives are the active, passive, and verbal components of an essence.</note> which are the *Deitativum* <note>The active principle of Deity; the "God-ing" power.</note>, the *Deitatum* <note>The passive principle; that which is "God-ed."</note>, and the *Deitatio* <note>The act of being God; the union of the two.</note>: which are formally distinguished although they are the same in one most simple nature, as has been said.

<margin>3</margin> And this nature, or God himself, possesses within himself every fulfillment, needing nothing outside of himself; by these words he differs from every other being, for all other beings need something outside of themselves. Shall I illustrate this subject further? God is that infinite, eternal, and perpetual being, before and after the beginning and end of all things, himself without beginning and end, in whom are the universal perfections of things, and from whom all imperfections are absent.

<margin>4</margin> *Lull* <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1316), a Majorcan philosopher and mystic known for his "Art," a system of logic using combinatorial diagrams.</note> in the *Short Art*, chapter 13, brings forward four conditions to be considered in this subject.

<margin>1</margin> *The first is*, that in God there is neither contrariety nor minority among the relational predicates placed in Circle T <note>A specific diagram in Llull's system representing relational principles like Difference, Concordance, and Contrariety.</note>, because those are privative and defective principles: nevertheless, in him there is majority in respect to other beings, and equality: for he has equal principles—namely goodness, greatness, etc.—and also equal acts.

<margin>2</margin> *The second is*, that in God there is a difference of Correlatives (as is clear from the examples cited above) without which difference those correlatives could by no means exist, nor could God have an intrinsic, infinite, and eternal action without them: indeed, without them all his reasons <note>The "Dignities" or attributes of God.</note> would be idle, which is impossible.

<margin>3</margin> *The third is*, that in God there is Concordance, by which he stands infinitely and eternally apart from contrariety, and

### LECTURE III.

### Concerning Subject C, or the Angel.

> 1. *The Angel is derivable through the circles. 2. Its definition. 3. Conditions of this subject. 4. What is understood by the name of Angel. 5. The three hierarchies of the good Angels. 6. Synonyms.*

<margin>1</margin> The Angel is derivable through principles and rules, for it possesses natural goodness, greatness, duration, etc. <note>The author refers to the Lullian method of using rotating circles or "volvelles" to combine divine attributes and deduce the nature of created things.</note>
It is defined as follows: An Angel is a spirit not joined to a body, and therefore not requiring time, nor motion, nor place; according to its essence, it is intellectual, immortal, and imperceptible to the senses; it is also by its own nature incapable of suffering <note>original: "impassibilis"</note>, unless it has corrupted itself in its character through that faculty which was permitted to it by free will.
A similar description applies to the soul, except that the soul is joined to a body and always desires it.

<margin>2</margin> The Prince of this wisdom <note>Likely referring to Ramon Llull, the 13th-century philosopher</note> in the *Short Art*, chapter 14, offers six conditions for this subject.
*The first is* that there is no natural contrariety in the Angel, for it is incorruptible.

<margin>3</margin> *The second* is that there is in the Angel a "matter of the weak" <term>materia debilibus: a philosophical concept suggesting a spiritual substance that can still receive further perfection</term>, namely that which is capable of being made good, made great, made durable, etc.

<margin>4</margin> *The third*: In the Angel there is "majority" <note>superiority or greater scale</note>, for it has a greater likeness to God than man does, because it possesses higher principles and rules than man. From this, the intellect understands that although man cannot use his senses without organs,

### LECTURE IV.

#### Concerning the subject D, or the Heavens.

*1. The Heavens are explainable through circles. 2. What they are. 3. A fifth simple body distinct from the elements. 4. Their conditions. 5. The number of the heavens. 6. Synonyms.*

<margin>1</margin>
The Heaven possesses goodness, magnitude, duration, etc., and is defined thus: The Heaven is an incorruptible body <note>In Aristotelian physics, "incorruptible" means it cannot decay, rot, or change its essential nature, unlike earthly objects.</note> which is not subject to change in its form, but only change in its location <note>original: "mutatio ad ubi"; referring to local motion or moving from place to place.</note>. It is composed of its own matter and form, not forced together by a composition of the Elements, and is more vastly extended than any other body. Although it agrees with the Elements in many respects—such as being inanimate, simple, and belonging essentially to the integrity of the universe—it nevertheless differs from them in species and nature. Thus, the Heaven is a fifth simple body <term>Quintessence: the "fifth essence" or element that makes up the celestial spheres, distinct from earth, water, air, and fire.</term> distinct from the remaining four elementary bodies.

<margin>2</margin>

<margin>3</margin>

<margin>4</margin>
In this subject, the following conditions must be considered:

<margin>1</margin>
*First:* In the heaven there is no contrariety, for it is not composed of contrary principles <note>Earthly things were thought to be made of opposing qualities like hot/cold or wet/dry; the heavens were believed to be perfectly uniform.</note>.

<margin>2</margin>
*Second:* Within it are natural instincts and appetites, and consequently motion, without which it could not possess its nature, instinct, and appetite.

<margin>3</margin>
*Third:* The motion of the Heaven is its own end and its rest <note>Unlike earthly movement which seeks a destination to stop at, celestial circular motion was seen as a perfect state of being.</note>.

<margin>4</margin>
*Fourth:* The Heaven is in its own place, just as a body is within its own surface.

<meta>catchword: In</meta>

<summary>This lecture defines the Heavens as a "fifth element" or quintessence that is incorruptible and distinct from the four earthly elements. It outlines the unique physical properties of celestial bodies, emphasizing their lack of internal conflict and their perpetual motion.</summary>
<keywords>Heavens, quintessence, incorruptibility, celestial motion, Aristotelian physics, four elements, natural philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 51 ---
<meta>This page continues the academic classification of the heavens, moving from the philosophical nature of time to the physical structure of the celestial spheres.</meta>

# Art Cabinet of the Academy, Second Class

<margin>Signature: B 4</margin>

*Fifth:* The Heaven exists within time, for it is itself something new <note>original: "novum"; likely referring to the Aristotelian or theological debate on whether the universe is eternal or created</note>, and it exists in time just as a cause exists within its effect, and likewise for its other properties in their own manner.

According to common opinion, there are only ten movable Heavens: namely, the seven Heavens of the planets, the Starry Heaven <note>the Firmament</note>, the Crystalline Heaven, and the First Movable <term>Primum Mobile: the outermost moving sphere in geocentric astronomy which carries all inner spheres with it</term>. However, according to more recent scholars, there are eleven movable spheres. They argue that between the firmament and the First Movable, a double sphere should be established instead of a single Crystalline one. This is because they demonstrate that the single "motion of trepidation" <note>a theory in pre-Copernican astronomy used to explain the shifting of the equinoxes</note> formerly attributed to the Crystalline heaven cannot be accepted. Instead, they propose a double "motion of oscillation" <term>librationis: a back-and-forth swinging motion</term>: one from east to west and back again, which they attribute to the ninth Sphere; and another from North to South and back again, which they assign to the tenth Sphere. Both of these spheres may be called by the name "Crystalline Heaven." To these is added the eleventh Sphere, or the First Movable. Finally, beyond the movable Heavens, Theologians admit a supreme, immobile Heaven which they call the Empyrean, which is the seat of the blessed; thus, there are twelve Heavens in total.

Synonyms for "Heaven" are: { The celestial Orb, / The domicile of God, etc.

<vocab>Heaven, Crystalline, First Movable, Sphere, Empyrean, motion of trepidation, motion of oscillation</vocab>

### Lecture V.

#### Concerning the subject E., or Man.

> *1. What man is. 2. Deducible through principles in two ways. 3. What the soul is in general. 4. The origin of the rational soul. 5. At what time it is infused into the body. 6. Its two faculties. 7. The adequate object of the intellect. 8. Intelligible species are necessary for the act of understanding. 9. How these species are produced: The twofold intellect: Both are the same faculty. 10. Which things are reduced to this subject.*

<margin>1
2</margin>
Man is a substance in which a rational soul and a mortal body are joined: therefore, by reason of both parts, he is deducible through principles and rules in two ways. In order for this to be easily accomplished, you must have a clear and known perspective of both natures—namely, of the soul and of the body; therefore, a few things must be said here concerning the Soul, but concerning the body under the section on Elemental matters. <note>The author refers to a later section of the curriculum dealing with physical elements.</note>

<margin>3</margin>
The Soul, therefore, is the *first actuality* <note>original: "ἐντελεχεία πρώτη" (entelecheia prōtē). A term from Aristotle's De Anima defining the soul as the realization or "form" of a living body.</note>, that is, the first act of a natural organic body having the potential for life.

It is called, first, *First Act*, or that which is first implanted; it is that substantial perfection of a thing as distinguished from its operations and faculties. For operations are *second acts* <note>The actual functioning or behavior of a being.</note>, while faculties are indeed *first acts* if compared with operations, but not absolutely and simply, since they flow from the very fountain of life.

<summary>This lecture defines Man as a union of a rational soul and a mortal body, introducing Aristotelian concepts of "first act" to explain the soul as the primary animating principle of a physical being.</summary>
<keywords>anthropology, Aristotelian philosophy, soul and body, rational soul, first act, second act, entelechy, intellect</keywords>

--- Page 53 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 24, which likely introduced the initial definitions of the soul. The catchword "teria" from the previous page suggests the completion of the word "materia" (matter) or a similar philosophical term.</meta>

# ACADEMIC ARTS CABINET: SECOND CLASS

It is said, secondly, to be the *Act of the body*. Understand this to mean the whole body, for no specific seat of the Soul is assigned by nature; rather, it is diffused throughout the entire body. <note>The author is following the Aristotelian view that the soul is the "actuality" or "form" of the body, rather than a separate substance located in a single organ like the brain or heart.</note>

It is said, thirdly, to be *of a natural organic body*, that is, one distinguished by a variety of dissimilar parts. The soul requires this variety because it is the principle of many operations, which can only be carried out through the service of multiple members. <note>original: "Naturalis organici"</note>

It is said, fourthly, to be *of that which has life in potential*. For it is not enough that a body be organic; it must also be able to exercise the functions of life. Therefore, since a corpse cannot do this, the soul cannot be said to be its "actuality." <note>original: "Potestate vitam habentis"</note>

And this is the general definition of the Soul, such that it applies synonymously not only to the vegetative or sensitive soul, but also to the rational soul. <note>In the scholastic tradition, there are three levels of soul: vegetative (plants), sensitive (animals), and rational (humans).</note>

It can also be defined thus: The Soul is that by which we first live, feel, and understand. This definition can be explained disjunctively, so that the soul is called the principle by which we either live, or feel, or understand; for it is not necessary that everything which possesses a soul must live, feel, and understand all at once. <note>For example, a plant lives but does not feel or understand.</note>

And because man, who is the subject here, is endowed with the most noble of all physical forms—namely, the rational—something must be said regarding this. This [rational soul] is by no means procreated by seminal power, because a bodily power cannot achieve the production of a spiritual thing. Instead, it receives its being from God through creation, and is infused by Him and united to the body at the same instant in which the matter [is first prepared] with organs and dispositions for that form...

<summary>This page provides a scholastic definition of the soul as the "actuality" of an organic body and distinguishes between the vegetative, sensitive, and rational souls, asserting that the human rational soul is created directly by God.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotelian philosophy, definition of the soul, rational soul, hylomorphism, creationism, biology, scholasticism</keywords>

# Art Cabinet of the Academy, Second Class

...is equipped with the requirements for undertaking [life], which occurs in males around the fortieth day from conception, but in females on the eightieth. This relates to that prescribed number of days for purification in the Old Law <note>Referring to Leviticus 12, which dictates different periods of ritual purification for mothers after the birth of a son versus a daughter</note>, namely 40 days for males and 80 for females.

<margin>6</margin> There are two principal faculties of the **rational soul** <term>Anima rationalis: the highest part of the human soul responsible for reason, distinguishing humans from animals</term>, namely the **intellect** and the **will**. Regarding the will, I shall speak under principle E, or "appetite"; here I speak of the intellect. Its adequate object is not "the true," as many contend, but rather **being** insofar as it is **intelligible** <note>original: "ens quatenus intelligibile"</note>. This is because it is prior by nature to understand being as being, rather than as truth. Since truth is a "passion" or a certain property of being, and every property is secondary by nature, it is therefore understood later than that thing to which the property belongs. Add to this that "intelligible being" is more widely evident than "true being," since even "false being" is intelligible in its own way; certainly, being is intelligible insofar as it abstracts from both the true and the false.

<margin>7</margin>

<margin>8</margin> Furthermore, for the act of understanding, **intelligible species** <term>species intelligibiles: the mental images or forms that allow the mind to grasp the essence of an object</term> are necessary, which are the instrumental principles of understanding. For the intellect is like a pure potentiality and is of itself indifferent to perceiving this or that; therefore, it is compared by Aristotle to a **blank slate** <term>tabula rasa: a tablet on which nothing has yet been written</term>. For this reason, it must be activated and determined before it can operate regarding an Object. The act by which the intellect is intrinsically activated and determined is the "intelligible species," which exists in the Intellect as the formal principle from which the act of understanding is exercised.

<meta>This page continues the philosophical discussion of the human mind, specifically focusing on the mechanics of how we form abstract thoughts from sensory data.</meta>

# Art. Kab. Acad. II. Classis.

In order to understand how these <term>species</term> <note>In this context, "species" refers to mental representations or "images" of things, not biological categories.</note> are produced, it must first be noted that within the cognitive part of the **rational soul**, a twofold intellect is assigned: one **active** <note>original: "intellectus agens"</note> and the other **passive** or **possible** <note>original: "intellectus patiens seu possibilis"</note>. Indeed, this latter one is more properly called the "intellect," since it is truly the faculty of understanding that draws forth the act of understanding itself. The primary task of the **active intellect** is to manufacture the **intelligible species**, which is a form of working or acting; hence it is named "active." The role of the **passive intellect**, however, is to receive these species, which is why it is called "passive."

Furthermore, although the function of the active intellect is one thing and that of the passive another, we believe that both are in reality the **same faculty**. Insofar as it has the power of forming species, it is called the active intellect; insofar as it receives them, it is called passive.

With these points noted, I say that intelligible species are created in this way: The **active intellect**—with the **imagination** <note>original: "phantasia"</note> simultaneously contributing through its bodily images or **phantasms** <note>original: "phantasmata"; these are the mental pictures we have of physical objects</note>—draws out other intellectual images that are purer and simpler, and simultaneously impresses them upon the [passive] intellect.

Here you will note that intelligible species can only be produced by the intellect for those things of which we have phantasms; this is because the active intellect does not draw out species unless the phantasms are contributing and determining the process.

<summary>This page explains the Scholastic theory of cognition, detailing how the "active intellect" transforms sensory images (phantasms) into abstract concepts (intelligible species) to be received by the "passive intellect." It argues that these two intellects are actually different functions of a single mental faculty.</summary>

<keywords>rational soul, active intellect, passive intellect, intelligible species, phantasms, imagination, theory of knowledge, cognitive philosophy</keywords>

### LECTURE VI.
### Concerning Subject F, or the Imaginative Faculty.

*1. What the Imaginative is. 2. Deducible through principles. 3. What things are contained under it. 4. How many internal senses there are, and how many are their cells in the brain. 5. Concerning each one individually.*

<margin>1</margin>
The **Imaginative** is that power in which a living being <note>original: *animal*; in this context, it refers to any sentient creature including humans.</note> imagines the likenesses of those things which were previously presented to the senses. It makes these likenesses good through the goodness of Circle A <note>A reference to the mnemonic "circles" or combinatorial wheels used in Lullian logic to categorize attributes.</note>, and makes them great through its magnitude—such as when one thinks of a great mountain of gold. Conversely, it makes them small through the smallness of Circle T, as when one imagines an indivisible point; and so it proceeds with the rest of the principles.

<margin>2</margin>
<margin>3</margin>
Under this subject are contained "perfect animals" <note>A philosophical term for complex organisms that possess a full range of sensory and biological functions.</note> which have being, life, sensation, and the ability to apprehend through both internal and external powers and senses. However, because the external senses are more conveniently dealt with under the "Sensitive" category, here we shall treat only the internal ones.

Therefore, the internal senses which lie hidden in the cells of the Brain are numbered at most as

# ACADEMIC CABINET OF ARTS. SECOND CLASS.

<image-desc>A bracketed list grouping four terms under the heading "Four".</image-desc>

-> The Four: <-
> { **Common Sense** <note>original: "Sensus communis"; the faculty that integrates data from the five external senses.</note>
> { **Phantasy** <note>original: "Phantasia"; the ability to form mental images.</note>
> { **Estimative** <note>original: "Aestimativa"; the faculty that perceives intentions or qualities not sensed by the five senses, such as danger.</note>
> { **Memory**

They lie hidden, I say, within the small cells <note>original: "cellulis"; referring to the ventricles or cavities of the brain.</note>. For it is well known and widely accepted that there are four cells for the four internal senses. The first, which is the **Common Sense**, is located in the front part of the **Brain** <note>original: "Cerebri".</note>. The second, extending to the middle of the brain, is called the home of **Phantasy**. The third, touching upon that one, is called the house of the **Cogitative** <note>original: "Cogitativae"; here used interchangeably with the Estimative faculty in humans.</note>. The fourth, truly, belongs to **Memory**.

From the arrangement of these senses, you have the order of the operations of these powers for the completion of the work of memory. For these operations are so ordered that we cannot progress through one to the other until the final chamber of memory, unless we proceed successively from one into the next in the same order in which they were established by Mother Nature and arranged according to their position. For they are fashioned like four chambers, not indeed set apart, but placed one inside the other, so that the entrance to the fourth lies open through the third; to the third through the second; and to the second through the first. This is to say: nothing enters **Memory** except through the hall of the **Cogitative** (or **Estimative**); nothing enters the Cogitative except through the hall of **Phantasy**; and nothing enters Phantasy except through the hall of the **Common Sense**. But something must be said about each of these individually.

<summary>This page describes the "ventricular theory" of the brain, mapping the four internal senses—Common Sense, Phantasy, Estimation, and Memory—to specific physical cavities (cells) within the head. It emphasizes that information must pass through these faculties in a strict linear sequence to be successfully stored in the memory.</summary>

<keywords>internal senses, brain anatomy, ventricular theory, memory, phantasy, common sense, estimative faculty, cognitive process</keywords>

# Art Cabinet of the Academy: Second Class

The **Common Sense** <note>Latin: *Sensus Communis*. In medieval and Renaissance philosophy, this was not "good judgment" but a specific faculty in the brain that combined data from the five individual senses into a single experience.</note> therefore relates to the particular senses just as a center relates to its circumference; for the "species" <note>The "sensible species" were the mental images or impressions of objects transmitted from the eyes, ears, etc., to the mind.</note> of all those senses are received within it. It is that which has the power to distinguish and separate the impressions of one sense from those of another, as is evident in the following figure:

# ACADEMIC ART CABINET: SECOND CLASS

<margin>I.</margin> It perceives not only all sensible things, but also all the acts of all the external senses regarding those things. For since animals perceive that they themselves feel, see, hear, and so forth, and since this knowledge does not belong to any of the five external senses—because no material power can reflect upon its own action—it must necessarily belong to a higher and more universal power. To this power are brought the images not only of the Objects, but also of the external functions; this power is nothing other than the <term>Common Sense<note>original: "Sensus Communis." In medieval and early modern philosophy, this was a specific internal faculty that unified the data from the five individual senses.</note></term>.

II. It distinguishes and judges between the Objects of different senses, which none of the external senses can provide. Since each individual sense is occupied only with the differences of its own proper Object—as sight is concerned with black and white, and the remaining senses similarly—the common sense is therefore said to be, as it were, the Arbiter and Judge of the others.

III. It imparts to the external senses the power to perform their functions through the communication of <term>animal spirits<note>original: "spirituum animalium." These were thought to be subtle fluids or vapors that traveled through the nerves to facilitate movement and sensation.</note></term>, which it pours out from itself as if from a fountain and primary instrument of feeling. Hence, when those pathways of the brain where the power of the Common Sense resides are obstructed—through which the aforementioned spirits exit, as happens in sleep—it is necessary for the external senses to take a holiday <note>original: "feriari," meaning to rest or be idle.</note>.

As I say, this happens in sleep: for in that state, the common doorways to the external senses are obstructed by vapors ascending from the stomach into the head. For when someone gives themselves to rest, the brain is soon cooled a little and the vapors are thickened.

<summary>This page defines the "Common Sense" as a central faculty that allows for self-awareness of sensation, distinguishes between different types of sensory data (like color vs. sound), and regulates the flow of "animal spirits" that enable or disable the senses during sleep.</summary>
<keywords>Common Sense, animal spirits, perception, philosophy of mind, sleep, brain physiology, sensory integration</keywords>

This obstruction not only hinders the descent of the animal spirits from the brain, but also the ascent of the vital spirits from the heart to that same brain; as long as the cessation of this exchange lasts, so long does sleep persist.

*Phantasy* <note>original: "Phantasia"; here referring to the faculty of imagination and mental representation.</note> is the power of the soul by which external things, previously perceived by the common sense, are conceived, whether they are present or absent. Its duties are:
I. To retain for some time the "species" <note>In this context, "species" refers to the mental images or sensory impressions of objects.</note> brought in through the common sense.
II. To recognize objects, even when absent, through these types of species which are called phantasms.
III. To compose and also to partition or divide the sensed species—those first received by the external senses and then brought to itself through the common sense. From this manifold construction of species, innumerable things can be fashioned and conceived, which is why those who excel in phantasy are ingenious, industrious, and quick to devise many things.
IIII. To rule the animal itself; for just as humans are led by reason, so irrational animals are led by imagination.

The *Estimative* or *Cogitative* faculty has a threefold function:
I. To recognize through "non-sensed" species; namely, to recognize something under the category of a friend, or as useful or useless, known or unknown, pleasant or troublesome, kin or stranger; there are enumerated

### LECTURE VII.

#### Concerning the Sensitive Subject.

*1. What it is. 2. What things are contained under it. 3. How many external senses there are. 4. They require a medium so that the sensible object may be perceived. 5. The sensitive faculty is that power with which the soul perceives sensible things through all the senses, just as it perceives color through sight, sound through hearing, scent through smell, flavor through taste, and the tangible through touch.

<margin>3</margin>
There are, moreover, specific external senses.
->Five: { Sight, Hearing, Smell, Taste, Touch <-

This number is gathered from the five kinds of sensible things <note>Objects that can be perceived by the senses</note>, and from the fivefold sensory organ that is observed in an animal. The organs of the first four senses have their own peculiar seats in the head, but the organ of Touch, because of its necessity, is spread throughout the whole body.

<margin>4</margin>
These, therefore, are the five external senses, all of which require a medium: either an internal or "adherent" one within the living being itself—such as the skin between poured cold water and the flesh or muscles—or an external or "disjunct" one, such as the air between a color and the eye, so that the sensible object may be perceived. For a sensible object placed directly upon the sense is not felt, according to Aristotle <note>original: "sensibile enim supra sensum positum non sentitur ex Aristotele"; a reference to Aristotle's *De Anima*, suggesting that senses require a medium to function</note>. In this context, "sensible object" should not be understood as the sensible quality propagated from a body to the sense; for nothing prevents a quality of that kind from being carried all the way to the sensory organ and being received in it, and yet being perceived by it, as is evident in light, sound, smell, taste, and tactile qualities. Rather, it must be understood as the body itself from which such a quality flows; for in this way, a luminous body placed directly against the organ of sight is not perceived. # ACADEMIC CABINET OF ARTS. SECOND CLASS.

Finally, for the operation of the senses, one must admit the existence of what are called **Intentional Species** <note>original: "species Intentionales." In scholastic philosophy, these are not "species" in the biological sense, but "mental images" or "formal signs" that carry the likeness of an object to the mind.</note>, which are certain qualities, or formal signs of things presented to the senses. These, having been sent out by the Objects and received into the sense, have the power of representing the Object itself, even though the species themselves are not perceivable by the sense at all. These qualities are called "Intentional" because through them the sense "tends toward" <note>The author plays on the Latin "tendit," the root of "intention," meaning to stretch out toward something.</note> the Object. For example, when the eye perceives a distant color, a certain quality is received in the eye which, having been propagated from the color itself through the intervening air and received in the sense of sight, has the power of representing that color. 

I say that such species must be admitted so that **sensation** <term>sensation: the process of perceiving through the organs</term> can occur, because every action happens through contact. Thus, nothing acts at a distance except through some medium. Yet, a sensible object is distant from the sense and acts upon the sense; therefore, it acts through some medium. That medium cannot be more aptly identified than as the "species" which reaches from the object all the way to the sense. This explains why things are not seen in the dark, namely because colors need light to emit their species. Likewise, we do not behold things that are too far away, because the species projected from them fail in the middle of the journey, so that they cannot reach us at all. Those, however...

# Academic Cabinet of Arts, Class II.

Images <note>original: "species"; in scholastic philosophy, these are the forms or "likenesses" of objects that allow them to be perceived</note> are transmitted from the object to the sense, not by a physical transition—in the way an arrow is shot by an archer toward a target—but through a continuous propagation, just as heat reaches a warmed object from a fire. And although the propagation of these intentional images <note>original: "specierum Intentionalium"; these are non-material representations that carry the "intent" or form of the object to the mind</note> is instantaneous, it is nevertheless understood by its nature that the object diffuses its image into the part nearest to itself before the part further away. Nor are such faculties to be admitted only in the external senses, but also in the internal ones. Let these general remarks suffice regarding the external senses; now we shall speak of them specifically.

->**On Sight**<-

<margin>6</margin> The organ of sight is the eye, the structure of which you will find described by the Opticians <note>referring to authors of treatises on optics and anatomy</note>.

Its adequate object is the "visible," that is, whatever can be seen primarily and by itself. Of this kind are light (which is a "passible quality" <note>a quality capable of acting upon a sense</note> and therefore material, yet most subtle and the most noble of all bodily qualities) and color, which is a passible quality in a defined body having the power to change a medium that is actually transparent. For whatever is seen is perceived either insofar as it is colored or insofar as it is luminous.

Vision occurs once the images or intentional species have been received from the object.

->**On Hearing**<-

The faculty of Hearing resides in the organ of the ears as its subject.

Its adequate object is sound, which is

<summary>This page explains the philosophical mechanism of sensory perception as a continuous propagation of "images" rather than physical projectiles, specifically defining the mechanics and objects of sight and hearing.</summary>
<keywords>sensory perception, intentional species, optics, light and color, theory of vision, hearing, scholastic philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 65 ---
<meta>This page continues the academic classification of sensory perceptions, moving from the general study of physics to the specific nature of sound and acoustics.</meta>

->Artistic Cabinet of the Academy, 2nd Class.<-

<margin>C iij</margin>

Sound is a perceptible quality affecting the sense of hearing. Moreover, sound is produced through the local motion <note>physical movement from one place to another</note> of bodies that are struck against one another. For since there is always some medium between the objects being struck which is forced out by their collision, sound follows from this expulsion and striking of the intervening body. In this way, the air between two rubbed bodies is first broken, and then, being broken, it makes a sound. Furthermore, the bodies that produce sound are called "sounding" not because they are affected by sound, but because they are the cause of it.

The medium through which sound is carried to the hearing is air and water: not only intentionally <note>original: "intentionaliter"; in medieval philosophy, this refers to the "image" or "species" of a thing traveling through space</note>, that is, as far as its appearance is concerned, but also really <note>original: "realiter"; meaning the physical vibration actually moves through the substance</note>. This is certainly true of air, and it is established by experience regarding water; for fish perceive sounds made in the nearby air while under the water, and croaking frogs are heard from within the water by us while we are in the air. The same is to be thought of elemental fire as is thought of air. As far as the earth is concerned, it is probable that the "image" of sound can be propagated through it to some extent, but not the sound itself, because its excessive solidity stands in the way; the same must be said of other solid bodies and especially of the Heavens—namely, that sound can be propagated through them intentionally, but not really.

Among the types of sound are the Voice and the Echo. The Voice is proper to animals—not indeed to all, but to those in whom the organs necessary for forming it are found. It is defined thus: The Voice is a sound produced by the mouth of an animal in the air drawn in by the lungs, with a certain intention of signifying <note>meaningful communication</note>, especially in humans.

<note>Vocabulary: sound, local motion, air, water, Voice, Echo, intentionally, really, organs</note>

It also distinguishes the "Voice" as a specific type of sound produced by animals with the intent to communicate.</summary>
<keywords>acoustics, physics of sound, sensory perception, vocal organs, Aristotelian science, mediums of sound</keywords>
