# Art Cabinet of the Academy, Class II.

An echo is a reflected sound: and it occurs when a concave body is encountered from which the sound, or its <term>species<note>In medieval science, a "species" was a likeness or formal image of an object that traveled through the medium to the sense organ.</note></term>, is reflected. However, almost nothing but the final words are heard in an echo; for although a reflection of the entire voice occurs, the earlier parts are disturbed by those immediately following, and therefore only the final parts—whose return is unimpeded—are perceived by us.

->On the Sense of Smell<-

The object of smelling is odor, which is a quality perceptible by scent that spreads through air and water. Regarding air, it is self-evident; regarding water, it is proven because even at night, fish situated under the water hasten to their food, attracted by the scent. The act of smelling occurs when the "species" of the odor is received, whether <term>intentionally or physically<note>The author distinguishes between the mental representation (intentional) and the physical particles (real/physical) of the scent.</note></term>, into the faculty of smelling itself. This faculty resides in two small portions of the nostrils which are of a fleshy substance.

->On the Sense of Taste<-

The object of taste is flavor, which is a "secondary, passive quality" affecting the sense of taste. The action of tasting occurs when the "species" is received into its organ, and this happens without an external medium—in which it differs from the three preceding senses <note>Sight, hearing, and smell require air or water to travel; taste requires direct contact.</note> and agrees with the sense of touch. However, it is not without an internal medium, namely the skin and perhaps the spongy flesh; through this intermediary, the flavor "intentionally" alters the faculty of tasting located in the tongue. The entire power of taste is in the tip or point of the tongue, because that part of the tongue is softer, and its nerves are more suited for perceiving flavors.

<keywords>acoustics, echo, olfaction, gustation, sensory perception, species intentionalis, anatomy of the tongue, aquatic biology</keywords>

Artistic Cabinet of the Academy. Class II.
C iiij

### On the Sense of Touch.

The object of Touch in general can be described as any quality perceptible by touch. Such qualities are either primary or secondary. The primary ones are the four common qualities: *Heat, Cold, Moisture, and Dryness*; the secondary ones are *Hardness, Softness, Roughness, Smoothness, etc.*

This sense claims a unique characteristic for itself: it requires no medium, whether external or internal, to perceive its object. Indeed, experience shows that it requires no external medium—just like the sense of taste—since we see that external bodies like water and air act immediately through their qualities upon the sensory organ of touch, or rather, they change and sensibly affect the animal body even if nothing is placed between them and the body.

Furthermore, it is proven that there is no need for an internal medium because the organ of Touch is the flesh, which encompasses both the skin and the nerves, although the flesh can in some way be called an internal medium <note>The author refers to the Aristotelian debate on whether flesh is the actual organ of touch or merely the medium through which sensations reach the nerves.</note> with respect to the nerve.

The organ of Touch is not fixed to any single part of the body, but is spread through all its members. However, that organ of Touch is not the flesh alone, nor the nerve alone, but both; here the name "flesh" is used in a broad sense insofar as it encompasses the skin, membranes, and other things of that kind.

# Art Cabinet of the Academy. II. Class.

### Lecture VIII.

### Concerning the Vegetative Subject.

*1. What it is. 2. In this are the specific principles and rules by which it acts. 3. It transforms the elemental into its own species. 4. The functions of the vegetative soul and its faculties, with their distinctions. 5. The general instrument of these powers. 6. Concerning these powers specifically.*

1 The Vegetative is that power through which a transformation from one substance into another occurs naturally. Therefore, we call "vegetative" that which possesses being and life, such as those things which are generated, nourished, and grown—like trees and plants, which live although they lack sensation <note>The author follows the Aristotelian hierarchy where plants have life but lack the "sensitive soul" found in animals</note>.

2 In the vegetative power are specific principles and rules with which plants act according to the species in which they exist. For the Oak acts according to its own species, Pepper and the Rose according to theirs, and the Lily according to its own.

3 This same power transforms the elemental <note>original: "elementatiuum," referring to the raw material of the four elements</note> into its own species by way of generation; from this, it lives, is nourished, and grows. For this reason, the vegetative power dies when the elemental material fails it.

4 Furthermore, the functions of the Vegetative Soul are three:

->Namely { Nutrition<-
->Growth<-
->Generation.<-

# Art Cabinet of the Academy, Class II.

The first of these pertains to the preservation of the Individual, the second to perfection according to the mass of the body, and the third to the propagation and preservation of the species. According to the number of these operations, three powers are likewise

->Established: { The Nutritive, The Augmentative, The Procreative.<-

Between these there is no small difference. First, because the object of nutrition is food insofar as it is substance; of accretion <note>growth or increase</note> insofar as it is quantity; and finally, of procreation insofar as something similar to the living being can be produced through its intervention.

The second difference is that the nutritive power persists throughout the entire duration of life, the augmentative <note>growing</note> power ceases when the living being reaches the age of consistency <note>physical maturity or "the prime of life"</note>, and the procreative power fails in those whose age is already exhausted <note>past child-bearing years</note>.

The third is that both the nourishing and the increasing faculties are diffused throughout the entire body, whereas the procreative faculty occupies a certain and defined seat—namely, where the surplus of food receives its seminal power.

The general instrument of the faculties of the vegetative soul <note>original: "anima vegetans"; the basic level of soul responsible for growth and reproduction in plants and animals</note> is the natural heat implanted by nature in every living thing, which for that reason they call native or natural heat. When this heat perishes, every operation of the living being dies away; for which reason Aristotle defines Death in the 5th book of the *Politics*, chapter 8 <note>The author likely intends a reference to Aristotle's "On Youth, Old Age, Life and Death," as the Politics does not typically define death in this manner</note> as: The extinction of the native heat within the moisture.

<summary>This page outlines the three primary powers of the vegetative soul—nutrition, growth, and reproduction—and distinguishes them by their duration, location in the body, and purpose. It concludes by identifying "natural heat" as the essential engine of life, the loss of which results in death.</summary>

# ACADEMIC CABINET ARTICLE II. CLASS.

Another heat can also be excited in a living being by external causes, which they call "acquired" <note>Latin: *ascititium*</note>, by which the natural heat can be assisted, or indeed sometimes even supplemented; for example, when warm medicines are applied to cherish the heat of the stomach and assist digestion <note>Latin: *concoctionem*, referring to the internal "cooking" or processing of food</note>.

<margin>6</margin> And let these things be said in general regarding the powers of the vegetative soul; those things which follow pertain to them specifically.

### ON THE FACULTY OF NOURISHING,
->*or nutrition.*<-

The faculty of nourishing converts food into the substance of the thing which is being nourished. The necessity of this power is gathered from the fact that heat daily thins out the radical moisture <note>The "radical moisture" (*humidum radicale*) was believed to be the vital fluid or "oil" that fueled the lamp of life</note>; therefore, it is necessary that by taking food, and by that food being converted into the substance of the living thing, that which has been lost is continually repaired.

Moreover, food is established as being of two types: proximate and remote. Proximate food is that which, having already been properly prepared and "digested" <note>Latin: *decoctum*, literally "boiled down"</note>, is immediately converted into the substance of the living being, such as the blood in the individual parts of the limbs. Remote food is that which requires some, or certainly multiple, "cookings" <note>Latin: *coctione*, the metabolic process of refining nutrients</note> performed by the living being before it can be converted into its own substance. This latter type, however, must be partly similar and partly dissimilar. For if the food were entirely similar before the digestion process, it would not need to be digested, because no action occurs between things that are entirely identical; if, however, it were en-

# Art Cabinet of the Academy: Second Class

...altogether dissimilar, so that the nature of the living being would utterly shrink from it, it could not be digested by it. Secondly, it must be a **mixed substance** <note>original: "substantia mixta"; a substance composed of multiple elements rather than a single pure one</note>, so that a pure element cannot be nourishment. Since nourishment must be converted into the substance of the living being, it must be such that its qualities can be broken down by the living being itself and reduced to a suitable moderation. However, **pure elements** <note>original: "elementa pura"</note> possess qualities repugnant to the living being and are far more powerful, such as the extreme heat of **fire** or the extreme cold of **water**. Nevertheless, nothing prevents some elements—when placed outside their natural state and mixed together—from being sufficient for nourishing certain living beings. For it is well known that the entire **genus of plants** <note>original: "stirpium genus"</note> draws its food through fibers and roots from earth moistened by water. Thus, certain animals seem to be sustained by the earth, such as **moles**, others from water, such as **fish**, others from the air, such as the **Chameleon**, and others from fire, such as **fire-flies** <note>original: "pyraustae"; mythical insects believed to live in fire</note>; yet in truth, they are not nourished by those elements in their pure form, but by the mixture of other small bodies joined with them, for among us, no pure elements exist.

<header>ACADEMIC ART CABINET. SECOND CLASS.</header>

### Concerning the Faculty of Growth.

Growth <note>original: "Accretio"</note> is a change according to quantity, by which the same living individual is led from a smaller and imperfect size to a larger size appropriate to its nature. Although inanimate things sometimes take on an increase—such as fossils or minerals in the bowels of the earth—nevertheless, the growth of living things differs from that type of increase in three primary ways. First, because this growth occurs through internal reception <note>the intake of nutrients into the body</note>; second, it proceeds from an intrinsic source; and finally, it occurs according to all parts, even down to the most inward. In contrast, that improperly named "Growth" <insert>seen in minerals</insert> occurs through the juxtaposition of matter from the outside, and only according to that specific part where the matter is placed.

You will note, however, that if more substance is acquired from food than flows out of the body, the living being is said to be both nourished and to grow at the same time. But if only as much is added as is lost daily, then only a regeneration <note>original: "adgeneratio"</note> occurs, rather than a growth of the entire living being; thus, the living being is nourished but does not grow. Here it must be observed that not just any acquisition of quality is properly called growth, but only that through which the living being acquires a greater mass of the entire body; otherwise, a living being could be said to grow throughout its entire lifespan.

<vocab>Growth, Faculty of Growth, fossils, minerals, internal reception, juxtaposition, nourishment, regeneration</vocab>

<summary>This page defines biological growth as an internal, holistic process that increases a living being's total mass, distinguishing it from the external accumulation seen in minerals. It further differentiates between simple nourishment (maintaining mass) and true growth (increasing mass).</summary>
<keywords>biology, growth, nutrition, minerals, physiology, Aristotelian science, regeneration</keywords>

--- Page 73 ---
<meta>This page continues the academic classification of biological faculties, moving from general nutrition to the specific mechanics of reproduction.</meta>

# ACADEMIC CABINET OF ARTS. CLASS II.

### *On the Power of Procreation.*

We have stated above that this power claims for itself a specific seat, namely where the surplus of nourishment receives its seminal force. But what is Procreation? Procreation is understood in two ways: in one sense, for any production of a living thing by anything else, which includes the production of imperfect animals <note>The author refers to "spontaneous generation," an old belief that small creatures like insects arose from non-living matter.</note> from equivocal causes out of decaying matter; and in this way it is defined as *The proceeding of a living thing from something*. In the second way, more properly, for the production of a living thing from another living thing, in which latter way it is defined as *The proceeding of a living thing from a joined principle of life according to the rule of likeness, at least in the lowest species*. It is said (*from a joined principle of life*) so that you may understand that the living thing must be joined to the producer at the start of production, at least by reason of the principle; thus the Lion was begotten in the generating Lion by reason of the seed. Due to the lack of this condition, the proceeding of Angels and of any created things from God is not called procreation <note>The author distinguishes between "creation" by God and "procreation" by nature; the former does not involve a physical "seed" or shared biological principle.</note>. It is added (*according to the rule of likeness*) so that you may understand that what is produced by this procreation must be similar to the generator, if not in sex or outward appearance, at least in nature.

<summary>This page defines the faculty of procreation, distinguishing between spontaneous generation from decay and true biological reproduction, which requires a shared nature and a physical connection between parent and offspring.</summary>
<keywords>procreation, spontaneous generation, principle of life, biological likeness, scholastic biology, reproduction</keywords>

# ACADEMIC KABBALISTIC ART. SECOND CLASS.

### LECTURE IX.

### Concerning the First Subject, or the Elementative.

> 1. *What the Elementative is.* 2. *What it contains under itself.* 3. *What an Element is.* 4. *How many kinds there are, how many primary qualities, and their combinations.* 5. *The ladder of nature.*

<margin>1</margin>The Elementative is the power within "Elementated" things <note>Things composed of the elements</note> through which the Elements exist and act in reality, <margin>2</margin>being present and powerful within those composed things themselves. Furthermore, the Elementative possesses only a substantial being and contains under itself, first, the four simple Elements; then, all inanimate mixtures in which there is no life, whether they are "perfect," such as metals and gemstones, or "imperfect," such as snow, hail, and rain. It also includes any other composed things whatever that are made of the four Elements, considered here without regard to life—even if they are not actually devoid of life—such as animals, plants, and man according to his physical body.

<margin>3</margin>It therefore encompasses the Elements: But what is an Element? Properly speaking, an Element is the same as a principle from which something is inwardly made, just as matter and form are called the elements of natural things. It is defined by Aristotle in the 5th book of his *Metaphysics*, chapter 3, as: *That from which something is first composed, being inwardly present and indivisible into another species.* <note>original: "Id ex quo primo insito & indiuisibili in aliam speciem aliquid componitur."</note> And since the four commonly known simple bodies <note>Earth, Water, Air, and Fire</note> are those from which all other bodies primarily consist, and into which they are resolved—since they do not consist of others, nor [are they resolved] into...

<meta>catchword: into</meta>

<summary>This lecture defines the "Elementative" as the foundational power of physical matter, encompassing both the four simple elements and all inanimate or physical compositions, including the human body. It cites Aristotle to define an element as the primary, indivisible building block of a substance.</summary>
<keywords>Elementative, four elements, Aristotle, Metaphysics, inanimate matter, metals, gemstones, physical body, matter and form</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the academic classification of natural philosophy, specifically focusing on the definition and categorization of the four elements. It follows the standard Aristotelian framework used in university curricula of the period.</meta>

# Art Cabinet of the Academy: Second Class

...others are resolved, for this reason they are called Elements <note>original: "Elementa"</note>. Therefore, in this context, an Element is a simple body into which other bodies are resolved. It is called a **Body** (*Corpus*) to exclude mere matter and form <note>In Scholastic philosophy, "matter" and "form" are abstract principles; a "body" is their physical manifestation</note>. Secondly, it is called **simple** (*simplex*) to reject any kind of mixtures. Thirdly, it is said to be **that into which others are resolved** to remove the heavens from consideration; for although the heaven is a simple body, other things are not resolved into it.

However, when an Element is called a "simple body," it should not be understood as if it were not composed of matter and form—otherwise it would not be a natural body—but rather because it cannot be further resolved into anything else. Here it must be noted that the essential forms of the Elements are not the "primary qualities" as some have thought (for example, believing the form of fire is simply extreme heat, etc.). Instead, they are **substantial acts** <term>substantial acts: the essential reality that makes a thing what it is</term> distinct from the primary qualities; for a substantial whole cannot consist of things that are not themselves substances.

There are neither more nor fewer than four Elements,

<margin>4</margin>
Namely:
-> Fire, which is above the air, near the hollow of the Moon, <-
-> Air, which is distributed into three regions: the highest, middle, and lowest, <-
-> Water, <-
-> Earth. <-

For it is discovered by experience that a mixture is resolved into these four Elements, as is evident in wood

<meta>catchword: wood</meta>

<summary>This page defines the "Element" as a simple body that serves as the final point of physical decomposition, specifically identifying the four classical elements—Fire, Air, Water, and Earth—and arguing that their essence is a substantial form rather than just a collection of qualities like heat or cold.</summary>
<keywords>Elements, Aristotelian physics, substantial form, four elements, cosmology, matter and form, Scholasticism</keywords>

# Art Cabinet of the Academy, Second Class

...which, while it is being burned, is resolved into flame, smoke, foam, and ashes. <note>The author is likely using the burning of wood as a classic Aristotelian proof that all things are composed of the four elements.</note>

The same is gathered from many indications, such as that there are only four primary qualities, each of which predominates in one specific Element.

Namely:
-> **Heat** in fire, <-
-> **Moisture** in air, <-
-> **Cold** in water, <-
-> **Dryness** in earth. <-

Secondly, that there are four combinations of these same primary qualities, each of which belongs to one specific Element,

Specifically:
-> **Heat and dryness** to fire, <-
-> **Moisture and heat** to air, <-
-> **Coldness and moisture** to water, <-
-> **Dryness and cold** to earth. <-

It belongs to them, I say, but in such a way that the qualities which are first in order in each combination meet in an *intense degree* <term>intenso: the highest or most concentrated state of a quality</term>, while those that follow meet in a *diminished degree* <term>remisso: a weakened or less concentrated state</term>. For fire is supremely hot and diminished in dryness; air is supremely moist and diminished in heat; water is supremely cold and diminished in moisture; Earth is supremely dry and diminished in cold. 

However, we are speaking here of the Elements considered according to their natural state, for in their present state, that temperament of qualities is often changed. For air is not always supremely moist, nor only diminished in heat, but sometimes it is also simultaneously intensely cold—at least in this lowest region of the air during winter time—nor is water always supremely cold, but sometimes...

...than moderately warm or lukewarm, especially in the summer. The same can be said of the earth, which in Autumn and Spring—indeed, at almost any time—appears moist, and sometimes seems more warm than cold; this arises from the proximity of the Elements and their mutual struggle against one another.

Here you will note in passing that among those primary qualities, some are called **Symbolic** <note>original: "Symbolas," meaning compatible or sharing a common trait</note> in the elements, while others are called **Dissymbolic** <note>original: "dissymbolas," meaning incompatible</note>. Those are called Symbolic in which two Elements agree, such as heat in fire and air, or cold in water and earth. Those are called Dissymbolic in which they disagree, such as heat and cold in respect to fire and water, or moisture and dryness in respect to air and earth. From this, certain Elements are called **Symbolic**, namely those which agree in some of the aforementioned qualities: such as fire and air in heat; air and water in moisture; water and earth in cold; and finally, earth and fire in dryness. However, others are called **Dissymbolic** because they agree in none of these, such as fire and water, or air and earth.

Thirdly, it is shown that there are four Elements because there are four **humors** <note>The "humors" were the four bodily fluids thought to control health and temperament in pre-modern medicine.</note> in human bodies:

> Blood, which corresponds to Air,
> Yellow bile or "cholera," which corresponds to Fire,
> Black bile or "melancholy," which corresponds to Earth,
> Phlegm, which corresponds to Water.

Fourthly, because there are four seasons of the year which also correspond to each Element...

Under this, there are many degrees of dignity according to which one thing has a more noble being than another: for where there are more noble properties, there is a more noble being. Thus, there is a more noble being in fire than in air, because in the former there is greater subtlety and lightness than in the latter. ### LECTURE X.
->Concerning subject K, or the Instrumental.<-
->1. *What it is.* 2. *What it contains under itself.* 3. *In how many ways it is considered.*<-

<margin>1 2 3</margin>
The Instrumental is that power which is the instrument of a substance, with which it performs some work and which cannot exist by itself. It contains under itself everything that has its being in another as in a primary subject, and it is considered in two ways: either absolutely according to itself, as if one speaks of Justice considered in itself; or in the manner in which it is joined to some subject, as if one speaks of the Justice of God, or of man, and then it is reduced to the category of its subject, whose conditions must be observed. But because we are going to treat this final subject more accurately in the following Class, let us withdraw this second Class from the sea <note>original: "ab æquore." A metaphorical expression for concluding a section of study.</note>.

and there is nothing in this entire universe of things, whether it be substantial or accidental <note>A "substance" is a thing that exists in itself, like a tree; an "accident" is a quality that exists only in something else, like the greenness of the tree.</note>, which does not belong to one of these.

<margin>3</margin> Furthermore, the natural arrangement of each highest class and of those things which fall under it is called either a **Category** (a term which signifies a series of many predicable things <note>Things that can be "predicated" or stated about a subject.</note> under one highest class, in which series or coordination the higher things, called **categoremata**, declare something about the lower things) or, in Latin, a **predicament**—that is, a receptacle of predicables.

<margin>4</margin> It must be known, however, that under each supreme class there is a double series: one is **direct**, in which many things lie directly beneath the supreme class, such as **genera**, **species**, and **individuals**; yet that order is preserved so that those which are prior, higher, or more general are closer to the supreme class, while those which are posterior, lower, and less universal are placed in a location closer to the individuals. The other series is **indirect** or **lateral**, which contains the differences that divide the genera and constitute the species; likewise the internal principles and...

Furthermore, since a <term>Categorical term</term> <note>Latin: *terminus Categoricus*; a word or concept that fits into one of Aristotle's ten basic classifications of reality</note> can be defined generally as that which is contained within the scope of any Category, but more specifically as the supreme genus of each Category—and since there are ten supreme genera—it follows that ten categorical terms are established in a general sense: namely, *Substance, Quantity, etc.* Specifically, however, there are as many Categorical terms as there are kinds of things contained under those supreme genera.

Besides Categorical terms, there are others called transcendent and super-transcedent. A <term>transcendent term</term> <note>Latin: *terminus transcendens*</note> is so named because it is seen to transcend the boundaries of the Categories; it is far more universal than a categorical term, as it can be said of every true and real being. There are six such terms, which are expressed by the technical mnemonic word *Reubau*.

> Namely:
> { Thing,
> { Being,
> { One,
> { Good,
> { Something,
> { True.
<note>The mnemonic *Reubau* is formed from the first letters of these six Latin terms: Res, Ens, Unum, Bonum, Aliquid, Verum.</note>

A <term>super-transcendent</term> <note>Latin: *supertranscendens*</note> term, however, is one that is even more universal than a transcendent one; it is truly predicated not only of every real being, but also of those that are fictitious and imaginary.

> Such as:
> { Imaginable
> { Thinkable
> { Conceivable, etc.

Now, we shall speak of each Category individually.

### LECTURE II.

### On Substance.

->*1. What it is. 2. Its properties. 3. Its sequence.*<-

<margin>1</margin>Substance is a subsisting being, or that which exists by itself. This definition applies not only to primary or particular substances—that is, Individuals <note>original: "Indiuiduis"</note> which primarily subsist by themselves—but also to secondary or universal substances. For although they do not subsist insofar as they are universal and abstracted, they are nevertheless fit to subsist within a particular thing. Six properties <margin>2</margin> of Substance are handed down by Aristotle <note>The Greek philosopher whose "Categories" defined these logical terms for centuries of students.</note>.

I. Not to be in a subject: understand this as not being in a subject by way of "inherence" in the manner of accidents <note><term>Accidents</term>: qualities like color or size that cannot exist without being "in" a substance.</note>. This applies not so much to secondary substances as to primary ones.

II. To be spoken of synonymously: this applies only to secondary substances, for primary or particular substances are not predicated of many things.

III. That a primary substance signifies "this certain thing" <note>original: "hoc aliquid"</note>, that is, a particular nature; whereas a secondary substance signifies "of what sort" <note>original: "quale quid"</note>, that is, a nature affected by a certain quality, namely universality.

IIII. To have no contrary: this indeed applies to all substances, but not to substance alone, for there are certain accidents which have no contrary, such as quantity and some qualities. And although certain substances seem to fight amongst themselves, such as fire and water, they do so not insofar as they are substances, but insofar as they are endowed with contrary qualities—namely heat and cold, or moist-

<summary>This page defines substance as an independent being and begins listing its six Aristotelian properties, distinguishing between individual objects and universal categories. It specifically addresses why substances like fire and water are not true logical contraries despite their physical opposition.</summary>
<keywords>Substance, Aristotle, Primary Substance, Secondary Substance, Accidents, Logic, Universal vs Particular, Contraries</keywords>

--- Page 85 ---
<meta>This page continues the scholastic discussion of the properties of substance, likely following a list of its primary characteristics.</meta>

# Art. Kab. Acad. III. Classis.
<note>Art. Kab. Acad. III. Classis: Likely an abbreviation for "Artistic Cabinet of the Academy, Third Class," referring to the archival classification of this manuscript.</note>

...are endowed with [moisture] and dryness, and thus substance is not contrary to substance by itself. Nor should you say that opposite differences are contraries—for example, <term>rational and irrational</term><note>original: "rationale & irrationale"</note>—for even if they are opposed, they are not, however, contraries in the proper sense.

V. Not to admit of "more" and "less"; or, it cannot be predicated according to "more" and "less" as certain <term>accidents</term><note>In philosophy, an "accident" is a quality that can change without changing the essence of the thing, like hair color.</note> usually are. For example, heat and cold; this arises from the fact that those accidents, according to their <term>entity</term><note>original: "entitatem"; referring here to the essential being or nature of the quality.</note>, are shared by their subjects to a greater or lesser degree. This, however, cannot be said of substance, whose being is indivisible in terms of how it is shared.

VI. That the same numerical substance can admit of contraries, which must be understood according to different intervals of time; for contraries cannot exist in the same subject at the same time, at least not in an intense degree. Yet one must not think that every substance is capable of every contrariety; indeed, there can be a certain substance free from all contrariety—namely, the <term>Heavens</term><note>original: "Cœlum"; in Aristotelian physics, the celestial bodies were thought to be made of a "fifth element" (aether) that was incorruptible and lacked the opposing qualities found on Earth.</note>—into which contraries cannot fall. Furthermore, a swan, being white by its very nature, cannot admit of blackness, just as a raven cannot admit of whiteness, even though they may admit of other kinds of contraries.

<summary>This page outlines the final logical properties of "substance," arguing that substances do not have opposites, do not exist in degrees of "more or less," and can host contrary qualities (like hot and cold) only at different times.</summary>
<keywords>substance, Aristotelian logic, accidents, contraries, celestial bodies, rationality, entity</keywords>

### LECTURE III.
### On Quantity.

*1. What it is. 2. In what the essential nature of quantity consists. 3. The types of quantity. 4. Properties.*

Quantity is the principle by reason of which created beings are finite or limited; or, it is the expression by which everything that exists outside of nothingness is measured as a "how much." And if you prefer an essential definition: Quantity is the principle <note>original: "ratio"</note> of the extension of parts in relation to themselves: Where by (*the principle-*

# Art Cabinet of the Academy, Class III

By <term>formal reason</term><note>The essential definition or core nature of a thing</note> is understood the underlying principle; by **Extension** is meant a certain order and position; and by **Parts** are understood the integral parts of a bodily substance. It is added, **In relation to itself**, lest you think that extension in relation to a place is essential. For there is a twofold extension of a quantified thing: one is internal and entirely removed from the senses—namely, the extension of parts in relation to themselves—by the arrival of which the integral parts of a substance, previously confused and mixed together, are distinguished and hold an order and position among themselves. In this lies the essential nature of quantity. <margin>2</margin> This opinion is confirmed by faith, which teaches that in the **Sacrament of the Eucharist**, the body of Christ has all its quantity which it has in Heaven, yet without extension in relation to a place <note>A reference to the Catholic doctrine of Transubstantiation, where Christ's body is believed to be present "substantially" rather than occupying space in the usual physical way</note>. The other extension is external, which is nothing other than a property of quantity flowing from that internal and essential extension, because the subject becomes divisible, measurable, equal or unequal to another, finite or infinite, one or manifold.

<margin>3</margin> Furthermore, the thing to which those extended parts belong is either permanent, or flowing and successive. Of the permanent, there are three species:

Namely $\left\{ \begin{array}{l} \text{The Line,} \\ \text{The Surface,} \\ \text{The Body or thickness.} \end{array} \right.$

->Of successive quantity, however, there are two<-

# Academic Art Cabinet, Class III

->Namely:<-
| | |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Quantity** | **Motion** |
| | **Time**, insofar as it is the internal measure of any motion; for insofar as it is an external measure, it belongs to the Category of "When" <note>The author distinguishes between time as an inherent duration of an action versus time as a specific point on a calendar or clock.</note>. |

A **Line** is length without width, and it is either straight or curved.

A **Surface** is width without depth, and it is either flat or curved.

A **Body** is a triple quantity, consisting of length, width, and depth, and it is either spherical, angular, or mixed.

**Motion** is the flow of form: or it is the continuous and uninterrupted succession of the parts of change.

Moreover, motion is threefold:

I. **Accretion and Decretion**, <note>original: "Accretionis & decretionis"</note> or increase and decrease according to quantity.

II. **Alteration** according to quality.

III. **Lation** <term>Lation: a technical term for local motion or change of place</term> or local motion according to place, and this is straight (upward or downward), circular, or mixed.

**Time** is the duration of motion by reason of prior and posterior parts: or according to Aristotle, the measure of motion according to "before" and "after." <note>Aristotle, Physics, Book IV.</note> This species is distinguished from motion, however, because motion is the succession of the parts of movement itself, while time is the duration of that succession.

Besides these five legitimate species of **Quantity**, <note>The five being Line, Surface, Body, Motion, and Time.</note> there are three other "bastard" <note>original: "nothæ"</note> and less legitimate species, of which...

# ART. KAB. ACAD. III. CLASSIS. 61

The first is *Number*, which is called discrete quantity, because it consists of distinct or separate parts, in contrast to continuous quantity, which consists of parts joined together by a common bond. <note>Discrete quantity refers to things you can count (like people), while continuous quantity refers to things you measure (like a line).</note>

The second is Speech, which consists of many syllables; moreover, each individual syllable is an articulated sound distinct from every other syllable.

The third would be Place, were it not more properly belonging to the Category of **Where**. <note>original: "Ubi". In Aristotelian logic, "Where" is one of the ten basic categories of being.</note>

The properties of Quantity, besides those we have noted in passing, are three:

I. That nothing is contrary to Quantity. This must be understood as referring to "proper" contrariety; for a certain "improper" contrariety is found between a greater and a lesser quantity. <note>In logic, opposites like "black" and "white" are contraries, but a large number is not the logical opposite of a small number in the same way.</note>

II. That Quantity does not admit of "more" or "less," even though it admits of "plus" and "minus." The reason is that multiple parts of quantity cannot be received into the same part of a subject, in the way that multiple degrees of the same quality—for example, heat—can be found in the same part of a subject. Thus, one thing is called hotter than another not because it has more "hot parts," but because it has "hotter" parts. <note>You cannot make a three-foot line "more" three feet long, whereas you can make a warm room "more" warm.</note>

III. That things are named equal or unequal because of it. This should be understood as referring to "proper" equality or inequality—namely, of extension. Otherwise, equality and inequality can be spoken of in other classes of things, but less properly; for instance, we call two equally learned men "equal," but this is an equality of perfection <insert>rather than size</insert>.

<vocab>Number, Speech, Quantity, Category of Where, contrariety, equality, inequality</vocab>

<summary>This page defines the species of discrete quantity, such as number and speech, and outlines the three primary logical properties that distinguish quantity from other categories like quality.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotelian logic, discrete quantity, continuous quantity, properties of quantity, equality, contrariety, philosophy of language</keywords>

# Art Cabinet of the Academy, Class III.

### Lecture IV.

### On Quality.

> 1. *The necessity of this category in this Lullian art. 2. What Quality is. 3. Proper and appropriated quality. 4. Appropriated qualities exist not only in created things but also in God. 5. The four species of quality. 6. The properties of the same.*

<margin>1</margin>
The knowledge of this category <note>original: "prædicamenti"; referring to one of Aristotle's ten categories of being</note> is extremely necessary in this Lullian art <note>The "Ars Lulliana" is a philosophical system created by Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1316) using logical combinations of concepts to find universal truths</note>, so that those things which we are unable to know through a formal definition, we may at least be able to know through a description. We achieve this by establishing the thing through its quantity (which was discussed above) and its quality (which we discuss now), applying all these things infinitely to God, as is fitting, but finitely to created things.

<margin>2</margin>
Quality, therefore, is the principle by reason of which things are "of a certain sort" <note>original: "quales"</note>; thus, by reason of Justice, a man is called just; by reason of goodness, good; by reason of whiteness, white. Or: quality is the secondary perfection of each being, whether it be proper or appropriated. It is called a *secondary perfection* because it derives its origin from the essential or primary perfection. For this reason, we call it "proper," and it is defined thus: Quality which belongs to a thing through itself, and not through another, such as the ability to laugh <note>original: "risibilitas"</note> belongs to a man, the ability to neigh <note>original: "hinnibilitas"</note> to a horse, heat to fire, moisture to air, and dryness to earth. It is said secondly, *Proper or appropriated,* because besides the proper qua-

<summary>This lecture introduces the category of Quality within the Lullian philosophical system, defining it as a "secondary perfection" that allows us to describe things—including God and nature—when a direct definition is impossible.</summary>
<keywords>Quality, Lullian Art, secondary perfection, logic, Aristotle's categories, Ramon Llull, properties of elements</keywords>

# Art Cabinet of the Academy: Third Class

...qualities which belong to a thing by its own nature, there are also other **appropriated perfections**, which belong to things through something else, such as heat in the air which is introduced by the power of fire; the moisture of water, which is caused by the air; and the dryness in fire, which it possesses by reason of the earth. <note>The author is using the Aristotelian theory of the four elements—earth, air, fire, and water—to explain how objects can take on characteristics from one another.</note> From this it follows that all qualities, both primary and secondary, are "appropriated" in composite bodies, and this occurs by reason of the elements which are virtually contained within them.

<margin>4</margin>
Appropriated perfections are also found in God; although by their nature these are common to all three Persons <note>The Father, Son, and Holy Spirit</note>, nevertheless, because of something fitting to one Person and not another, certain perfections become appropriated. For **Power** is attributed to the Father, since He is the principle <note>The source or origin</note> of the entire Trinity; **Wisdom** is appropriated to the Son, because He is generated by the Father by way of the Intellect, and His Intellect is subsisting wisdom and the highest perfection; to the Holy Spirit, however, **Goodness** is appropriated, because He is breathed forth from the Will, which regards the divine essence as the highest good.

<margin>5</margin>
The species of Quality, to which every kind of quality must be referred as if to main headings.

->There are four: { Habit and disposition,<-
->Natural power and incapacity,<-
->Affective quality and passion,<-
->Figure and form.<-

# ACADEMIC CABINET OF ARTS: THIRD CLASS

...as an incipient state, not yet firmly rooted in the subject. From this, it is understood that habit and **Disposition** are taken here as the same type of quality; when it is lightly attached, it is called a disposition, but when firmly attached, it is called a habit. Therefore, just as the same person can grow from a boy into a man without changing their species <note>In this context, "species" refers to the essential nature or category of a being</note>; so too can the exact same quality change from a disposition into a habit.

There are, moreover, various kinds of habits: some belong to the Soul, others to the body. Those in the soul are either divinely infused—such as sanctifying grace <note>original: "gratia gratum faciens," a theological term for grace that makes a soul pleasing to God</note>, the Theological virtues <note>Faith, Hope, and Charity</note>, the gifts of the Holy Spirit, and freely given graces—or they are acquired, such as prudence, knowledge, art, error, opinion, virtues, and vices.

Those in the body either affect it well or poorly, and this occurs either in the whole or in part. Health, strength, etc., affect the body well as a whole; whereas infirmity and weakness affect it poorly. In part, however, the speed of the feet affects it well; while the indisposition, sluggishness, and specific diseases of each individual limb affect it poorly.

The second type <note>of quality</note> is natural power and frailty, or inability. Natural power is that by which any thing acts strongly or resists. Frailty here is not a total lack <note>original: "privatio," a philosophical term for the absence of a quality that should be present</note>, but rather a weak power.

This second type differs from the first type in two ways: first, because habit and disposition are acquired qualities, whereas natural faculty is born within us by nature. Second, because natural faculty grants the simple power of acting, such that if it were removed...

# ACADEMIC CABINET OF ARTS. THIRD CLASS.
E

...is removed, no power of acting remains; whereas a habit provides the power of acting more easily, so that even if the habit is removed, the thing can still be performed, though not with such great ease.

Of the <term>faculties</term><note>original: "facultas"; refers to the inherent power or capability of a thing to act</note>, some are proper to inanimate things, which vary according to the different conditions of each nature; others belong to animate things, of which some are general and some are specific. The general ones are of three kinds: some follow the level of the **vegetative soul**, others the level of the **sensitive soul**, and others the level of the **rational soul**. Hence, the faculty of the soul in general is threefold: vegetative, sensitive, and rational. <note>This reflects the Aristotelian hierarchy of life: plants (vegetative), animals (sensitive), and humans (rational).</note>

The specific faculties, however, are different: some are proper to certain plants, such as the wound-healing power of the dittany plant; others are proper to certain beasts, such as roaring in lions or neighing in horses. Finally, others belong to humans, such as the ability to feel wonder, to speak, and so on.

The third species is **affective quality** and **passion** <note>original: "patibilis qualitas & paffio"; these refer to qualities that act upon the senses or result from an emotional/physical state</note>, which are so named not because they all arise from some suffering, but because they all produce a certain impression in the senses by themselves. I said *by themselves* to exclude form and figure, which are perceived by the senses by means of light, color, and quantity, but not by themselves.

Moreover, an affective quality differs from a passion in that an affective quality lasts for a longer time, such as paleness contracted from a long-lasting illness, or gray hair resulting from extreme fear; but a passion lasts for a shorter time, such as paleness from a sudden fright, or a blush from shame.

The fourth species is **form** and **figure**, which arise—

# Art. Kab. Acad. II. Classis.
<note>Academic Cabinet of Arts, Second Class</note>

It is derived from the site and position of quantitative parts, so that it is in reality the same as quantity, although it is formally distinguished from it. Moreover, although this same exterior composition can be called "form" and "figure" indiscriminately, Philosophers nevertheless more willingly call exterior compositions in natural and physical things **forms**, but in mathematical and intelligible things, they call them **figures**.

The general properties of this category <note>original: "prædicamenti"; referring to the Aristotelian Category of Quality</note> are three:

**I.** To have a contrary; although this does not apply to all things, it applies only to qualities. This is because that mutual struggle which is necessary for the nature of contrariety is properly found only among qualities, and by the benefit of those qualities, that same struggle is found between certain substances and their actions.

**II.** To undergo "more" and "less," or to be intensified and diminished. This does not apply to all qualities—for example, it does not apply to natural faculties, forms, and figures—but it does apply to most, such as habits <note>term: "habitus" - acquired dispositions or tendencies</note> and "passible" qualities <note>term: "patibilibus qualitatibus" - qualities that involve a capacity for feeling or undergoing change, like heat or sweetness</note>.

**III.** It is from quality that things are said to be similar or dissimilar.

### Lecture V.

### On Relation.

1. *What it is.* 2. *The distinction between relation properly so-called and improperly so-called.* 3. *The properties of Related Things.*

<margin>1</margin>
Relation is the principle by virtue of which any being is formally referred to another, not as if to a subject, object, or end, but as if to a <term>terminus<note>The "terminus" is the endpoint or the "other side" of a relationship; for example, if a father is the subject, the son is the terminus.</note></term>.

<margin>2</margin>
And because relation is of two types:
<note>The original text uses a bracketed diagram to divide these concepts.</note>
Namely: { Categorical, or properly so-called, or "according to being" <note>original: "secundum esse"</note>,
And:
Improperly so-called, or "according to speech" <note>original: "secundum dici"</note>:
so that the true relation may be distinguished from the improper one, you will observe that in a relation properly so-called, three things must be considered: the **subject** in which the relation exists; the **foundation** <note>The "foundation" is the reason or cause for the relationship, such as the act of birth.</note> because of which such a relation exists in the subject; and the **terminus** toward which the relation looks. For example, fatherhood is a categorical relation, or the formal reason through which a father is referred to a son; the **subject** of this relation is the man who fathered; the **foundation** is both the faculty by which he was able to procreate and the past act of procreation; the **terminus**, however, is the son who was fathered.

Furthermore, since categorical relations are mutual, so that an opposite relation corresponds to a relation, it follows that the subject of the relation is called the **related** or **relative** thing, while the terminus—

<summary>This page defines the metaphysical concept of Relation, distinguishing between true categorical relations and those that exist only in speech, while outlining the three essential components: subject, foundation, and terminus.</summary>
<keywords>Relation, Categorical Relation, Fatherhood, Metaphysics, Subject, Foundation, Terminus, Logic</keywords>

--- Page 96 ---
<meta>This page continues the scholastic discussion of Aristotelian categories, specifically focusing on the nature of "Relation" (Ad aliquid) and its theological implications.</meta>

# ACADEMIC ARTS CABINET: SECOND CLASS

be called the correlative or the correlate; indeed, both are called correlates, or correlatives, and also "extremes," because opposite relations exist between them. For example: between a father and a son, there is the relation of fatherhood on one side, and the opposite relation of sonship on the other.

Because the extremes of Categorical relations <note>Categorical relations are those that exist in reality, independent of the mind, as defined in Aristotle's Categories.</note> and the foundations upon which they rest are real and actually existing, and are really distinct from one another, it follows that relations of this kind are positive and real; for they belong to things positively and exist in them before any operation of the intellect. Therefore, if a relation is given whose extremes or foundations are not real, or are not really distinct from one another, or are not actually existing, it must be denied that such a relation is real, and thus it is not categorical. Such is the relation of a thing known to the intellect, of a thing seen to the eye, and the relation of God to His creatures. I say the relation of God to created things because if such a relation were real, it would follow that something real could both be and not be in God. Since relations depend for their existence not only on the subject and the foundation, but also on the "term" <note>The 'term' is the endpoint or the object to which the subject is related.</note>, it follows that if the term is removed, the relation perishes. Therefore, since created things can cease to exist, it would follow that those relations to creatures could cease to exist, and thus there would be something real and contingent <note>Contingent: something that can change or cease to be, as opposed to necessary.</note> in God, which is repugnant to His immutability.

Five properties of relatives are handed down.
<margin>3</margin> The first is: that contrariety is found in relatives, such as "similar" and "dissimilar," which are called contraries.

<summary>This page defines the nature of "real" versus "logical" relations, arguing that while human relationships like fatherhood are real, God's relationship to creation must be logical to preserve His unchanging nature.</summary>
<keywords>logic, Aristotelian categories, relations, fatherhood, sonship, divine immutability, scholasticism, theology</keywords>

--- Page 97 ---
<meta>This page continues the scholastic discussion of Categories, specifically focusing on the properties of Relations (Relata), following the definitions established in previous sections.</meta>

# Art. Kab. Acad. II. Classis.

...because their boundaries—namely, whiteness and blackness—are opposed as contraries. But if by "contrariety" you understand opposition in general, then mutually related things especially will be called contraries, that is, opposites: in this way, a father and a son are relatively opposed to one another.

II. Related things admit of "more" and "less," or can be intensified and diminished: for in this way, two similar things can become more similar or less similar. However, this property does not apply to all related things, but only to some; even so, some change is always required, either on the part of the <term>basis<note>original: "fundamentum"; the underlying quality or fact that gives rise to a relation, such as the specific shade of white in two objects.</note></term> or on the part of the <term>boundary<note>original: "terminus"; the endpoint or the second object to which the first is being compared.</note></term>. It is not always necessary for the basis or boundary to be intensified for the relation to be intensified, or diminished for it to be diminished; indeed, the opposite often happens. For example, the basis or boundary might be diminished while the relation is intensified: as when there are two white things, one of which is whiter than the other; if the whiter one is diminished <insert>in its whiteness</insert>, then their mutual similarity becomes greater. Conversely, a relation can be diminished when one of the extremes is intensified: as if, of two equally white things, one becomes whiter, then their mutual dissimilarity will be greater than before.

III. Related things are reciprocal: this is to be understood regarding <term>correlatives<note>original: "correlatiuis"; terms that exist only in reference to each other.</note></term>; thus a father is called the father of a son, and a son the son of a father.

IV. Related things exist simultaneously by nature: this is to be understood regarding correlatives insofar as they are formally related. For if they are considered as absolute entities, one can be prior to the other by nature, or even by time; for example, a father and a son.

<column-break/>

<note>Vocabulary: related things (relata), opposition (oppositio), basis (fundamentum), boundary (terminus), similarity (similitudo), dissimilarity (dissimilitudo), reciprocal (reciproca), correlatives (correlatiua), simultaneous by nature (simul natura)</note>

->E iij<-

Art Cabinet of the Academy, Class III.

according to the formal reasoning of fatherhood and sonship, they exist simultaneously by nature; for no one can be called a father without a son, nor the reverse. If, however, they are considered insofar as they are men, then the father precedes the son both by nature and by time.

V. When one of two related things is known, the other is also known: the reason is that one relative is usually defined and explained by the other. Thus, no one can know what a father is without a son, nor what a son is without a father; for a father is the father of a son, and a son is the son of a father.

### Lecture VI.

->On Action and Passion.<-

1. *What action and passion are.* 2. *Their properties.* 3. *Various divisions and species of actions and passions.*

Although Action and Passion constitute different <term>categories</term> <note>original: "praedicamenta"; the ten fundamental classes of things defined by Aristotle</note>, because they are perpetually connected to one another and one cannot easily be understood without the other, both must be discussed together.

<margin>1</margin> Action, therefore, is the principle by reason of which the <term>agent</term> <note>the one performing the act</note> acts upon the <term>patient</term> <note>the one receiving the act</note>: just as heating is the principle by reason of which fire acts upon wood.

Passion <note>In this context, "passion" refers to the state of being acted upon or receiving an external influence, rather than an emotion.</note>, however, is the principle by reason of which the patient suffers under the agent, such as that same heating insofar as it is received in the wood.

<margin>2</margin> There are two properties for each.

I. To admit of "more" and "less": for according to the greater or lesser power of the agent, the action is more intense or more relaxed

<meta>catchword: [inten]sior</meta>

<summary>This page concludes a lesson on the logic of relationships (fatherhood/sonship) and introduces the categories of Action and Passion, explaining how they are interconnected through the example of fire heating wood.</summary>
<keywords>logic, Aristotle, categories, action, passion, agent, patient, relations, scholasticism</keywords>

--- Page 99 ---
<meta>This page continues the academic classification of actions and passions, likely following a discussion on the definitions of cause and effect in a scholastic or Aristotelian framework.</meta>

# Art Cabinet of the Academy: Third Class
->E iiij<-

the action is [stronger]; likewise, according to the lesser or greater resistance of the recipient <note>original: "patientis," referring to the object being acted upon</note>, the action is more intense or more relaxed.

II. There is a contrariety in action and passion; for in this way, heating is the contrary of cooling <note>original: "calefactio est contraria frigefactioni"</note>.

<margin>3</margin> There are various divisions of actions and passions. For action is divided especially either by *reason of duration*, or by *reason of the subject in which it is received*, or by *reason of the principle from which it arises*.

By reason of *duration*, Action is divided:
| | |
| :--- | :--- |
| Into | **Instantaneous**, such as illumination, |
| & | **Successive**, such as heating. |

By reason of the *subject*, it is divided:
| | |
| :--- | :--- |
| Into | **Immanent**, which remains in the agent itself, such as understanding, willing, nourishing, growing, etc. |
| & | **Transient**, which passes from the agent into another subject, such as heating, which passes from the fire into the wood that is being heated. |

By reason of the *principle*, it is divided:
| | |
| :--- | :--- |
| Into | **Artificial**, the principle of which is art <note>or "skill/craft"</note>, such as building; |
| & | **Natural**, the principle of which is nature. |

<note>Technical vocabulary used: action (actio), passion (passio), heating (calefactio), cooling (frigefactioni), reason of duration (ratio durationis), reason of the subject (ratio subiecti), reason of the principle (ratio principii), immanent (immanens), transient (transiens), artificial (artificialis), natural (naturalis).</note>

### LECTURE VII.

->Concerning Habit.<-

->*1. What it is. 2. It is also called "to have."*<-

<margin>1</margin> Habit <note>Latin: *Habitus*; here referring to the category of "State" or "Condition" rather than a repetitive behavior</note> is not understood here as it was in the first type of Quality, but as the relation of some external thing adjacent to another thing by way of clothing or ornament; whether it regards the whole, such as the relation of a tunic to the one who is clothed in it, or the parts, such as the relation of a ring to the finger.

<margin>2</margin> This Category is also called "to have" <note>Latin: *habere*</note>, which is a concrete term. Its material meaning is that which possesses something, while its formal meaning is the thing possessed by way of clothing or ornament.

---

### LECTURE VIII.

->Concerning Position.<-

->*1. What it is. 2. The difference between Position and "Where." 3. Its meaning. 4. The manifold nature of Position.*<-

<margin>1</margin> Position or posture <note>Latin: *Situs*</note> is the order of the parts of some whole in relation to the whole itself and to its location. In this, <margin>2</margin> Position differs from "Where" <note>Latin: *Ubi*; the category of location</note>, because "Where" signifies only the relation to a place, whereas Position denotes another relation besides this one. To be "positioned," moreover, has the character of a concrete term, whose material <margin>3</margin> meaning is the thing positioned, while the formal meaning is the position itself, insofar as it is understood as a certain arrangement of the parts of a thing.

<meta>catchword: in</meta>

<summary>This page defines the final Aristotelian categories of Habit (external coverings/ornaments) and Position (the internal arrangement of parts within a space).</summary>
<keywords>Aristotelian categories, Habit, State, Position, Posture, Ubi, Location, Scholastic philosophy, Logic</keywords>

--- Page 101 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Aristotelian categories, specifically focusing on Position (Situs) and Time (When/Quando).</meta>

->Art Cabinet of the Academy, Class III.<-

located in relation to itself and to the parts of its location.

<margin>4</margin>From the first arrangement of a thing's parts—namely, in relation to the thing itself, or from the order of the parts of any whole relative to that whole—various external forms or figures arise. Hence, the arrangement of limbs in beasts is one way, and in man another. Furthermore, there is one arrangement of limbs in a man lying down, another in the same man sitting, and another when he is upright; these arrangements do not seem to be distinguished, according to the opinion of some, from the form or figure which is the fourth species of quality <note>In Aristotelian logic, "Quality" is divided into four types; the author is debating whether "Position" is merely a subset of "Shape/Form".</note>.

From the other arrangement of parts—namely, of the thing in relation to its location—various positions also arise. For a position is either natural, such as a man standing with his head upright or beasts having a prone body; or it is accidental. This occurs either in animate things, such as standing, lying, kneeling, leaning forward or backward, sitting, etc.; or in inanimate things, especially in artificial works, such as a house which is said to be well or poorly situated.

### Lecture IX.

## Concerning Time, or "When."

*1. What it is. 2. The meaning of the word "When." 3. To be "in time" is said in two ways.*

<margin>1</margin>"When" <note>original: "Quando"</note> is the relation of time to a temporary thing, such as the relation of this hour to a thing existing at such a time, whose operation or motion it measures.

<margin>2</margin>Moreover, the word "When" is a concrete term whose material meaning is the thing that coexists with time; its formal meaning, however, is time insofar as...

<column-break/>

<term>Position (Situs): The category describing the arrangement of parts.</term>
<term>Form/Figure: The outward appearance or shape of a thing.</term>
<term>When (Quando): The category of time as it relates to an object's existence.</term>

# ACADEMIC CABINET OF ARTS. THIRD CLASS.

is the external measure of some duration.

It is said: *Insofar as it is an external measure*: for internal time, which is the internal measure of any motion joined to it, belongs to the Category of quantity <note>The author distinguishes between 'internal time' (the duration of an object's own movement) and 'external time' (the clock or calendar we use to measure it).</note>: for example, the continuous and successive duration of celestial motion, insofar as it is the internal measure of that same motion, is a species of successive quantity: but truly, insofar as it is the measure of the duration of sublunary things <note>original: "rerum sublunarium" — referring to everything on Earth, beneath the moon's orbit</note>, or insofar as we use it, or its parts such as hours, days, months, years, etc., to measure the durations of these earthly things, it belongs to this predicament <note>term: predicament — another word for a Category or classification of being</note>.

<margin>3</margin>
Furthermore, to be "in time" can be spoken of generally, such as being in the past, present, or future time: or specifically, such as being in an age, a year, a month, a day, or an hour.

<margin>1</margin>
Place, or "Where," is the principle by reason of which one substance contains another within itself. Moreover, "Where" is manifold.

# ACADEMIC ARTS CABINET: THIRD CLASS

->Namely<-
{ **Replenishing** <note>original: "Repletiuum"</note>, as God is in the world and the soul is in the body.
**Definitive**, namely according to operation even in the absence of the thing, as the Sun is on the earth; in this manner Angels are said to be in a place.
**Occupying**, as a body occupying space within another body.
**Containing**, that which contains within itself.

However, this Category <note>The "Predicament" or category of "Where"</note> properly belongs only to corporeal things which are surrounded by another body; hence Angels and souls are not properly said to be "in a place." Indeed, not even the **Empyrean Heaven** <note>The highest heaven in medieval cosmology, believed to be the abode of God and the angels</note> can be properly said to be anywhere, because it is surrounded by no other body; nor can it be said to be in a real and positive place, but only in an imaginary place.

Finally, to be in a place can be spoken of either **absolutely** or **relatively**. Absolutely, one can be said to be in a common place, such as being in Paris, being in France, etc., or in one's own proper place. To be in a place relatively, however, is spoken of:

->In these six ways<-

Behind
To the right
To the left.

# Art Cabinet of the Academy, Class III.

### LECTURE XII.

### On the Virtues.

*1. What Faith is. 2. What Hope is. 3. What Charity is and its sign. 4. What Patience is. 5. What Piety is. 6. On the Cardinal Virtues in general. 7. What Prudence is. 8. Justice and its integral parts. 9. Fortitude. 10. Temperance.*

<margin>1</margin>
Faith is a gift of God and a light by which a person, being enlightened, firmly adheres to those things which have been divinely revealed to be believed, and proposed to us by the Church: such as that God is Three and One <note>The doctrine of the Trinity</note>; that the world was created out of nothing; that God became man and endured death for us; that the Blessed Mary exists as both Virgin and Mother of God; that all the dead are to be raised to life; that man is reborn from water and the Holy Spirit; that the whole Christ is contained in the Eucharist <term>Eucharist: the sacrament of Holy Communion</term>, and other venerable mysteries of our religion of that kind.

<margin>2</margin>
Hope is a divinely infused virtue through which we await the goods of eternal happiness with certain confidence: or according to the Master of the Sentences <note>original: "Magistrum sententiarum"; referring to Peter Lombard, a medieval theologian whose textbook was the standard for centuries</note>, Hope is the certain expectation of eternal salvation, proceeding from grace and merits: for to hope for something without merits cannot be called hope, but rather presumption.

Charity is a divinely infused virtue by which God is sincerely loved for His own sake, and one's neighbor is loved for the sake of God, according to the two pre-

...thus proposed in the Old and New Law: *You shall love the Lord your God with all your heart, and with all your soul, and with all your strength: this is the first and greatest commandment. Indeed, the evidence of sincere charity, or the proof of love, is the performance of works and the observation of the divine commandments, according to 1 John 5: *This is the true love of God, that we keep his commandments, and his commandments are not burdensome.* These are the three virtues called <term>Theological</term> <note>The "Theological Virtues" are Faith, Hope, and Charity (Love), so named because they have God as their immediate object.</note>, because all Theology is concerned with them.

4. **Patience** is the habit by which the sufferer conquers and is not conquered; or, according to Cicero in the second book of *On Invention*: *Patience is the voluntary and long-continued endurance of arduous and difficult things for the sake of honor or utility.* <note>original: "Patientia est honestatis..." Marcus Tullius Cicero was a Roman statesman whose definitions were frequently used by Christian scholars to bridge classical philosophy and theology.</note>

5. **Piety** is the habit by which the pious person feels compassion for the weaknesses of their neighbor; or, according to Cicero in the second book of *On Invention*: *Piety is that through which duty and diligent service are rendered to those joined by blood and to those devoted to the fatherland.* Theologians define it thus: *Piety is a gift of the Holy Spirit, by which we are imbued with an ardent affection for rightly and sincerely worshiping God, and for doing good to our neighbor—even to one who has treated us poorly—for the sake of God.*

There are, moreover, four: namely **Prudence**, which pertains to reason; **Justice**, which pertains to the will...

# ACADEMIC CABINET OF THE ARTS: THIRD CLASS

...will: *Temperance* which pertains to the concupiscible appetite <note>The "concupiscible appetite" refers to the soul's natural inclination toward things that seem good or pleasurable.</note>; and *Fortitude* which pertains to the irascible appetite <note>The "irascible appetite" refers to the soul's drive to overcome obstacles or resist perceived evils.</note>. Indeed, this division of virtue is a division of a genus into four principal species, to which all others are referred, either as inferior species or as potential or integral parts of the same.

Prudence holds the first place among them, both because it resides in the intellect—which is nobler than the appetite—and because it governs the others. Hence Saint Gregory <note>original: "D. Gregor." referring to Pope Gregory the Great</note> teaches in Book 2 of his *Homilies on Ezekiel* (Homily 22) that the other virtues can by no means be virtues unless they act prudently. Plato also referred all virtues back to Prudence. This virtue is defined by Aristotle in Book 6 of the *Ethics*, Chapter 5, as: *A habit of acting with true reason concerning those things which are good or evil for man.* I say "good or evil" in particular instances, so that it may be distinguished from Moral Philosophy, which is concerned with good and evil in a general sense.

Justice is defined by Lull <note>original: "Lullio," referring to Ramon Llull, the 13th-century philosopher and mystic</note> in his *Short Art* as: *The habit by which a just person acts justly.* By others it is defined thus: *Justice is the virtue which renders to each his own right, that is, what is just and upright.* Ulpian <note>A famous Roman jurist whose definitions formed the basis of Western law</note> in the *Digest* (on Justice and Law) explains the nature of Justice in more abundant words: *Justice,* he says, *is the constant and perpetual will to render to each person his due right.*

The integral parts of Justice are two, which fulfill its duty: *To turn away from evil and to do good*, according to that verse in the Psalms: *Turn away from evil and do good.* In this context, "good" is the same as what is owed to another, while "evil" is the same as what is harmful to another.

Fortitude is defined by Aristotle in Book 2 of the *Ethics*, Chapter 7...

### LECTURE XIII.
#### Concerning Vices.

*1. The cause of vices. 2. The division of the same. 3. What is Pride. 4. Greed. 5. Lust. 6. Envy. 7. Gluttony. 8. Wrath. 9. Sloth. 10. The virtues opposed to the aforementioned vices.*

The study of opposites is one and the same; therefore, the logic of teaching requires that since we have spoken with Lull <note>Ramon Lull (c. 1232–1315), a Majorcan philosopher and theologian known for his "Art" of logical categorization</note> concerning the virtues, we should also discuss the vices.

<margin>1</margin> The cause of vices is not an efficient cause, but rather a "deficient" cause, so to speak; it is the free will of man. Because the will is finite and created—and therefore originating from nothing—it participates in nothingness, and for that reason, it is capable of falling.

Although there is a manifold division of vices, they are commonly divided into the seven capital sins, as proposed in the second figure of the eleventh lecture of this Class. They are called "Capital" because they are the sources and heads <note>Latin: *capita*; the "head" or origin from which others flow</note> of all sins, to which Lull adds inconstancy and lying. Concerning these nine special—

<keywords>Aristotle, Ramon Lull, Ethics, Fortitude, Temperance, Seven Capital Sins, Free Will, Vice, Moral Philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 111 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of moral instruments within the framework of Raymond Lull's philosophy, specifically categorizing the seven deadly sins.</meta>

# Art Cabinet of the Academy, Second Class

<note>Signature: F ij</note>

It is our intention to act briefly, since Raymond <note>referring to Raymond Lull, the 13th-century philosopher and mystic</note> considers these things as moral instruments under the "Instrumental" category, as we have stated previously.

<margin>3</margin>
Therefore, **Pride** is an inordinate desire for excellence, whether it lies hidden within the soul or reveals itself outwardly; this is the mother, leader, and queen of all vices.

<margin>4</margin>
**Greed** is an excessive and inordinate desire for possessing; or as Tully <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero, the Roman orator</note> says, an immoderate love of having.

<margin>5</margin>
**Lust** is an inordinate desire for sexual matters.

<margin>6</margin>
**Envy** is sadness or grief over the good of another and a hatred of another's happiness: in respect to superiors, because one is not equal to them; in respect to inferiors, lest they become equal to oneself; and in respect to equals, because they are equal to oneself, as Augustine says. Furthermore, Envy <note>Latin: Invidia</note> is so named from "not seeing" <note>Latin: non videndo</note>, because it cannot bear to look upon the good fortune of others.

<margin>7</margin>
**Gluttony** is an inordinate appetite for food and drink, or an immoderate greediness for food; or even a voracious devouring that is not content with the limits of nature.

<margin>8</margin>
**Wrath** is an immoderate desire for punishing him by whom one considers oneself to have been harmed. It is so named from the verb "to go" <note>Latin: ire</note>. For he who is angry "goes away" from himself and rages; hence, he who lays aside his anger is said to "return to himself."

<margin>9</margin>
**Sloth** <note>Latin: Acedia</note> is an immoderate sluggishness of a relaxed mind, and an excessive laziness toward performing good works. It is called *Acedia* from the Greek <term>akēdia</term> <note>original: ἀκηδία</note>, that is, sadness, laziness, or weariness, because through it the soul feels weariness toward every difficult spiritual task.
