# THE FOURTH CLASS.

### Concerning absolute predicates.

-> LECTURE I. <-

*1. The definition of absolute predicates. 2. Their number. 3. In how many ways they may be predicated. 4. That every created principle exists with another innate accidental quality. 5. Likewise, that everything is full of itself and of the parts it assumes from other principles, and in what manner. 6. That these predicates also become subjects. 7. The Figure. 8. Why it is circular. 9. Finding the middle term. 10. The Ladder of the Intellect <note>A method for ascending from sensory knowledge to divine understanding</note>. 11. That all things are reduced to these principles. 12. That the terms of this figure are considered in two ways. 13. The division of Circle A. 14. The terms of propositions. 15. What kind of predicates exist in individual subjects.*

Thus far we have dealt with Figure S, or universal subjects; now we must treat Figure A, or absolute predicates. I say "absolute" because there are other relative or relational predicates marked by the letter T, as will become clear in the fifth class.

<margin>1</margin> Here, absolute predicates are understood as certain general terms that can be predicated of every thing, either by attributing them simply or by showing that a certain predicate exists within a subject.

<summary>This page introduces the "Fourth Class" of Lullian logic, focusing on absolute predicates (Figure A) and outlining fifteen points of study regarding their nature, structure, and application in the "Ladder of the Intellect."</summary>

<keywords>absolute predicates, Figure A, Figure S, Figure T, Ladder of the Intellect, universal subjects, logic, principles of creation</keywords>

--- Page 114 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Art of Ramon Llull, specifically focusing on the nature of the "General Principles" or "Absolute Predicates" and their grammatical structure.</meta>

# ACADEMIC CABINET OF THE ARTS, SECOND CLASS

...thrown in because of the suitability that will exist with these terms.

<margin>2</margin> It should be noted, however, that although a nearly infinite multitude of predicates can be found in this Art, Ramon <note>original: "Raymundus," referring to Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1316), the Majorcan philosopher and mystic who created this system.</note> nevertheless reduced them to a certain number and order, which he called "absolute predicates" or "general principles." He intends for these to be predicated of a thing not only accidentally, but also substantially and causally—just as a thing can be called "good" or "great" not only as a passing quality, but in its very substance. From this, one must understand that every created general and substantial principle exists with another innate accidental principle of its own kind: such as "accidental goodness," which is an attribute of "substantial goodness" and the instrument with which it makes other things good. Thus, every principle is full of itself insofar as it is substantial, having within itself essential and concrete parts of its own essence, species, and nature.

<margin>3</margin>

<margin>4</margin>

<margin>5</margin>

->Namely: $\begin{cases} \text{-TIVE,} \\ \text{-ABLE,} \\ \text{-TO [verb].} \end{cases}$<-

->As when one says, for example:<-
->Good $\begin{cases} \text{Bonificative (that which makes good)} \\ \text{Bonificable (that which can be made good)} \\ \text{To Bonify (the act of making good).} \end{cases}$<-

Where <term>-TIVE</term> <note>original: "IVVM"</note> signifies the active principle, whether effective or communicative; <term>-ABLE</term> <note>original: "ABILE"</note> signifies the passive principle, whether receptive or participative; and <term>TO [verb]</term> <note>original: "ARE," the Latin infinitive ending</note> signifies the copulative or connective principle, or the actual operation itself.

<summary>This page explains Ramon Llull's theory of "Absolute Predicates," arguing that qualities like goodness are both substantial essences and active forces. It introduces the linguistic "triads" (active, passive, and verbal) used to describe how these principles function in the world.</summary>
<keywords>Ramon Llull, Absolute Predicates, General Principles, Bonification, Scholasticism, Active and Passive Principles, Lullian Art</keywords>

# THE ACADEMIC KABBALISTIC ART: THIRD CLASS

Furthermore, it is complete in all parts which are similarities derived from other principles, provided that the principle itself acts as the subject. Those similarities, however, are accidental <note>In Aristotelian logic, an "accidental" property is one that a thing may or may not have without losing its identity, as opposed to "substantial" properties.</note> which proceed from the substantial parts. It is said "when that principle is the subject" because these principles are not only predicated <note>original: "praedicantur"; to be stated or asserted about a subject</note> of one another, but are also subjected to one another, whether in the concrete or in the abstract.

> For example, if we say:
> { Good greatness. or
> { Great goodness.
> Or,
> { A great good.

# ACADEMIC ART OF THE CABINET: FOURTH CLASS

when it is said { Goodness is great & Greatness is good.

<margin>9</margin> In this, the Lullist <note>A follower of Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1316), a philosopher who created a system of logic using rotating circles to combine concepts.</note> investigates the natural connection between the predicate and the subject, so that he may find a "middle term" to reach a conclusion. For any principle taken by itself is entirely general, such as when it is said *Goodness is greatness*. But if one principle is narrowed down by another, then it becomes "subalternate" <note>A logical term for a category that sits between the most general and the most specific.</note>, such as when it is said *Great Goodness*: if, however, it is narrowed down to an individual, then it is a most specific principle, such as when it is said *The goodness of Peter is great*. And in this way, the Intellect has a ladder for ascending and descending from an entirely general principle to one that is neither entirely general nor entirely specific; and from a principle neither entirely general nor specific to one that is entirely specific.

<margin>10</margin> In the principles of this figure <note>Referring to the circular diagrams used in Lullian logic.</note> is implied whatever exists. For whatever exists is either good, or great, etc. Therefore, all things are reduced to the principles mentioned above.

<margin>11</margin> Furthermore, the terms of this circle can be considered in two ways. First, as they exist in the First Cause or First Subject—signified by the letter B in Figure S <note>In Llull's system, 'B' usually represents Goodness and the first position in his symbolic alphabet.</note>—in which all terms are equally convertible. For in God, one attribute can never exceed another: He is as good as He is eternal; indeed, all these terms, whether signifying concretely or abstractly, are the same thing in God. Secondly, they are considered as they exist in other subjects <note>i.e., created beings like humans or animals.</note> in which one attribute can exceed another, as is clearly evident.

<summary>This page explains the Lullist method of logical "descent" from universal divine principles to specific individuals and clarifies that while God's attributes are all equal and identical, they vary in degree when found in created things.</summary>
<keywords>Ramon Llull, Lullism, logic, divine attributes, Goodness, Greatness, First Cause, ladder of intellect, Figure S</keywords>

# Art Cabinet of the Academy: Fourth Class

<margin>13</margin> Finally, this Circle A <note>Circle A represents the divine attributes or "Dignities" in the system of Ramon Llull</note> is divided both generally and specifically. Generally, like all the others, it contains nine terms, which are indicated by the letters B, C, and so on. Specifically, however, it is divided into a circumference and a plane. Within the plane is a triangle in which the more general terms reside.

->Namely: { Essence, Unity, Perfection.<-

<margin>14</margin> On the circumference are the absolute predicates: *Goodness, Greatness*, etc. From these terms, when combined in various ways with the Subjects of Figure S <note>Figure S typically represents the soul or the thinking subject in this mnemonic system</note>, propositions are formed.

Here, therefore, we are taught to embrace the terms of Subjects and predicates; that is, to remember skillfully and promptly which predicates can be affirmed of any given Subject. Here you will note <margin>15</margin> that such predicates are only what the subjects permit them to be, and the words must be understood according to the subject matter at hand. Therefore, "Goodness" is predicated of God in one way, of an Angel in another, and of a human being in yet another.

And let these things said of the circle in general suffice; now let us proceed to the explanation of the individual terms.

# ACADEMIC CABINET OF ARTS. CLASS IV.

### LECTURE II.
### On Goodness.

*1. What the good is. 2. The division of the good in general. 3. The division of the good by participation. 4. What evil is. 5. How technical terms of the art are to be understood. 6. Related and opposing terms.*

<margin>1</margin>
If Goodness is viewed according to its formal nature, it must be defined *a priori* <note>from the cause or theoretical first principles</note> as: *That which is suitable to each thing*. If, however, it is weighed according to the properties that follow from it—one of which is "desirability"—it is correctly described *a posteriori* <note>from observed effects or experience</note> as: *That which is desirable by all: or that which all things seek*. It could also be described differently by reason of other properties, as when it is said: *That which is by its own nature diffusive* <note>original: "natura sui diffusiuum"; a philosophical principle that goodness naturally spreads or shares itself</note>.

<margin>2</margin>
Goodness in general can be divided
Into { Good from itself & Good from another.

The former is called Good by essence, which is God alone according to the Lord’s decree in Luke 18: *No one is good except God alone* <note>original Latin: "Nemo bonus nisi solus Deus"</note>. The latter, however, is called good by participation <note>meaning it possesses goodness only by sharing in the goodness of a higher source</note>.

<margin>3</margin>
In turn, good by participation can be divided
Into { Transcendent or metaphysical Good, Natural or physical Good, Moral or human Good.

<note>Technical Vocabulary: Goodness, desirability, Good by essence, Good by participation, Metaphysical, Physical, Moral</note>

<summary>This lecture defines the nature of "The Good" using Scholastic philosophy, distinguishing between essential goodness (God) and goodness that things acquire by participating in a higher source. It categorizes goodness into metaphysical, physical, and moral realms.</summary>
<keywords>Goodness, Metaphysics, Ethics, Scholasticism, Participation, Divine Essence, Desirability</keywords>

--- Page 120 ---
<meta>This page continues the academic classification of philosophical concepts, specifically focusing on the definitions of Good and Evil within the framework of the "Art of the Academy."</meta>

-># ACADEMIC ART OF THE CABINET: FOURTH CLASS<-

Transcendent Good is that which belongs to everything insofar as it exists, or is capable of existing. <note>In medieval philosophy, "transcendental" refers to properties like goodness or truth that apply to all things regardless of their specific category.</note>

Natural or physical good is that which belongs to the nature of each thing, such as the natural faculties and powers inherent to it.

Finally, Moral or human good is that which can be sought by a human being spontaneously and freely. This is divided, by reason of its subject or matter, into the Good of the soul (such as knowledge), the Good of the body (such as health), and the Good of fortune (such as wealth). By reason of its end or purpose, it is divided into the Honorable, the Useful, and the Pleasant.

<margin>4</margin> Since the study of opposites is one and the same, as we have said elsewhere, we must also inquire into the nature of Evil. I say, therefore, that it is the privation <note>original: "privatio"; the absence of a quality that should normally be present</note> of some specific good—specific, I say, not transcendental—because being, as being, cannot be evil; rather, evil exists in some specific or particular aspect of being. Thus, a natural being is called "evil" (or defective) if it lacks something required for its integrity. A work of art is called "evil" (or poor) if it deviates somewhat from the rules of the craft. A moral evil occurs if one wanders from the dictates of right reason.

<margin>5</margin> From this, you may gather that the terms of the art are not to be taken simply as they sound, but must include related and opposing terms together, which we shall bring forward at the end of each lecture for this and the following figure. Let them be as follows:

<term>Transcendent Good</term> <note>Goodness as a universal property of existence.</note>, <term>Natural Good</term>, <term>Physical Good</term>, <term>Moral Good</term>, <term>Good of the Soul</term>, <term>Good of the Body</term>, <term>Good of Fortune</term>, <term>Evil</term>, <term>Natural Being</term>, <term>Artificial Being</term> <note>A thing created by human skill or art.</note>.

<summary>This page defines the various categories of "The Good" (transcendental, natural, and moral) and defines "Evil" as the specific absence or privation of a required good.</summary>
<keywords>ethics, metaphysics, transcendental good, moral philosophy, privation of good, natural law, classification of knowledge</keywords>

# ACADEMIC ART CABINET: FOURTH CLASS

Transcendent good <note>original: "Bonum transcendens"; in scholastic philosophy, this refers to "goodness" as a property that applies to all existing things simply because they exist</note> is that which belongs to each thing insofar as it exists, or is able to exist.

Natural or physical good is that which belongs to the nature of each thing, such as the natural faculties and powers of any given object.

Finally, moral or human good is that which can be sought by a human being spontaneously and freely. This is divided, by reason of its subject or matter, into: Good of the soul, such as knowledge; Good of the body, such as health; and Good of fortune, such as wealth. By reason of its end <note>its purpose or goal</note>, it is divided into the honorable, the useful, and the pleasant.

<margin>4</margin> Since the study of opposites is the same <note>a logical principle: to understand one thing is to understand its opposite</note>, as we have said elsewhere, we must also inquire into what evil is. I say, therefore, that it is the privation <note>the lack or absence of a quality that should normally be present</note> of some specific good—specific, I say, not transcendental—because being, as being, cannot be evil; rather, evil exists in some specific or particular aspect of being. Thus, a natural being is called "evil" <note>or defective</note> if it lacks something required for its integrity. A work of art is called "evil" <note>poorly made</note> if it deviates somewhat from the rules of the craft. A moral evil occurs if one wanders from the dictates of right reason.

<margin>5</margin> From this, you may gather that the terms of this art are not to be taken simply as they sound, but must include related and opposing terms together, which we shall bring forward at the end of each lecture concerning this and the following figure. Let them be as follows:

<gloss>Transcendent Good, Natural, Physical, Moral, Good of the soul, Good of the body, Good of fortune, Evil, Being, Privation</gloss>

# ACADEMIC CABINET ART: FOURTH CLASS

### LECTURE III.

### On Magnitude.

> 1. What magnitude is. 2. Its various types. 3. The different degrees of majority and minority. 4. Things related to and contrary to majority.

1 Magnitude is that being by reason of which Goodness, Duration, and the other principles are considered "great," encompassing all the extremities of existence. Therefore, magnitude is nothing other than the extension of Substance, goodness, power, etc.; whether according to substance—as when a thing is great in itself—or according to motion, growth, or operation. <note>The author uses "Magnitude" here not just as physical size, but as a metaphysical "greatness" or "scale" that applies to abstract concepts like time and virtue.</note>

2 Moreover, magnitude is:
->Threefold: { Of Virtue <note>original: "Virtutis"</note> in spiritual matters;<-
->{ Of Bulk <note>original: "Molis"</note> or matter, insofar as they are considered in or around a body;<-
->{ Of Actions or operations.<-

3 From this it follows that the degrees of majority <note>being greater</note> and minority <note>being lesser</note> are taken diversely according to the specific magnitude in which they are grounded. Thus, also, by the intense or relaxed quality upon which a likeness is based, similar things are more similar or less similar to one another. <note>This refers to the "intensity" of a quality—for example, two things are "more similar" if the heat they share is of the same high intensity.</note>

<summary>This lecture defines magnitude as the metaphysical extension of a thing's substance or power and categorizes it into three types: spiritual virtue, physical bulk, and the scale of actions.</summary>
<keywords>magnitude, metaphysics, substance, virtue, physical bulk, majority and minority, degrees of intensity</keywords>

### LECTURE IV.

->On Duration.<-

*1. What duration is. 2. Its various types. 3. Related and opposing concepts.*

1 Duration is the being by virtue of which goodness, greatness, and the other principles and entities endure. It is, therefore, the permanence or the staying of the essence of goodness, greatness, etc., or of a thing within its own being. However, Duration varies according to the different conditions of existing things.

2 Generally, it is fourfold.
The first is Eternity, which is without beginning and without end, and signifies no succession or change; this belongs to God alone, because He is measured by no duration, since a measure is prior to the thing measured in either dignity or nature. <note>The author argues that God cannot be "measured" by time because the creator must be superior to the scale used to measure Him.</note>

The second is the "aeon" <note>original: "ævum"; a philosophical term for a duration that has a beginning but no end</note>, which, having a beginning but being without end, belongs only to the Angels and the rational soul.

The third is discrete or "angelic" time, which is the duration of several angelic operations succeeding one another. This time is not a single unit in itself, but exists through the gathering of many "instants" from which it is composed. An "instant" of discrete time is the duration proper to each individual internal angelic operation; this duration coexists with our time and has a "before" and "after" in its own way, and therefore it is less properly called an "instant" <note>In scholastic philosophy, an "instant" usually implies a point without extension, but here it refers to the duration of a single thought or action by a spiritual being.</note>.

<note>Vocabulary: duration, eternity, aeon (everlastingness), discrete time, the instant, God, Angels, rational soul</note>

<summary>This page defines duration as the permanence of a thing's essence and categorizes it into three distinct types: the eternal (God), the everlasting (Angels and souls), and discrete time (the sequence of angelic thoughts).</summary>
<keywords>metaphysics, duration, eternity, aeon, angelic time, discrete time, scholastic philosophy, nature of God</keywords>

# Art Cabinet of the Academy, Second Class.
G

The "instant" of our time, properly so called, is the specific duration of the indivisible moments of motion, which are termed <term>Changed-beings<note>original: "Mutata esse." In Scholastic philosophy, this refers to the state of a thing immediately after a change has occurred, viewed as an instantaneous point rather than a process.</note></term>; indeed, this instant can never coexist with any part of time.

The fourth type is successive time, which belongs to the successive and continuous operations of natural things, and is defined by Aristotle as: *The measure of motion according to before and after.*

# ACADEMIC ART CABINET. FOURTH CLASS.

### LECTURE V.

->Concerning Power.<-

*1. What power is. 2. Its threefold nature. 3. The breadth of power. 4. Concerning act and potency. 5. Related and Opposing concepts.*

<margin>1</margin> Power is that through which goodness, greatness, and the other principles and remaining beings are able to be, to exist, and to subsist. <note>The author uses "Power" (Latin: *potestas*) here as the metaphysical "engine" that allows any quality or thing to actually function or be real.</note>

<margin>2</margin> Not without reason do we assign a threefold nature to "being": because those things which possess only essence, and to which a simple "being" belongs, exist through power no less than those things which exist or subsist.

That, however, is said to "exist" which has its being outside of its cause, whether it be a substance or an accident. <note>In medieval philosophy, an "accident" is a property like color or shape that cannot exist on its own, whereas a "substance" is an independent thing.</note> But that "subsists" which is a substance and has being outside of its cause, such as all complete individuals in the category of substance. From this it is clear that Existence is broader than <margin>3</margin> Subsistence itself, because Existence applies to both accidents and substances, whereas subsistence applies to substances alone.

Nor are things merely "existent" through power, but they are also able to act through that same power; therefore, if any being acts, it possesses this from power: if goodness "good-ifies" <note>original: *bonificat*; a technical term meaning the active exercise of goodness</note>, if greatness magnifies, and so with the others: they obtain this through power. In its own way, power is not incompatible with beings formed by the Intellect, insofar as they exist and are able to have some operation.

<margin>4</margin> However, so that you may more correctly use the term "power"—

<summary>This lecture defines Power as the fundamental principle that allows all other attributes and beings to exist and act, distinguishing between simple being, existence, and subsistence. It establishes Power as the bridge between a thing's essence and its ability to perform its natural functions.</summary>

<keywords>Power, Essence, Existence, Subsistence, Substance, Accident, Intellect, Goodness, Greatness, Metaphysics</keywords>

--- Page 129 ---
<meta>This page continues the scholastic discussion of metaphysical categories, specifically focusing on the distinction between "Act" and "Potency" (or potentiality), following the previous definitions of Being.</meta>

# ACADEMIC CABINET OF ARTS: FOURTH CLASS

Furthermore, it seems necessary to say something regarding **Act** and **Potency**. <note>In Aristotelian philosophy, "Act" (Actus) refers to reality or fulfillment, while "Potency" (Potentia) refers to the capacity or possibility of being something.</note> Therefore, take note that these three distinctions are made:

| | |
| :--- | :--- |
| Namely, | That a Being is **actually** or **potentially**, |
| | That a Being is an **act** or a **potency**, |
| | That a Being is **in act** or **in potency**. |

A Being is said to be **actually** <note>original: "actu"</note> when it truly exists. A Being is said to be **potentially** <note>original: "potentia"</note> when it does not yet exist, but can nevertheless exist at some time.

For a Being to be an **act** is nothing other than to be the perfection or the completion of another. To be a **potency** is to be fit for undergoing <note>passive capacity</note> or for doing <note>active capacity</note>. For example, in a physical body, the **form** is called the "act," while the **matter** is called the "potency." <note>This refers to Hylomorphism, the theory that all things are composed of matter and a shaping form.</note> In a composite viewed metaphysically, the **difference** is called the "act," while the **genus** is called the "potency." <note>In logic, a "genus" like "animal" is a potentiality that becomes an "act" or specific reality when a "difference" like "rational" is added to create the species "human."</note> Finally, everything that arrives, completes, and perfects is called **Act**; that which receives, is completed, and is perfected, is called **Potency**.

For a Being to be **in act** <note>original: "in actu"</note> is for that being to possess some act or perfection. To be **in potency** <note>original: "in potentia"</note> is for that being to possess a power for another thing, whether active or passive.

<note>Vocabulary: Act (Actus), Potency (Potentia), Being (Ens), Metaphysics (Metaphysica), Form (Forma), Matter (Materia), Genus (Genus), Difference (Differentia)</note>

<summary>This page defines the fundamental metaphysical distinctions between Act and Potency, explaining how things exist either in reality or as a possibility, and how form perfects matter.</summary>
<keywords>Metaphysics, Act and Potency, Aristotelian philosophy, Form and Matter, Genus and Difference, Scholasticism</keywords>

--- Page 131 ---
<meta>This page continues the classification of knowledge and being within the academic cabinet's fourth class, focusing on the hierarchy of cognition.</meta>

# ACADEMIC CABINET OF ARTS: FOURTH CLASS

-> Three Entities: God, Angel, Rational Soul. <-

If, however, we are speaking of any kind of cognition <note>original: "cognitione"; here meaning the act of knowing or perceiving</note>, there are certain operations not inconsistent with all beings which always presuppose some form of cognition. No being can be found that lacks all appetite <note>original: "appetitu"; in this philosophical context, it refers to a natural inclination or drive toward a goal</note>, since it must at least have an appetite for its own end, and appetite presupposes cognition; thus, Cognition is manifold.

-> Cognition: Natural, Sensitive, Intellectual, Rational. <-

**Natural cognition** is that by which nature knows what is proper or suitable for itself; for a thing would not be carried toward its own nature—as heavy objects move downward—nor would it rest in its own place, if there were no certain cognition of it.

**Sensitive cognition** is that which knows by means of some interior or exterior sense directed toward its object. Brute animals know by this, and in them we call this cognition "instinct," such as the foresight of the ant and the diligence of bees.

**Intellectual cognition** is of the kind found in Angels and in the mode of infused knowledge; in prophecy, it occurs by a simple intuition <note>a direct "seeing" of truth</note> without the need for discourse <note>step-by-step reasoning</note>.

**Rational cognition**, however, occurs through the discourse of reason, whether it be **Wisdom**, which is the cognition of the highest things; or **Science** <note>original: "scientia"; meaning systematic knowledge or a branch of learning</note>, which is a habit of cognition acquired through demonstration; or the **Speculative Intellect**, which arises from speculative principles...

<column-break/>

<summary>This page categorizes the four types of cognition—natural, sensitive, intellectual, and rational—arguing that all beings possess some form of "knowing" that directs them toward their natural ends.</summary>
<keywords>cognition, natural law, instinct, angelic knowledge, wisdom, types of intellect, scholastic philosophy</keywords>

#### On Virtue.

<meta>This page continues the philosophical investigation into the nature of "Virtus" (Power or Virtue), distinguishing it from the mere capacity to act discussed on previous pages.</meta>

# Art Cabinet of the Academy: Class IV

...consistent reasons. It is distinguished, however, from the power of operations mentioned above, because power only implies a lack of resistance to acting, whereas **Virtue** truly removes that resistance and, furthermore, implies a certain property in the agent according to which a similar operation is produced. It is therefore the origin and principle of operations, remaining within the substance and flowing out into the actions themselves.

Now, this is manifold: for

->One kind is { Intellectual,<-
->Theological,<-
->Animal,<-
->Natural,<-
->Celestial.<-

**Intellectual** virtue is that which is administered only by the intellect, such as Wisdom, science, prudence, etc.

**Theological** virtue is that which comes only from above, such as Faith, Hope, Charity, and the graces which produce virtues, whether they be freely given <note>original: "gratis datae"</note>, or making one pleasing <note>original: "gratum facientes"</note>, or prevenient <note>grace that comes before a human decision</note>, or subsequent, or operating, or cooperating.

**Animal** virtue <note>From the Latin "anima" meaning soul or life-force; refers to the functions of living creatures</note> is either sensitive—that is, which operates through the internal or external senses—or motive, by which a living creature moves itself. To this is joined the **Concupiscible** <note>the faculty of desiring or avoiding</note> and **Irascible** <note>the faculty of resisting or overcoming obstacles</note> power; also the vital power by which something lives, and finally the vegetative power.

**Natural** virtue encompasses the powers of the Elements and of things composed of elements. Nature itself can also be called a virtue.

**Celestial** virtue receives within its embrace the heavenly influences which are called "hidden virtues" <note>original: "occultae virtutes"; refers to the unseen astrological or spiritual forces exerted by the stars and planets</note>.

<summary>This page defines 'Virtue' as an active principle within a substance and categorizes it into five distinct types: intellectual, theological, animal, natural, and celestial. It provides a framework for understanding how different forces—from divine grace to biological instincts and astrological influences—operate in the world.</summary>

<keywords>virtue, intellectual wisdom, theological grace, animal soul, natural elements, celestial influences, scholastic philosophy, faculties of the soul</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical definitions within the Academic Kabbalah series, moving from the nature of truth to the definition of glory.</meta>

# Art Cabinet of the Academy. IV. Class.

Truth is the adequacy or conformity of the understood thing to the Intellect. <note>This is the classic scholastic definition of truth: *adaequatio rei et intellectus*.</note>

The truth of a thing is the true preservation of its nature in its own being; hence, falsity exists in a thing when those qualities which rightfully belong to it are not actually present in the thing itself. And this truth is considered either according to **act** (or use) or according to **habit** <note>In philosophy, "habit" refers to a stable disposition or potential, while "act" refers to the current expression of that potential.</note>: thus, a man in a state of fury cannot truly be called a man according to his current act, but according to his habit, he is truly called a man.

Eternal truth is that which is interchangeable with God, and which causes other created truths that are not interchangeable and are capable of failing. This created truth is necessary for things so that they may act naturally and skillfully, and so that God may act naturally within them. You should understand the same regarding other principles. Finally, there are:

<column-break/>

->Oppositions to Truth { Falsity, Error, Lie, Simulation.<-

### LECTURE X.

### Concerning Glory.

*1. What Glory is. 2. Two things concerning the nature of glory. 3. Related and opposing concepts.*

Glory is the very delight in which goodness, greatness, and the other principles and remaining beings find rest. It is, therefore, the rest of a thing in its final end, or the very consummation itself which brings delight, which is found in all things when a thing delights in the attainment of its desire.

# ACADEMIC CABINET OF ARTS. CLASS IV. LECTURE XI.

### On the distinction and order of absolute predicates.

*1. Essential predicates. 2. Personal. 3. Notional. 4. Five notions accepted by Theologians regarding divine matters. 5. Four relations. 6. Four properties. 7. Three notions constituting the persons. 8. Four notional acts. 9. Essential predicates. 10. Causal. 11. Perfecting.*

<margin>1</margin> Enough will have been said concerning the nine absolute predicates of Raymond <note>Referring to Raymond Lull (c. 1232–1315), a philosopher and mystic who developed a system of logic known as the "Ars Magna" or Great Art.</note> once we have shown their distinction and order among themselves.

->Therefore, the first three as they relate to God:<-
->Namely { Goodness, Greatness, Duration,<-

are essential <note>In theology, "essential" refers to attributes shared by the whole Godhead (the Essence), rather than those unique to the Father, Son, or Spirit.</note>; for this reason, Athanasius speaks of goodness in the Creed <note>The Athanasian Creed, a statement of Christian belief focused on Trinitarian doctrine.</note> as follows: *The divinity of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit is one; that is, one and the same supreme goodness.* Regarding greatness: *The Father is immeasurable, the Son is immeasurable, the Holy Spirit is immeasurable.* Finally, regarding duration: *The Father is eternal, the Son is eternal, the Holy Spirit is eternal. Concerning all three together, it is said by the same author: As the Father is, so is the Son, and so is the Holy Spirit.*

<margin>2</margin>
->The three following principles { Power, Knowledge, Will,<- <note>original: "Appetitus" - in this context, it refers to the divine Will or Desire.</note>

are called personal, because they are attributed to the persons—

<summary>This page categorizes the first six of Raymond Lull's absolute predicates into "essential" attributes (Goodness, Greatness, Duration) shared by the Trinity and "personal" attributes (Power, Knowledge, Will) that distinguish the divine persons.</summary>

# ACADEMIC KABBALISTIC ART: FOURTH CLASS

...so that they may be distinguished. For although all essential attributes belong equally to all the persons, power is nevertheless appropriated to the Father, knowledge or wisdom to the Son, and the harmony of unity and equality to the Holy Spirit, because He is the love of both—that is, of the Father and the Son.

<margin>3</margin>
->The final three: { Power, Truth, Glory,<-

are called "notional" <note>original: "notionalia"</note>, as if importing certain notions. A "notion" in divine matters is nothing other than the means of distinguishing one person from another.

In such a notion, however, two conditions are required: first, that it be signified by an abstract name; second, that it be something pertaining to origin, and thus expressed by some notional, or rather personal, term.

<margin>4</margin>
->Of this kind are: { Unbegottenness, Paternity, Filiation, Common Spiration, Procession:<-

These are the five notions accepted by theologians <note>These terms refer to the specific relationships within the Trinity: the Father is not born (Unbegottenness) and is a father (Paternity); the Son is a son (Filiation); and the Holy Spirit proceeds from the Father and Son (Spiration and Procession).</note> which lead us to distinguish the persons. This number is proven by an induction made through the ways in which the persons become known. For the Father becomes known in three ways: First, from the fact that He is from no one, from which exists **unbegottenness** <note>original: "innascibilitas"</note>; Second, from the fact that another is from Him through generation, from which exists **paternity**; Third, from the fact that another is from Him through breathing <note>original: "spirationem," the theological term for the "breathing forth" of the Holy Spirit</note>, from which exists...

<summary>This page explains the theological "notions" used to distinguish the three persons of the Trinity, identifying five specific terms—unbegottenness, paternity, filiation, spiration, and procession—that define their unique relationships.</summary>
<keywords>Trinity, Divine Notions, Paternity, Filiation, Spiration, Unbegottenness, Kabbalistic Art, Scholastic Theology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the scholastic discussion of Trinitarian theology, specifically focusing on the "notions" or characteristics that distinguish the persons of the Trinity.</meta>

# ACADEMIC CABINET ARTICLE IV. CLASS 111

->Common Spiration<- <note>original: "communis Spiratio"; the act of "breathing forth" the Holy Spirit, shared by the Father and the Son.</note>

Similarly, the Son is made known in two ways: first, from the fact that he himself is from another through generation, hence **Filiation** <note>Sonship</note>; second, from the fact that another is from him through spiration, hence also **Common Spiration** with the Father.

But truly, the Holy Spirit is not made known by the fact that another proceeds from him, but only by the fact that he himself is from another, and that through spiration; from which exists his own **Procession**. From these points, it is easy to gather the five aforementioned notions.

Of these five notions, however, four are:

**Relations** { **Paternity**, **Filiation**, **Spiration**, **Procession**

<margin>5</margin>
For **Inascibility** <note>the state of being unbegotten or having no origin</note> is not a relation except in a reductive sense.

There are also four properties of the persons: <margin>6</margin>

**Namely** { **Inascibility**, **Paternity**, **Filiation**, **Procession of the Holy Spirit**

For common spiration is not a property of a single person <note>because it is shared by both Father and Son</note>.

And there are three notions that constitute the persons: <margin>7</margin>

**To wit** { **Paternity**, **Filiation**, **Procession of the Holy Spirit**

<summary>This page outlines the technical theological distinctions—Relations, Properties, and Notions—used to define the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit within the Trinity. It specifically explains how "Spiration" and "Procession" serve to identify the persons and their origins.</summary>
<keywords>Trinity, Paternity, Filiation, Procession, Spiration, Inascibility, Scholastic Theology, Divine Persons</keywords>

--- Page 142 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous discussion regarding the internal relations of the Trinity; the catchword "from these" (ex iis) links to the subsequent development of the divine persons.</meta>

# Art Cabinet of the Academy: Fourth Class

For "unbegottenness" <note>original: *innascibilitas*; the quality of not being born or derived from another</note> and "common breathing" <note>original: *communis spiratio*; the joint action of the Father and Son in producing the Holy Spirit</note> do not, by themselves, constitute the persons.

<margin>8</margin> To this also belong the four "notional acts" <note>technical terms used to describe the internal activities that distinguish the Persons of the Trinity</note>: for to "Fatherhood" belongs active generation; to "Sonship" belongs passive generation; to "common breathing" belongs the active production of the Holy Spirit; and to "procession" belongs the passive breathing of the same Holy Spirit. Finally, it must be observed here that by "unbegottenness," which is the property of the first person, we understand a negation—not only of birth, but of any origin or proceeding whatsoever.

With these things established, we must inquire by what reasoning the three principles—**Power**, **Truth**, and **Glory**—can be called "notional" <note>characteristics that allow us to know or distinguish the persons</note> within divine matters, and how one person can be discerned from another. Therefore, because the Son exists from God the Father through generation, the term **Power** clearly understands "Fatherhood," by which the Father becomes known. And because the Son calls himself the **Truth** in John 14, why should the term **Truth** not signify "Sonship," by which the Son also becomes known? Finally, since **Glory** is the very delight in which beings find rest, and the Father and Son love each other with an essential love—which is attributed to the Holy Spirit, in whom there is the highest delight and highest rest—it is established that by the term **Glory**, as a notional property, the Holy Spirit can be distinguished from the other persons.

These things concern the distinction and order of the "Predicates" <note>the attributes or names applied to God</note> insofar as they refer to God. But if these same things are referred to created things, the first three are called "essential," because they enter into the essence in a certain way, or because the essence subsists through them. The following three are called "causal," because with these and...

# ACADEMIC ART CABINET: FOURTH CLASS

<margin>2</margin> They are understood to approach [the truth], for this Essence indeed receives several other names: for it is called Nature, Quiddity <note>original: "Quidditas" - the "what-ness" or defining essence of a thing</note>, the form of the whole, or the Formal Reason—that is, the essential nature of each thing. It is twofold in its genus, namely Simple and <margin>3</margin> Composite. The Simple is devoid of multiple degrees or entities, since it is the purest and simplest act, such as the divine essence alone.

The Composite admits several degrees of essential perfection, from which it is composed as if from parts. Some of these are called potential parts <term>potential parts: the general characteristics a thing shares with others</term> in respect to others, which are more common; others are called actual parts <term>actual parts: the specific characteristics that define a thing's unique identity</term>, which are more specific. Thus, genera are called potential parts in respect to their differences, while the differences are called actual parts. Indeed, one more universal generic degree will be called a potential part in respect to another more specific generic degree, while the latter is called an actual part. This is evident in a human being, in whom is found the being of a substance, of a body, of a living thing, of a sensing thing, and of a rational thing. From these degrees of essential perfection, the total essence of the human is composed, so that the being of substance is a potential part in respect to the others, and the being of a body is potential in respect to the being of a living thing.

<margin>4</margin> And since everything has an essence according to how it participates in the nature of being, and not all things participate in the nature of being in the same way, it follows that the nature of essence behaves differently in different kinds of things. For in complete material substances, the entity of the whole is composed of both its parts, namely matter and

# ACADEMIC CABINET OF THE KABBALAH, CLASS IV. 115

The form, coming together, holds the character of essence. In spiritual substances, the character of essence is placed in the simple form alone, subsisting through itself, because in Angels no composition of matter and form is found since they are incorporeal. <note>The author is using Scholastic terminology where "form" is the organizing principle and "matter" is the physical stuff; Angels, being pure spirit, lack the "stuff."</note> Note here that the essence of an Angel is called a "simple form" in comparison to physical things, not because it is devoid of all composition, since it consists of a genus and a difference <note>In logic, a "genus" is a general category and a "difference" is the specific trait that identifies a species within that category.</note> and various levels of essential perfections.

However, the character of the Divine essence consists in a most simple form subsisting through itself, entirely devoid of all composition—even that being which is made of genus and difference—and in no way differing from existence itself. In this, it is distinguished from the Angelic essence, which is composed of genus and difference, and which by the nature of the thing differs from its own existence. <note>The author argues that God's "what-ness" (essence) is identical to His "is-ness" (existence), whereas an Angel's existence is a gift added to its nature.</note>

Finally, the essence of an <term>accident</term> <note>In philosophy, an "accident" is a property that can change without changing the nature of the thing, like the color of a shirt.</note> is a certain mode of substance; for every accident modifies the substance in some way. Every accident is, in respect to substance, a form and a certain act, partly agreeing with the substantial form and partly differing from it.

It is **partly agreeing**: for just as the form cannot be separated from the matter in which it has once been received, so an accident cannot exist separated from its subject. And just as form is defined in relation to its subject, so too is the accident. And just as substantial being is constituted from matter and form, so a certain "accidental being" is constituted from the accident and the subject.

It is **partly differing**: first, because the subject of a substantial form—namely, prime matter—does not...

# ACADEMIC CABINET OF ARTS: FOURTH CLASS

can exist without it, or without another succeeding in its place. However, a subject can be entirely deprived of its **accidental form** <note>A property that can change without destroying the thing itself, like the color of a wall.</note>, even if no other replaces it—as when the air is deprived of light—because the subject does not cease to be a complete being without it. Secondly, from matter and **substantial form** <note>The essential nature that makes a thing what it is, such as the "soul" of a living being.</note> there arises a "unity in itself," because they relate to one another as act and potency of the same kind; but this is not the case with an accidental form and its subject. Thirdly, a substantial form is essentially incomplete on its own, whereas an accidental form is complete within its own formal definition; therefore, an accident can be placed directly within the series of its own Category <note>Referring to Aristotle's ten categories, such as Quality or Quantity.</note>, while a substantial form cannot.

From what has been said, it is clear that the essence of an accident is rightly called by us a certain "mode" of substance, for it modifies the substance by existing within it. This mode is called **inherence**, in which the formal definition of an accident is placed. However, since it is one thing for an accident to inhere in actuality, and another for it to be able to inhere, we must distinguish between two types of inherence: one is **actual inherence**, when the accident is currently in the subject; the other is **aptitudinal inherence** <note>The inherent capacity or "fitness" to belong to a subject, even if currently separated.</note>, when the accident is not currently inhering but is nevertheless capable of doing so. We maintain that the formal definition or essence of an accident is placed not in actual, but in aptitudinal inherence. Thus, even if an accident is conceived as separated from its subject, it does not cease to be essentially and formally an accident, because it is always conceived as "fit" to inhere in a subject. Therefore, there is no contradiction in God, by His infinite power, separating an accident from its subject and preserving it in that separated state—which we believe by faith to happen in the au- <note>The text cuts off at "au-", likely referring to the "August" mystery of the Altar or the Eucharist, where accidents of bread and wine remain without their original substance.</note>

<summary>This page distinguishes between substantial and accidental forms, defining the essence of an 'accident' as its capacity to belong to a substance (aptitudinal inherence). It concludes by arguing that God can miraculously sustain properties, like color or shape, even when separated from their physical substance.</summary>

# Art Cabinet of the Academy: Fourth Class

->The Most August Sacrament of the Eucharist.<-

But since, as we have said before, **Existence**, **Subsistence**, **Inherence**, and **Entity** are added to the **Essence** <note>Essence refers to the "what-ness" or definition of a thing, while existence refers to the fact that it actually is.</note> of a thing, we must also treat these under this term.

**Existence**, therefore, is a certain intrinsic mode of essence by which a thing is formally said to be "in act," or outside of its causes. For before a thing exists, it is said to be "in power" <note>original: "poteftate"; referring to potentiality or the possibility of being.</note> and, as it were, to lie hidden within its causes; it begins to exist only when it comes forth by the power of those causes.

However, **Existence** belongs both to God and to created things, yet with a distinction. For God exists not through an existence added to His nature, but through His very essence itself, just as **Quantity** is said to be extended by its own self. Created things, however, exist through an existence added to their essence. From this it follows that it is essential for God to exist, so much so that it is a contradiction for Him not to exist. This is not the case for created things, which are able to be and not to be. Therefore, the Divine nature cannot be conceived except as actually existing; for if He were conceived as not actually existing, some perfection would be lacking in Him, which is utterly inconsistent with His actual infinity. In contrast, the formal or essential concept of a creature abstracts from existence <note>Meaning we can define what a human or a tree is without those things necessarily existing at this moment.</note>.

It must also be said that existence belongs to **Substance** and **Accident** <note>In Aristotelian philosophy, a Substance is a thing that exists in itself (like a horse), while an Accident is a quality that exists only in something else (like the color brown).</note> in different ways. For it belongs to substance primarily and most properly; but to accident secondarily and less properly, for an accident does not exist naturally except by the benefit of the substance to which it adheres.

->Art Cabinet of the Academy. IV. Classis.<-

Furthermore, **Existence** belongs primarily and through itself to singular things and individuals; secondarily and through something else (or in a qualified sense) to universals <note>In medieval philosophy, "universals" are general concepts like "humanity," while "singulars" are specific individuals like "Socrates."</note>. For those things properly exist which are primarily and through themselves created and produced; and yet it is singular things that are of this kind, not universals, which are said to exist only within singular things.

Finally, existence belongs primarily and through itself to the **whole composite**, and secondarily (and less properly) to the parts. For that which is made or brought forth through itself is the whole composite, because the whole is the natural end-point of the process of generation; the parts, however, exist only insofar as they pertain to the integrity of the whole.

**6.** **Subsistence**, properly understood, is the real mode by which a nature is constituted as a "supposit" or "hypostasis" <note>original: "suppositum seu hypostasis." These terms refer to an individual substance that stands on its own.</note>—that is, a singular substance that is complete and substantially incommunicable <note>In this context, "incommunicable" means something that cannot be shared or divided among multiple individuals, marking it as a unique "self."</note>.

**Personality** is an incommunicable subsistence belonging to an intelligent nature. A **Person**, moreover, is a rational "supposit," such as, in divine matters, the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit; and in created matters, this specific Angel, or this specific man, etc.

From these points, you understand that neither accidents, nor universal substances, nor even parts, nor communicable substances (such as the Divine Nature which is common to the three Divine Persons) can be called "supposits."

**7.** You may ask here whether a nature can exist without subsistence. I respond that a created, individual, and complete substance cannot exist without subsisting; for that mode of existing is necessarily owed to a complete substance actually exi—

Nevertheless, since a singular nature <note>A specific, individual instance of a nature, such as a specific human being's humanity.</note> is prior in nature to its own subsistence, it can happen by divine power that it exists without its natural mode of subsistence, provided it is terminated by another substituted mode. This occurs in the mystery of the Incarnation, in which the human nature, distinct from its own proper personhood <note>original: "supposito"; the individual substance or "hypostasis" that acts as the subject of existence.</note>, is terminated by a foreign one—namely, the subsisting Divine Word <note>original: "verbo diuino"; referring to the second person of the Trinity.</note>. This mode of existing can be called not "subsistence," but "actual inexistence" <note>In this context, "inexistence" does not mean "not existing," but rather "existing within" another.</note>. This is different from the "inherence" of accidents, since through inherence, accidents are in matter as if in a subject, informing it accidentally. However, the human nature (for example) is not in the Divine Word as if in a subject that it informs, but rather as in a boundary by which it is extrinsically terminated, so that it cannot be communicated to any other personhood. Furthermore, the mode of inhering in a proper subject is natural to an accident, whereas the mode of "inexisting" is supernatural for a substance. Finally, the former is accidental, while the latter is substantial.

Just as essence is spoken of regarding both accidents and substances, so too is entity. For it is really the same as the essence itself, yet differs from it formally, just as a mode differs from the thing modified. Therefore, entity—taken formally in corporeal things, to which it most greatly applies—is a certain intrinsic mode according to which things are entitatively divisible, as...

Nor are entitative parts found only in substances, but also in <term>accidents</term> <note>non-essential qualities like color or heat</note>, especially those which admit of "more" and "less": For many degrees of heat, cold, and other qualities which can be intensified or diminished, are their own entitative parts. Note, however, that sometimes "entity" is taken for the nature or essence of any thing, whether it be divisible into entitative parts or not, and thus the substance of an Angel can properly be called an entity.

# ACADEMIC CABINET OF ARTS. CLASS III.

### LECTURE XIII.
### On Unity.

->1. *What unity is.* 2. *What "one" adds to "being."* 3. *How many kinds of unities there are.* 4. *Related and opposing concepts.*<-

<margin>1</margin> Unity is that according to which something is said to be "one." Moreover, the term "one" adds to the concept of "being" <note>original: "enti"</note> the negation of division from itself; from which it follows that "one" is nothing other than being that is undivided. <margin>2</margin> Although that which is called "one" is not only undivided in itself, but is also simultaneously divided from everything else, the essence of transcendental unity <note>Transcendental unity refers to a property that applies to all things that exist, regardless of their specific category.</note> consists primarily in the former (internal wholeness) rather than the latter (separation from others).

<margin>3</margin> Generally, five kinds of unities are established:
Namely:
{ Transcendental.
{ Categorical.
{ Numerical.
{ Formal.
{ Universal.

Transcendental unity is that which primarily belongs to being insofar as it is being, and to all other things insofar as they are beings.

Categorical unity is that which they <note>referring to the Scholastic philosophers</note> maintain is proper to "discrete quantity" <term>discrete quantity: a mathematical term for things that can be counted as individual units, like integers, rather than measured continuously like water</term>, such that discrete quantity—or number—consists of a multiple of this kind of unity.

Numerical unity is that which renders a nature incapable of being shared with subordinates; or, that which follows the "individual difference" of any nature whatsoever <note>This refers to 'haecceity' or 'this-ness,' the quality that makes an object a specific individual rather than just a member of a species.</note>.

<note>Vocabulary: unity, being, transcendental, categorical, numerical, formal, universal, discrete quantity</note>

<summary>This lecture defines unity as the state of being undivided and distinguishes between transcendental unity, which applies to all existence, and specific types like categorical and numerical unity. It establishes the philosophical foundation for how an object is identified as a single, distinct entity.</summary>
<keywords>metaphysics, unity, transcendental properties, discrete quantity, numerical identity, being, scholastic philosophy</keywords>

# ACADEMIC CABINET OF ARTS: FOURTH CLASS

...although that nature can be numerically one and yet communicable to several subjects <note>The Latin 'suppositis' refers to 'supposita' or individual substances that act as the foundation for a nature.</note>, as is evident in the divine nature; for although it is essentially one, it is nevertheless communicated to three Persons.

**Formal unity** is that which follows the formal definition <note>original: 'formalem rationem'</note> of any given thing.

**Universal unity** can be understood in two ways: first, as that which characterizes a universal nature fit to be in many things prior to any operation of the Intellect; second, as a certain unity of reason which belongs to natures abstracted from lower forms by the operation of the Intellect. For by that operation, they are called "universal" by a certain universality of reason; hence, only a unity of reason belongs to them when considered in that way.

### Lecture XIV.

### On Perfection.

1. *What is perfect and perfection.* 2. *Related and opposing concepts.*

<margin>1</margin> If you look at the force of the name, "Perfect" signifies that which lacks nothing according to its own nature: or rather, that to which entity and perfection are present. Therefore, for a thing to be "transcendentally perfect" <note>In scholastic philosophy, "transcendental" refers to properties that apply to all beings regardless of their specific category.</note> is for a thing to be that which it ought to be in the nature of being. Perfection, therefore, will be that according to which some thing is finished and complete within its own boundaries.

# THE FIFTH CLASS.

### Concerning Relative Predicates.

-> LECTURE I. <-

> 1. *Why relative predicates are so named. 2. How many types of relations there are. 3. Why they are arranged into an angular figure and three intertwined triangles. 4. The use of these triangles.*

<margin>1</margin>
We enter the fifth class, by God's favor, in which we shall examine Figure T <note>In the Lullian Art, Figure T represents the principles of relation</note>, that is, the relative predicates. They are called "relative," "relational," or "habitudinal" because they are not spoken of absolutely regarding a single subject, but instead create a comparison between different things. For example, if it is said *God is good*, there is no comparison; however, if it is said *God differs from an Angel* or from a Human by infinite goodness and eternity, a comparison is made.

<margin>2</margin>
Raymond <note>referring to Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1316), the philosopher and mystic who created this system</note> divides the types of relations into three triangles, within which almost all remaining relations are contained.

The first triangle contains *Difference, Concordance, and Opposition (or Contrariety).*

The second contains *Beginning, Middle, and End.*

The third contains *Majority, Equality, and Minority.*

<summary>This page introduces the fifth category of the Lullian system, focusing on "Figure T" and the nine relative predicates used to compare different subjects. These predicates are organized into three distinct triangles: Difference/Concordance/Opposition, Beginning/Middle/End, and Majority/Equality/Minority.</summary>

<keywords>Relative Predicates, Figure T, Ramon Llull, Logic, Comparison, Triangles of Relation, Difference, Concordance, Opposition, Beginning, Middle, End, Majority, Equality, Minority</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the visual and conceptual explanation of the Relative Predicates (Figure T) introduced on page 124, transitioning from the list of terms to their geometric representation.</meta>

<page-num>125</page-num>

# THE ACADEMIC KABBALISTIC ART. <note>original: "ART. KAB. ACAD." - Here "Kabbalistic" refers to the Lullian method of combining symbols to discover truths, which contemporary scholars often compared to the Kabbalah.</note> THE FIFTH CLASS.

<image-desc>A circular diagram containing a nine-pointed star (enneagram) formed by three overlapping equilateral triangles. The center of the star is marked with the letter "T" <note>Figure T represents the "Principles of Relation" in Llull's system</note>. The outer ring of the circle is divided into nine segments, each containing a letter and its corresponding translated philosophical term. Starting from the top and moving clockwise, the segments are: B - Difference, E - Beginning, H - Majority, C - Concordance, F - Middle, I - Equality, D - Opposition, G - End, K - Minority.</image-desc>

They are arranged into an angular figure so that their penetration through all things might be signified; for each of these individual terms possesses a certain "penetration" or interconnectedness among the others. <note>In this context, "penetration" refers to how these logical categories overlap and apply to one another—for example, a "Beginning" can also have "Difference" or "Equality."</note>

**Technical Vocabulary:**
<term>Artis Cabalisticae</term> (The Kabbalistic Art), <term>Differentia</term> (Difference), <term>Principium</term> (Beginning or Principle), <term>Majoritas</term> (Majority or Greatness), <term>Concordantia</term> (Concordance or Agreement), <term>Medium</term> (Middle), <term>Æqualitas</term> (Equality), <term>Oppositio</term> (Opposition), <term>Finis</term> (End), <term>Minoritas</term> (Minority or Smallness), <term>penetratio</term> (penetration/interweaving).

# ACADEMIC CABINET OF THE ARTS: FOURTH CLASS

They are arranged by triangles insofar as in all relations and conditions there are two related terms and a middle relation between them. <note>The author refers to the Lullian method of using geometric shapes to visualize how different concepts—like Goodness or Greatness—interact with one another.</note>

There are three intertwined terms, so that not only their entry into other things but also their mutual penetration into one another may be observed: for we speak of *differing goodness and the difference of goodness; likewise the Difference of that which has a beginning, and of the beginning itself; likewise the beginning of that which differs and of the difference.* <note>original: "bonitatem differentem & bonitatis differentiam." This is a linguistic exercise to show how the fundamental categories of reality are inseparable.</note>

Therefore, they penetrate not only themselves, but also all the subjects of circle S and the absolute predicates of circle A, whether they are explicit or implicit. <note>In this system, "Circle A" usually represents the attributes of God (Goodness, Greatness, etc.) and "Circle S" represents the soul or the human subject.</note>

<margin>4</margin>
But what is the use of these triangles? We use Accordance <note>original: "Concordantia"</note> in proving any affirmative proposition; in destroying a proposition, we use difference or opposition. In a negative proposition, difference is suited for solutions, Accordance for proving and finding a middle term, and Opposition for disproving, which difference serves.

From the second triangle, we gather the necessity, causality, or contingency of any thing.

The third triangle proves little or nothing except from the topics joined to it; for this reason, it is more useful for the amplification of speech <term>amplification: the rhetorical expansion of an argument to make it more persuasive</term> than for proof. Now, let us speak of these individually.

# ACADEMIC CABINET OF ARTS. CLASS IV.

### LECTURE II.

#### On Difference.

1. *What difference is.* 2. *Its multiple forms.* 3. *The multiple forms of Genus and its definition.* 4. *What Species is and its multiple forms.* 5. *Difference of reason.* 6. *Related and opposing things.*

Difference is that through which goodness, magnitude, etc., are clear and not confused concepts <note>original: "rationes"</note>: or otherwise, Difference is that by which one thing differs from another essentially, accidentally, or really. Therefore, Difference is:

->Other { Essential, Accidental, Real.<-

Essential difference is when things compared to one another differ in something essential, just as a human differs from a stone by being animate <note>having a soul or life-force</note>, and this is called a "more proper" difference. This is twofold: namely, that which constitutes a species and that which divides a genus. For example, "rationality"—along with the difference opposite to it—divides the category of "animal," and when added to that category, it constitutes a species, namely "human" and "beast." Thus, whatever things differ by their action <note>original: "actum"</note> and their object also differ essentially, such as the Will and the Intellect <term>Intellectus: the faculty of understanding or thought</term>.

Accidental difference occurs through an "accident" <note>a non-essential quality</note>, whether it be a property unique to the thing or one held in common, such as the ability to laugh or being two-footed.

Real difference is the general principle by which things differ through the distinction they possess within themselves.

<summary>This lecture defines "Difference" as the quality that distinguishes one concept or thing from another, categorizing it into essential, accidental, and real types. It explains how essential differences define species (like humans) within broader categories (like animals).</summary>
<keywords>Logic, Metaphysics, Essential Difference, Accidental Difference, Genus and Species, Intellect and Will, Scholasticism</keywords>

# Art Cabinet of the Academy, Fifth Class

And in these three ways, Difference is derived from primary intentions <note>Primary intentions refer to our direct mental grasp of things in the world, like "a tree" or "a person."</note>: However, when it is derived from secondary intentions <note>Secondary intentions are logical concepts we use to categorize our thoughts, such as "genus" or "species."</note>,

Others differ by { Genus, Species, Number.

Things differ in **Genus** when diverse genera <note>The plural of genus; a broad category or "family" of things.</note> are predicated of them that are neither subordinate nor closely related: such as a human and a pear tree, of which "animal" and "tree" are predicated.

<margin>3</margin> I said *subordinate*, for a genus is twofold: one is supreme, which acknowledges no other above itself, such as **Substance**, **Quantity**, etc.; the other is subordinate, which is contained under another genus, and in respect to that higher category, it is called a species. For example, *Animal*, which is a genus in respect to humans and beasts, is called a species when compared to "living thing," which contains both plants and animals. Here we understand both types of genus, in which sense a twofold definition of Genus is usually assigned: one Dialectical <note>Relating to logic and formal debate.</note>, the other Metaphysical <note>Relating to the fundamental nature of reality and being.</note>.

**Genus** is defined dialectically as: That which is predicated of many things differing in species, answering the question "What is it?" <note>original: "in quid"</note>.

But it is defined metaphysically as: That which is apt to be in many things differing in species essentially and not through something else.

In the first definition, it is said first *to be predicated of many*, in which a genus differs from any particular individual thing. Secondly, it is said *to be predicated of many things differing in species*, in which it differs from a species and a specific property, which are only predicated of many things differing in number <note>i.e., individuals of the same kind, like two different people.</note>. Thirdly, it is said *to be predicated in the "what"* <note>original: "in quid"; this means it defines the essence of the thing</note>, so that it may be distinguished from **Difference** and **Accident** <note>An "accident" is a non-essential quality, like being tall or sitting down.</note>, and

<summary>This page defines the logical concept of "Genus" using both dialectical and metaphysical frameworks, explaining how it categorizes things that are fundamentally different in kind.</summary>
<keywords>Logic, Genus, Species, Dialectics, Metaphysics, Categorization, Substance, Quantity, Predication</keywords>

# ACADEMIC ART OF THE CABINET, FIFTH CLASS

...from you, and from any property—even a generic one—which are predicated *qualitatively* <note>original: "in quale"; this refers to describing the qualities of a thing rather than its essential "what-ness."</note>. Now, however, regarding what is stated in the second definition—that a *Genus is fit to be in many things differing in species*—it also differs from the species itself. In the phrase *that which is essentially present*, it differs from the property and the accident, which do not pertain to the essence of the thing. Finally, in the phrase *said to be in many*, it is distinguished by nothing else from the generic difference; for although the generic difference is fit to be essentially in many things differing in species, it is not so through itself and immediately, but through something else—namely, by means of the genus of which it is called the difference. Generic properties are also excluded by these specific terms.

Those things differ in species which fall under different species, such as "this man" and "this horse" falling under Man and Horse. Moreover, a Species is that which is placed under a genus; or, a species is that of which the genus is predicated. These two definitions are suited to the "subalternate" <note>intermediate</note> and "lowest" species. However, the following definition, which states that *a species is that which is predicated of many things differing in number in answer to the question 'what is it?'* <note>original: "prædicatur in quid"; a technical term for defining the essential nature of a thing.</note>, applies only to the lowest species.

From these points, you may gather that the species is twofold: subalternate and lowest.

The **Subalternate** (which is also called the "interposed" or "middle" species), although it is a species in comparison to the genus it is under, is nevertheless a genus in comparison to the lower things of which it is predicated.

The **Lowest** (which is also called the "most specific" <note>original: "specialissima"</note>), however, can never be a genus.

The former is predicated immediately of species, while the latter is predicated of individuals. The former contains only a part of the nature, while the latter contains the entire nature of the individual.

<term>Genus</term>: A general category. <term>Species</term>: A sub-category. <term>Generic difference</term>: The characteristic that divides a genus. <term>Subalternate</term>: An intermediate level in a hierarchy. <term>Lowest/Specialissima</term>: The most specific category that cannot be divided further. <term>Predicated in quid</term>: Stating the essential "whatness" of a subject. <term>Essence</term>: The necessary internal nature of a thing.

5 And because we understand "difference" here to mean every distinction and diversity—not only of the thing itself, but also of the reasoning applied to it—that must also be discussed.

However, a difference of this kind is not a real being <term>ens reale: a thing that exists independently of human thought</term>, nor is it a general principle, like the difference between the finite and the infinite; otherwise, a middle ground would be granted which would be neither God nor creature.

### LECTURE III.
### On Concordance.

*1. The utility of concordance and difference. 2. What concordance is. 3. Its many forms. 4. The method of fashioning infinite identities. 5. What Difference, Property, and Accident are as universals. 6. Related and Repugnant things.*

<margin>1</margin>
From those things which have been said regarding Difference, the nature of Concordance <note>The state of agreement or harmony between concepts</note> will be no less evident, since it is the condition of opposites that when one is known, it leads to the knowledge of the other. Nor does the knowledge of Concordance bring less utility than Difference; for just as through the latter truth is severed from falsehood and objections are dissolved, so through the former the intent is proven and the necessary means for arguing are discovered.

<margin>2</margin>
Concordance, therefore, is that through which goodness, greatness, and the other principles placed in the Art, and all beings, agree in one or in many things.

Furthermore, Concordance is taken in this place for any "convenience" <note>original: "conuenientia"; here meaning a coming together or suitability</note> whatsoever, to which we shall assign many degrees, so that each person at their pleasure among

# ACADEMIC ARTS, CLASS V.

...finds some harmony and consensus. Therefore, it is called Concordance or agreement.

<margin>3</margin>
Another type is:
<note>The author uses a branching diagram to show that "Concordance" is a broad category containing these twelve specific types.</note>
*   Of Union,
*   Of Mixing,
*   Of Composition,
*   Of Continuity,
*   Of Separation,
*   Of Matter and form,
*   Of many things meeting at a shared boundary,
*   Of Proportion,
*   Of Equality,
*   Of Effect,
*   Of Similarity,
*   Of Identity.

**Concordance of union** exists according to one of the modes of unity, such as the union of parts within a quantity, or of universal concepts within time.

**Concordance of mixing** is the harmony of mixed things within a mixture. If the mixing is "true," it is found only among the four elements <note>Earth, Air, Fire, and Water</note>, insofar as they are altered and mixed together to constitute a single composite in which their powers remain. If, however, the mixing is "improper" (which is called mixing by <term>juxtaposition</term> <note>placing things side-by-side without them merging into a new substance</note>), it is found among things already made of elements; by this kind of mixing, water and earth are combined to form mud.

**Concordance of composition** takes place in those things where one part has the nature of <term>act</term> <note>the completed or active state</note> and the other has the nature of <term>potency</term> <note>the capacity or potential to be acted upon</note>. Here you will note in passing that "union" is a broader term than "mixing" or "composition," because the latter two cannot exist without union, yet union can be found without them—as is evident in the human nature joined to the Divine Word... <note>original: "verbo divino"; a reference to the Christian doctrine of the Incarnation</note>

# Art Cabinet of the Academy: Fifth Class

not hypostatically <note>original: "hypostaticè"; referring to a union of distinct natures into one person or substance without mixing them</note> united without mixture or composition.

The concordance of continuity is the agreement of continuous things within a continuum.

The concordance of discretion <note>or "distinction"</note> is the agreement of discrete things within a discrete whole.

The sixth is the concordance of matter and form within a single subject <term>supposito: an individual substance or person that exists in its own right</term>.

The seventh is the concordance of several things in a concurring end-point, as a man and a woman are joined in a son.

The eighth is the concordance of proportion, by which several things meet in a third thing and are equal among themselves; this is not to be understood as the equality which is a property of measurable quantity, but taken in the most transcendent sense. Hence we say there is a proportion between goodness, greatness, and duration, because just as goodness regards the essence, so greatness regards the perfection of the essence, and duration regards its existence. But to speak more clearly, any concordance or agreement of things toward a third point is a proportion.

The ninth concordance is that of equality, that is, of equal things within the same quantity.

The tenth is the concordance of effect, such as heat and cold agreeing in the hardening of the earth.

The eleventh is the concordance of similarity, by which diverse things agree among themselves through some likeness.

The twelfth is the concordance of Identity.

<margin>4</margin>If you wish to prepare for yourself an infinite number of Identities, you will be able to use this means, namely by running through <note>original: "discurrendo"; the process of moving from one premise to another in reasoning</note> them.
