-># ACADEMIC KABBALISTIC ART: FIFTH CLASS<-

First, through the five <term>predicables</term><note>Also known as the five universals; these are the five ways a characteristic can be related to a subject in logic.</note> or universals, which are: **Genus**, **Species**, **Difference**, **Property**, and **Accident**.

You already have the definitions of **Genus** and **Species** as universals from the second lecture of this class; take also the definitions of the remaining terms for this purpose.

<margin>5</margin> **Difference**, therefore, is that which is suited to be inherent in a species, such that it is its actual part, and through the species, it is inherent in all those things contained under that species.

**Property** is that which is suited to be inherent in a nature—whether generic or specific—by itself and necessarily, though not essentially, and in all its subordinates. Or, a property is that which is predicated of many things by itself and necessarily, but not essentially. <note>In logic, a "property" is a characteristic that always belongs to a species (like "the ability to laugh" for humans) but does not define its core essence.</note>

**Accident** is that which is suited to be inherent in many things and to be predicated of them contingently. <note>An "accident" is a non-essential quality that can change without the subject losing its identity, such as being "seated" or "tall."</note>

Secondly, you will provide yourself with innumerable identities by running through the ten <term>predicaments</term><note>The ten Categories of Aristotle (Substance, Quantity, Quality, Relation, etc.).</note>, which were discussed in the third class.

Thirdly, through the eighteen principles of Figures A and T, which were discussed in the fourth and this fifth class. <note>These refer to the mnemonic wheels and diagrams used in the Lullian Art to combine concepts.</note>

Fourthly, through the nine subjects of Figure S, which we dealt with in the second class.

Fifthly, through the rules or questions of Figure C, which are to be dealt with in the sixth class.

And to these **Concordances**, all other Concordances are reduced.

# ACADEMIC ARTS CABINET, FIFTH CLASS.

We consider difference so that through it, beings remain distinct and not confused; however, we consider opposition so that things are not only distinguished, but also maintain a certain conflict among themselves.

<margin>2</margin> Opposition, therefore, is a mutual resistance of certain things on account of different ends, which is found

<margin>3</margin> Between <note>The author distinguishes between complex statements and simple terms</note>
->Complex<-
->&<-
->Incomplex.<-

When it occurs among complex things—that is, propositions—it is fourfold. For in propositions which agree in having a similar subject and predicate, four types of opposition arise.

Namely:
->Contrary,<-
->Contradictory,<-
->Subcontrary,<-
->Subalternate.<-

**Contraries** are the universal affirmative <note>original: "universalis affirmativa"; e.g., "All men are wise"</note> and the universal negative <note>original: "universalis negativa"; e.g., "No men are wise"</note> when opposed.

**Contradictories** are the universal affirmative and the particular negative <note>e.g., "Some men are not wise"</note>; or the universal negative and the particular affirmative <note>e.g., "Some men are wise"</note> when opposed.

**Subalternates** are the universal affirmative and the particular affirmative; or the universal negative and the particular negative.

**Subcontraries** are the particular affirmative and the particular negative, as is evident in this figure <note>The text refers to a diagram of the Square of Opposition, likely appearing on the following page or in the margin</note>.

<term>Opposition: The relationship between two propositions that have the same subject and predicate but differ in quantity (all vs. some) or quality (is vs. is not).</term>
<term>Complex: In logic, this refers to full statements or propositions rather than single words.</term>
<term>Incomplex: Simple terms or individual concepts that have not yet been combined into a sentence.</term>

| | \ / | |

| | / \ | |

<meta>This page continues the classification of logical and physical oppositions, likely following a discussion of general categories or the first type of opposition (contradiction) on the previous page.</meta>

# Art Cabinet of the Academy: Fifth Class

**Relative Opposition** exists between those things which are referred to one another by mutual relations: thus a Father is relatively opposed to a Son, and a son to a father; likewise, one similar thing to another similar thing. This type of opposition is lighter than the other three types of opposition, for related things do not fight against one another since they rather bring each other into being <note>original: "se mutuò inferant" - a logical term meaning the existence of one implies the existence of the other</note>.

**Contrary Opposition** is properly between those accidents <term>accidents: non-essential qualities that can change without destroying the thing itself</term> which, while being suited to be received in the same subject, positively expel one another from it. Such an opposition exists between the primary qualities, namely **heat** and **cold**, **moisture** and **dryness**; hence even things endowed with these qualities are also said to be contrarily opposed, though less properly so.

Furthermore, some contraries are **mediated**, such as **white** and **black** <note>meaning there are intermediate states like grey</note>; others are **immediate**, such as **virtue** and **vice**.

**Privative Opposition** is seen between a privation <term>privation: the absence of a quality that should naturally be present</term> and the thing of which it is a privation, as between **blindness** and **sight**; and also by name between their subjects, as between a **blind person** and a **seeing person**. Moreover, privation can best be defined as the negation of a natural form in a subject suited to receive it. From this definition, you understand that a physical privation is not formally a "being" <note>original: "ens"</note> since it is the negation of a being; nor is it the negation of just any being, but of a natural form—not an artificial or supernatural form. Finally, that negation must be observed in some subject that is naturally suited to receive such a form, which is why blindness is not <note>The text cuts off here, likely continuing to explain why a stone cannot be called "blind" because it lacks the natural capacity for sight.</note>

<summary>This page defines three types of logical and physical opposition: Relative (interdependent relations), Contrary (conflicting qualities like heat and cold), and Privative (the absence of a natural faculty like sight).</summary>
<keywords>logic, Relative Opposition, Contrary Opposition, Privative Opposition, primary qualities, accidents, privation, natural form</keywords>

### LECTURE V.
### On the Principle.

*1. The Principle is distinguished from power and virtue. 2. What a Principle is. 3. The multiple meanings of Principle. 4. Related and Opposing terms.*

<margin>1</margin>
Someone might be of the opinion that this universal principle is not distinct from power and virtue; however, it differs greatly from them, because a "principle" is a certain kind of relation, whereas power and virtue are absolute qualities. <note>The author distinguishes between a "principle" (the starting point or source of a thing) and the "power" or "virtue" (the inherent capacity or strength) that the thing possesses.</note>

<margin>2</margin>
Therefore, a Principle, as Llull <note>referring to Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1316), the Majorcan philosopher and mystic whose "Art" this text explains</note> says, is that which stands in relation to another thing by reason of some priority; and of this, there are multiple meanings.

<margin>3</margin>
First, it is taken for the beginning of any thing or action, just as we say that in every motion there is a beginning, a middle, and an end: likewise, the tiny portion from which the extension of any quantity begins can be called the "beginning" of that quantity. This type of principle can be called "ordinal," by which something is prior either by nature or by time.

Secondly, it is taken for that from which a thing begins, not as if from a part of itself, but as if from that by which the first part itself begins; in this way, a point is called the principle of a line, and an instant is the principle <note>The text cuts off here, likely continuing to define the "instant" as the beginning of time or a moment.</note>

<summary>This page introduces Lecture V on the concept of the 'Principle,' defining it as a relational starting point and distinguishing it from absolute power or virtue. It begins to categorize the different ways a principle can be understood, such as ordinal beginnings in motion or geometry.</summary>
<keywords>Principle, Ramon Llull, Power, Virtue, Motion, Quantity, Geometry, Logic, Definitions</keywords>

# ACADEMIC ART CABINET, FIFTH CLASS

of time, or even of an action that occurs in time; just as a spring is the beginning of a stream, unity is the beginning of number, and a father is the beginning of a son. This type of principle is called **original**.

Thirdly, it is taken for every superior thing according to its classification <note>original: "prædicationem," referring to how terms are predicated or assigned to categories</note> which belongs to the essence of its inferior. For example, a genus is a principle in respect to its species, and a species is a principle of its individuals and singular things of which it is predicated as to "what" they are. Even a "difference" <note>the specific characteristic that distinguishes one species from another</note> is in a certain way the principle of its species. This type of principle is named **universal**.

Fourthly, it is accepted for a cause, such that whatever is the cause of a thing is called its principle. This is termed a **causal** principle, and it is a primitive principle because no being can exist before its cause; for a cause is that upon which something depends by its very nature.

Furthermore, among causes, one type is "through itself" <term>per se: a direct or essential cause</term> and another is "by accident" <term>per accidens: an incidental or indirect cause</term>. Causes "through themselves" are said to be either intrinsic or extrinsic. **Intrinsic** causes are matter and form, which enter into the very constitution of the thing. **Extrinsic** causes are the efficient <note>the agent or builder</note> and the end <note>the purpose or goal</note>, which do not enter into the constitution.

The **material cause** is the essential principle by which a substance exists secondarily, such as the body in a human being. Indeed, matter is everything from which something is made as if from a subject—just as a statue is made from gold, or a house is made from stones, cement, wood, iron, and tiles. And this is either **proximate**, such as the bricks of a wall, or **remote**, such as the clay from which the wall was fabricated. This division is also accepted in other types of causes.

The **formal cause** is the essential principle through which a substance most greatly consists in its own being,

# ACADEMIC ART CABINET, FIFTH CLASS

and is preserved within it. **Form**, however, is that through which an action chiefly consists, and outside of it no action can exist; it therefore gives being to a thing, not as an efficient cause, but as that by which a thing is what it is. This is either **essential**, such as the form informing matter—like the <term>rational soul</term> <note>The "anima rationalis" was considered the defining essence of a human being in Aristotelian philosophy</note> through which a human exists—or **accidental**, such as a shape existing around something; or **exemplary** (or ideal), such as the patterns or models of a thing.

The **Efficient Cause** is the principle that makes some thing, acting as a natural or artificial agent; or the principle from which motion begins.

The **Final Cause** is the principle for the sake of which something is done. The end, however, is first in intention and last in execution. <note>This refers to the teleological idea that while a goal is the first thing a person thinks of, it is the last thing actually achieved.</note>

The **Accidental Cause** is that which of itself does not imply the necessity of a subsequent effect, such as occasion, fortune, or chance.

### LECTURE VI.
### Concerning the Medium.

1. *What a medium is.* 2. *Species of the medium.* 3. *Related and Opposing concepts.*

<margin>1</margin>
A <term>medium</term> <note>The "middle" or "mean" that connects two things</note> is the reason by which certain extremes are connected to one another in some manner. It is said "in some manner," because it is not required that the conjunction of the medium be always real and actual, but sometimes it is intentional and potential or based on aptitude.

Three species of the medium are posited by <term>Lull</term> <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1316), a philosopher and mystic known for his "Great Art" of logical combinations</note>:
<margin>2</margin>
Namely $\begin{cases} \text{Of Conjunction,} \\ \text{Of Measurement,} \\ \text{Of Extremities.} \end{cases}$

The **medium of conjunction** is that which exists between a subject and a predicate, as when it is said: "Man is an animal." For between "man" and "animal" there are media, namely life and his body, without which he cannot be an animal.

The **medium of measurement** is that which exists through an act existing between the agent and the object of the action, just as "loving" exists between the lover and the beloved.

The **medium of extremities** is that which is concluded—

# ACADEMIC ART OF THE KABBALAH. VOLUME V. CLASS.

between two extremes, just as a line exists between two points.

Under these three modes of mediums, certain other types of mediums can be identified.

The first is the **instrumental**, such as a nail joining two pieces of wood.

The second is that of **participation**, which, being placed between two extremes, draws from the nature of both, such as intermediate colors between white and black.

The third is that of **comprehension**, which contains those things between which it is placed, as a genus <note>A general category in logic</note> exists between two dividing differences.

The fourth is the medium of **negation**, such as a temperate state between hot and cold.

The fifth is that of **transition**, through which motion occurs.

# ACADEMIC CABINET OF THE ART. FIFTH CLASS. LECTURE VII.

### Concerning the End.

*1. What the end is. 2. How it differs from glory. 3. The types of the end. 4. Related and opposing concepts.*

<margin>1</margin> The End <note>Latin: *Finis*; in this context, it refers to both a conclusion and a purpose.</note> is the principle by which all beings are bounded or finished. <margin>2</margin> However, the end differs from Glory, because Glory is considered as an absolute end; whereas the end is considered as a relationship added onto glory.

<margin>3</margin> Lullius <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1316), a Majorcan philosopher and mystic known for his logical system intended to prove Christian truths.</note> proposes three types of ends. The first is the end of privation, which signifies a state of being deprived, such as death which ends life; blindness which terminates sight; and deafness which terminates hearing.

The second is the end of termination, in which a thing is not extended further, and it is the extreme limit of some thing—whether of substance or accident—according to essence, or time, or place, or quality, or quantity, and such things, whether before or after; and to this, the infinite is opposed.

The third is the end of perfection, which is the ultimate end, just as a human being exists in order to multiply their species and so that they may understand and love God, etc.

->THE END.<-

|----------|--------------|---|
| Perfection, | Restlessness, | |
| Completion, | Beginning, | |
| Goal. | Halfway point. | |

<note>Vocabulary: End, Glory, Lullius, death, blindness, deafness, the infinite, Perfection, Completion, Goal, Restlessness, Beginning, Halfway point.</note>

## LECTURE VIII.

### On Superiority. <note>original: "De Maioritate." In this scholastic context, it refers to the state of being greater or higher in rank, essence, or quantity.</note>

*1. Types of superiority. 2. Related and Opposing concepts.*

1 There are three types of superiority to which others can be reduced.
The first is between substance and substance <note>A "substance" in philosophy is something that exists in itself, like a person or an animal, rather than a quality like "red" or "tall."</note>, which is considered according to the greater or lesser perfection of essence; in this way, a human is greater than a Donkey.
The second is between a substance and an accident <note>An "accident" is a property that can change without destroying the subject, such as size or color.</note>, as between a human and his physical size.
The third is between one accident and another accident.

# THE SIXTH CLASS.

### Concerning questions, their rules, and their types.

#### LECTURE I.
#### Concerning questions in general.

*1. What a question is in this context. 2. What a rule is. 3. That questions are the vessels for all things that can be understood. 4. Some questions are essential, others are accidental. 5. There are ten forms of inquiry, under which many other types are contained. 6. That this science is general to all other sciences.*

<margin>1</margin>
In this class, we must treat the figure Q <note>original: "figura Q." In the Lullian system, letters of the alphabet represent specific sets of concepts; Q specifically refers to the table of questions.</note>, which concerns the forms of questions and the rules for answers. Here, a "question" is not called a doubtful proposition, but rather the very sign of inquiry, or the specific term through which we search. We call a "rule," however, the correct method of responding to a question. Through the knowledge of these rules, the human intellect is so directed and illuminated that it is able to respond to all inquiries without any difficulty. It can arrive at a definitive concept of anything unknown, because the logic and method of defining is taken from these rules. They are called "rules" <note>original: "regulae," meaning guides or rulers</note> because they direct the human intellect so that it can define things appropriately. For this reason, Lull <note>original: "Lullius." Referring to Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1316), the Majorcan philosopher who created this system of logic.</note> calls these questions the "vessels for all things that can be understood." These questions are distinguished among themselves—
<margin>2</margin>
<margin>3</margin>
<margin>4</margin>

# Art Cabinet of the Academy: VI Class

These are the general questions of this art <note>original: "huius artis," referring to the Lullian or Kabbalistic Art of reasoning</note> in which all other questions are entwined. For just as all declinable nouns are included within the five declensions, so in their own way are other questions included within these ten; they are reduced to these ten by reason of the universality they possess. # Academic Cabinet. VI. Classis.

to consider: and there are five.

The first is **precaution in the care and consideration of oneself**, which consists in ardent prayer and intent meditation, lest the mind wander, but rather that it remains focused on the task at hand.

The second is **precaution in the care of the persons inquiring**, of whom account must be taken. For if they are serious people, let the response be brief and with fitting dignity; if they are persistent, more words are needed; if they are simple folk, let them be satisfied with a simple answer and reasoning; if they are learned, let the speech take on ornamentation, especially using arguments drawn from authority <note>In this context, "authority" refers to citing established texts, scriptures, or recognized scholars to bolster an argument.</note>.

The third is **precaution in the care and consideration of the question proposed**, which is occupied with these points:
1. That we properly grasp the meaning and circumstances of the questioner.
2. That we first consider if there is any ambiguity in the vocabulary, and point it out.
3. That we distinguish between types of questions and do not confuse them; for he does not respond appropriately who, when asked *why* Christ died <note>original: "quare Christus sit mortuus"</note>, wishes to begin his response with an explanation of *who* Christ is, unless an application is finally made and the cause for which he died is indicated.
4. That we respond to any question either by affirming, by denying, or by making a distinction <note>The "distinction" was a core tool of scholastic logic, allowing a speaker to agree with one part of a statement while rejecting another.</note>.
5. That we concede nothing until we have heard the conclusion.

The fourth is that **account be taken of other circumstances**. For if what is proposed is sufficiently well-known, or self-evident, so that it neither requires proof, or cannot be proven or demonstrated (such as a first principle <term>first principle: a foundational proposition or assumption that cannot be deduced from any other</term>), or if it has already been decided, or if it is va—

# Art of the Academic Cabinet, VI. Class.

If the matter is of little importance, or if it is beyond the grasp of the human mind, it is sufficient to respond with a simple affirmation or negation. Furthermore, consider the causes and occasions why, and the time in which, the inquiry is made: whether someone asks to test the strength of your intellect, so that you may then show with all your might what kind of person you are; or whether only your opinion is desired, for then I believe it is better to state what you think briefly, but appropriately and with reason. But if the matter seems doubtful and probable on both sides, you shall propose both opinions; finally, you shall strengthen the one you choose with reasons and refute the contrary.

<margin>3</margin> There are, however, special rules for responses which are added to individual forms of questions: each of which is indicated by a certain and specific word, as is clear from the figure <note>likely referring to a diagram on a previous or subsequent page</note>, and will be evident from what is to be said.

### Lecture III.
### On the question "Whether" <note>original: "vtrum" - a technical term in scholastic logic used to introduce a question with two possible sides, usually "whether it is or is not."</note>.

> 1. *What is sought through "whether."* 2. *The possibility of the rule for responding to this question and how it is to be known.* 3. *That there are three species of this question.* 4. *Another question joined to this one.*

<margin>1</margin> Through "whether," we ask first if that thing which has been proposed exists, or is happening, or has been, or will be.

Secondly, through the same question, we further inquire about the reasons, species, and rules of all other questions. For if an answer has been given to any question, we investigate again through this question whether it was answered correctly; for example, if it is asked "what is a human?" once that is said, this question emerges again: "is a human that which was just stated?" and similarly in other questions.

<summary>This page outlines the rhetorical and logical strategies for answering academic inquiries, specifically introducing the "Whether" (Utrum) question as a tool for verifying existence and the correctness of definitions.</summary>
<keywords>logic, scholasticism, rhetoric, Utrum, academic debate, methodology, inquiry, definition</keywords>

# Art Cabinet of the Academy: Sixth Class

The rule for responding to this question is *possibility*, which advises that in responding, we should consider whether the affirmation or the negation is possible.

How that possibility is to be recognized, however, you will learn from six rules, of which:

The first is: That which is more consistent must be affirmed, until the contrary is proven, or we ourselves understand the contrary.

Therefore, let us not concede that something is true which is repugnant to the senses or the intellect, with the exception of the articles of faith; for these are indeed not against, but above reason. <note>The author distinguishes between "contrary to reason" (illogical) and "above reason" (divine mystery), a common distinction in Scholastic philosophy.</note>

Second: Whenever the destruction of principles and definitions follows from an affirmation, the negative must be held, and vice versa. For example, if someone asks, *Whether God exists*, the destruction of the following principles follows from a negation: namely, *That nothing can be the cause of itself* <note>original: "Nihil posse esse causā suiipsius"</note>, and *That nature shrinks from a progression into infinity* <note>original: "Naturam abhorrere à progressu in infinitum"</note>. This is because if God does not exist, the world either has no cause for itself, or it is its own cause, but both are absurd. Thus, if another question arises that must be immediately deduced through principles—whether absolute or relative—it is necessary to concede that which is more in harmony with goodness, greatness, difference, concordance, and the other principles. <term>Principles (Principia): the fundamental laws of logic and reality used in the Art of Ramon Llull, such as Goodness and Greatness.</term>

Third: That for which either no demonstration, or only a weak one, has been brought forward by us or others, is not to be conceded.

Fourth: [One responds] not always by simply affirming or denying, but also very often by doubting de- <note>The text cuts off here due to the page break; the catchword "de-" indicates the next word likely begins with "deliberating" or "defining."</note>

<summary>This page outlines the first four logical rules for determining whether to affirm or deny a proposition, emphasizing consistency with reason and the preservation of fundamental philosophical principles. It specifically applies these rules to the existence of God, arguing that a denial would violate the principle of causality.</summary>

<keywords>logic, possibility, articles of faith, reason, causality, existence of God, first principles, demonstration, Scholasticism</keywords>

# ACADEMIC ARTS CABINET: SIXTH CLASS

we must respond: for to doubt rightly is the beginning of wisdom, as Aristotle says in the *Metaphysics*, book 3, chapter 1. <note>original: "rectè dubitare est initium sapientiæ"</note>

Fifth: Disputants ought to agree on certain principles. Therefore, he is foolish and ridiculous who, when disputing with a Jew, brings forward the New Testament <note>The author argues that a debate requires shared foundational texts to be valid</note>. Furthermore, one who denies basic principles is not to be heard; this is especially true regarding that first and most general principle: *It is impossible for the same thing to be and not to be.* <note>The Principle of Non-Contradiction</note> For if this is granted, something would be nothing and vice versa, and thus the entire nature of things would be overturned.

<margin>3</margin> Moreover, according to Raymond <note>Referring to Raymond Lull, a 13th-century philosopher known for his logical "Great Art"</note> this question has three forms: *Affirmation, negation, and doubt*, to which we can add *Possible, Contingent, necessary, and impossible.* Finally, to this question "Whether it is" <note>Latin: "Utrum"</note> is joined the question "if it is," the rule of which is *Entity* <note>Latin: "Entitas," referring to the actual being or existence of a thing</note>.

<margin>4</margin>

->LECTURE IV.<-

->On the question: What?<-

> 1. *By this question, a definition is sought.* 2. *The rule of the response.* 3. *The parts of a definition.* 4. *What a genus is and its discovery.* 5. *The fourfold method of finding a difference.* 6. *The types of this question, namely "What."*

<margin>1</margin> Having gained the knowledge that a thing exists, it is rightly asked *What it is*. This question is the instrument by which a definition is sought; therefore, the rule of the response is called *Quiddity* <note>From the Latin "quid" (what); the "what-ness" or essential nature of a thing</note>, or definition, by which it is clearly explained what the thing is; or rather, by which the Quiddity—that is, the essence of the subject—is expressed.

# ART OF THE ACADEMIC CABINET: SIXTH CLASS

<margin>3</margin> Truly, there are two parts to a definition: the Genus <note>The general category or family to which a thing belongs</note> and the Difference <note>The specific characteristic that distinguishes a thing from others in its category</note>. The Genus is the symbol of the matter, while the Difference is the symbol of the form; the latter serves to divide the superior genus and to constitute the inferior species. Here, there is a need for knowledge of both parts.

<margin>4</margin> The Genus is the part of the definition that designates the common part of the subject; or, the Genus is a term common to many, explaining the cause by which the subject is a being. For example, in this proposition: *An Angel is a spirit*. Here, *Spirit* is the cause by which an Angel is a being, or something that exists. Truly, the discovery of the genus depends on the rotation of the Circles <note>Likely referring to Lullian circles or mnemonic diagrams used in Renaissance logic to find connections between concepts</note>; by this rotation, one seeks the term to which the subject belongs, concerning which the following rules apply.

The First Rule: That term under which a subject is placed is either a genus or a species; it is a genus if it is only a part of the essence common to many; it is a species, however, if it is the whole essence. For example, Aristotle belongs to the term E of circle S; for that reason, I say *Aristotle is a man*. Likewise, Christ belongs partly to term B of circle S, wherefore I say *Christ is God*; and partly to term E of the same circle, wherefore I say *Christ is man*. Under the consideration of both terms, Christ is called <term>God-man</term> <note>original: "θεάνθρωπος" (theanthropos)</note> or <term>Man-God</term> <note>original: "ἀνθρωπόθεος" (anthropotheos)</note>, that is, God and man.

The Second Rule: The terms of universal subjects are to be distributed by species and divided by parts, and you will find the nearest genus. For example, one action of the soul is the Intellect, another is the Will—

# ACADEMIC ART OF THE CABINET: SIXTH CLASS

<margin>of reality</margin> under which knowledge is contained; I say, therefore, that knowledge is an action of the Intellect, or of the intelligent soul.

Third: We often use the terms of predicates <note>In logic, a predicate is what is affirmed or denied about the subject of a proposition.</note> in place of "genera" <note>A "genus" is a general class or category under which species are grouped.</note>. For example, if the Human Intellect is to be defined, it can be defined using various terms from Circle A. First, from term B, thus: **The Intellect is the goodness of human nature.** Second, from term E: It is the faculty of the rational soul. Third, from term K: It is the glory and perfection of the image of God in man. It can also be defined from various terms in Circle T, such as from term B: **The Intellect is that by which man differs essentially from the beasts;** and from term E: **It is the principle of knowledge, etc.**

Fourth: Often the epithets of the circles, or the common terms into which the circles are divided, are taken in place of genera. For example, if "quantity" is to be defined, I say it is an "accident" <note>In Aristotelian philosophy, an "accident" is an attribute that does not belong to the essence of a thing, such as its color or size.</note>, specifically because it is located in the circle of accidents. Likewise, God is a "substance," because He is contained in Circle S—that is, the circle of subjects or substances.

Fifth: Genera can be drawn from Circle Q using the same method. Finally, the genus of any thing can be brought forth from the terms of Circle T; for this reason, these terms are called "transcendent predicates," for which this single rule must be held:

If a subject is to be defined, one of these terms will be the genus, *such as: the hand is the "beginning" <note>original: "principium"</note> by which man touches; man is the "originated thing" <note>original: "principiatum"</note> or the work of God; Omnipotence is the "difference" by which God is distinguished from created things; or, it is the "superiority" of God; it is the "Principle of acting" in God, etc.*

# Academic Cabinet of Arts, Class VI.

Thus the first part of a definition, namely the **genus**, is to be recognized and found. Now, observe the method for recognizing and finding the **difference**, which is the other part of a definition and its perfection; for it makes a thing be "this specific thing" and "such a being," and it establishes the thing in a certain degree of being <note>original: "entitatis gradu" — the ontological level or status of an object</note>.

The difference is therefore found by three questions, acting as principles:

Namely: { Of Whom/What, What Kind, Why.

By the question ***Of Whom/What*** <note>Latin: "Cuius"</note>, we investigate either the **possessor** or the **thing possessed**. By the word "possessor," we understand everything to which the verb "to have" can be attributed, such as related things <term>relata: things that exist only in relation to something else, like 'father' and 'son'</term> and things considered relatively: thus a Subject "has" an accident <term>accidens: a non-essential quality, like the color of a shirt</term>. The "thing possessed," however, is that which is held or is inherent: thus a part is held by the whole. Now, therefore, if a **Master** is to be defined, he will be said to be one who "has a servant"; if a **Magistrate**, he is one who has subjects; if **Quality**, it will be said to be an accident of its subject, through which that subject is disposed or affected in this or that manner.

Moreover, the discovery of both the possessor and the thing possessed is a process of reasoning and a "conversion of circles" <note>This likely refers to a mnemonic or diagrammatic system of logic, possibly Lullian circles or a similar pedagogical tool used in the 17th century</note> and a consideration of the terms within them, which is done in this manner:

First: let it be weighed to which circle the matter regarding the question *Of Whom/What* refers. For once that is found, the term under which it is contained—or some other term either superior or inferior to it—will be the possessor or the thing possessed.

Second: if in the circle of subjects the question...

# ACADEMIC ART OF THE KABBALAH: SIXTH CLASS

If the definition of a thing is not fully grasped, the remaining circles must be consulted. For example, in the third rule regarding the discovery of a genus <note>The general category to which a thing belongs</note>, we defined the **Understanding** as a "faculty." This definition is incomplete, for the "difference" <note>The specific characteristic that distinguishes one thing from others in the same category</note> is missing. To find this difference, I ask what thing this faculty belongs to—that is, I seek its possessor. First, I look in the specific circle of "Subjects" and find **E**, under which the Understanding is contained. I say, therefore: *The Understanding is a faculty of man*, and thus it differs from the Understanding of God or of an Angel. Again, I ask of which part it is a faculty; here it is necessary to make a partition <note>A logical division of a whole into its parts</note> and to apply judgment to those parts, so that you may rightly perceive which one is worthy of the title of "possessor." Now, the parts of man are the body and the soul. It cannot be said of the body; therefore, I say that the Understanding is a faculty of the rational soul. But the Will is also a faculty of the soul; therefore, a final difference must be added: the Understanding is the *cognitive* faculty of the soul, while the Will is the *elective* <note>The power of choosing or deciding</note> faculty.

In the question of "What kind?" the discovery of the difference is very easy; for by changing the substantive nouns into adjectives, the terms of the differences are readily available. All things hidden in the individual circles—except for the last circle, **Q**—are substantives. Therefore, a traversal of the circles is necessary here as well. For example, it was said above that the Understanding of man is a faculty; now I ask, what kind of faculty is it? I answer from the letters **b, c, d, e, f** of circle **S**: divine, angelic and spiritual, celestial, rational, or imaginative. Likewise, from the letters **b, c, d, e, f, g** of circle **A**: good, finite or infinite, perpetual, actual, cognitive, etc. The same traversal occurs—

# Art Cabinet of the Academy: Class VI

...proceeding through each of the terms of Circle T. <note>Circle T is a combinatorial tool in Ramon Llull's "Great Art" used to relate concepts like Difference, Agreement, and Opposition.</note>

The question of "Why" <note>original: "quare"</note> finds the difference by first inquiring about the subject—not why a being exists, but why it is called by that specific name.

Finally, you will observe that it is not unfitting to seek the genera and differences of things from the series of the Predicaments <note>The "Predicaments" refer to Aristotle's ten Categories of being, such as Substance, Quality, and Quantity.</note>. Therefore, if you wish to define a human being, it is your task to observe in which predicament they reside, so that you may borrow the genus and difference from there. And these are the points concerning the discovery of the genus and the difference, which are the parts of the definition sought by the question "What?" <note>original: "quid"</note>.

<margin>6</margin> Furthermore, according to Raymond <note>Referring to Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1316), the Majorcan philosopher and mystic.</note>, there are four species of this question, "What?":
Namely:
{ What is it in itself?
{ What is it in another?
{ What does it have in itself?
{ What does it have in another?

To the question **"What is it in itself?"** the answer is essence and being <note>original: "essentia & esse"</note>: or whatever is denoted through an essential definition. This includes not only the essential definition but also any other that identifies the thing. For this reason, "What" encompasses the explanation of the name of the thing, whether through interpretation, the meaning of the word, or through a figure of speech—as if someone were to call old age the "sunset of life"—or through allegory, metaphor, or paraphrase, all of which can enter into a definition.

To the question **"What is it in another?"** the answer is given through relative quiddity <note><term>Quiddity: the "what-ness" or essential nature of a thing.</term></note>. For example, if it is a substantial or accidental form in matter, a part in a whole, a whole in its parts, a lower term (according to predication) in its higher term, or a potentiality in a power, or a thing located...

It emphasizes that definitions can be literal, metaphorical, or based on the relationship between a part and its whole.</summary>

->Artistic Cabinet of the Academy. VI. Class.<-

in place, the cause in the effect and vice versa, the thing originated in its own principle, and other such things.

To the question (*What does it have in itself?*), by which question one asks what a certain thing possesses naturally and essentially, one must respond through those things without which the thing cannot exist—such as through the **-ing, -er, and -able** <note>original: "ivvm, are, & bile." These are the Latin suffixes used in the Art of Ramon Llull to denote the active power, the action, and the capacity to be acted upon (e.g., Bonificativum, Bonificare, Bonificabile).</note>. In this way, goodness and other principles possess several co-essential or connatural elements. This method of defining is very safe since it is assigned through the innermost parts of the thing, and it is valid for constructing necessary demonstrations. Understand, however, that **-ing, -er, and -able** are not considered insofar as they regard something particular (because that is accidental), but as these three relate indifferently to all things, which is essential.

Not only is it asked through the question (*What does the thing have in itself?*) what a thing has in itself naturally and essentially, but also in several other ways: for beings are said to have something in themselves:

->Or<-
*   Eminently <note>Possessing a quality in a higher, more perfect form, as a cause contains the perfections of its effect.</note>,
*   Virtually in real being,
*   Virtually in known being,
*   Potentially,
*   Actually,
*   Identically,
*   Unitively,
*   Immensively <note>In an immeasurable or boundless manner.</note>,
*   Through dominion,
*   Through influence,
*   Through mixture,
*   By way of place, whether common or proper,
*   By way of a perfecting agent, whether substantially or accidentally.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the four primary questions used in the Lullian art of inquiry, specifically focusing on the relationship between a subject and its environment or attributes.</meta>

->ACADEMIC CABINET OF ARTS. CLASS VI.<-

To the question (What does one have in another?) one must respond through those things which signify action or passion <note>In this context, "passion" refers to the philosophical concept of being acted upon or receiving an impression</note>, just as the Intellect is in an object by understanding it: it is in the intelligible form because it is acted upon by receiving it. God is in all creatures through wisdom, power, and essence: truly, created things are found in Him, because they are preserved and directed by Him. Here is an example of these four types.

What is man in himself? A rational, animated substance.

What is man in another? In Italy, an Italian; in wisdom, a wise man; in a place, one who is situated.

What does man have in himself? Humanity, Intellect, will, sense, etc.

What does man have in another? In demonstrable things, knowledge; in sensible things, sense and imagination; in possessions, lordship; in God, hope.

Besides these four types of the question "What," there is another personal "what" which can be called "Who," inquiring about the person or the underlying subject <note>original: "supposito," a technical term in scholasticism for an individual substance</note>, such as: Who created the world? We use this both in the direct case and the oblique case <note>referring to grammatical cases like nominative versus accusative or ablative</note> through a certain method reducible to the direct, such as: *Who accuses you?* or *By whom are you accused?* or, *From whom are you accused?* The rule for this question, "Who," is personality.

<meta>This page continues the systematic exploration of the "Nine Questions" used in the Art of Ramon Llull, specifically focusing on the nuances of the question "From what?" and introducing the question "Why?"</meta>

# Art of the Academic Cabinet, Class VI.

The first is *From what* in an original or primitive sense, which can be called *From whom*, the rule of which is derivation or Originality. To this question, there are three possible answers:

> Either
> { Not from anyone, nor out of anything, such as God.
> { From someone, but not out of anything, such as an Angel and every primitive form. <note>In this philosophical context, "primitive form" refers to the essential soul or spark that does not come from physical matter.</note>
> { From someone and out of something, such as physical bodies.

The second is (*From what*) in a material sense, the rule of which is materiality; for example, if it is asked, *From what* was man made? The answer is: from the dust of the earth.

The third is (*From what*) in a possessive sense, which asks about possession, lordship, regard, or relation. The rule for answering this is drawn from every type of relationship. This question can be noted by the sign *Whose*; for example, if it is said, *Whose father is he? Whose goodness is it?* and things of this sort.

### LECTURE VI.

### Concerning the question "Why."

*1. The question "Why" follows all the aforementioned questions. 2. The practice of the rule for this question. 3. This question is the key to knowledge.*

<margin>1</margin>
The question *Why* follows all the previously mentioned questions in an orderly fashion; for after them, we ask: *Why is it? Why is it this? Why is it from this? Why is it of this?*

<margin>2</margin>
The rule which determines the method of answering this question is *Causality*, which explains the thing to be described through its causes. The practice of this rule will become clear by an example. Let this, therefore, be the ques-

<meta>This page continues the theological examination of Christ's roles, specifically focusing on the office of the "Good Shepherd" using Aristotelian logic.</meta>

->### Academic Cabinet of Arts, Class VI.<-

#### Page 163

The question: **Why is Christ a shepherd, and why is he called one?** The answer is given:

**First**, through the <term>efficient cause</term> <note>The primary source or agent that brings something into being.</note> of this shepherd’s office, which is God’s preordination, appointment, and anointing for this specific type of duty.

**Second**, through the <term>impelling and antecedent cause</term> <note>The motivating factor that comes before the action.</note> which is the good pleasure and free will of God.

**Third**, through the <term>material cause</term> <note>The "matter" or substance that makes up the office.</note>, which is threefold:

The first response is taken from the matter *out of which* that shepherd’s office consists—namely, governing, nourishing, and defending—because he governs, nourishes, and defends us. The second is taken from the matter *in which* the subject of this office must be: the strongest, wisest, and most kind of all. No one, however, is mightier, wiser, or kinder than Christ. The third response is taken from the matter *concerning which* the objects of this shepherd’s office are found: namely, with wolves on the right and <term>mercenaries</term> <note>Hired hands who care nothing for the sheep; a reference to John 10.</note> on the left. Against the former, Christ protects his sheep; against the latter, he offers comfort.

**Fourth**, the answer is given through the <term>formal cause</term> <note>The essential nature or "form" that makes a thing what it is.</note>, because he nourishes with the spiritual food of his own flesh, and rules by his Word and Spirit.

**Fifth**, the answer is given through the <term>final cause</term> <note>The ultimate purpose or goal.</note>, namely so that Christ may be known and celebrated, as in Ezekiel 34: *And they shall know that I am the Lord* <note>original: "Et scient quia ego Dominus"</note>; and so that the sheep may be preserved and be without fear, etc.

<margin>3</margin> And because everyone understands that saying of Aristotle to be true—*To know is to understand a thing through its cause* <note>original: "Scire, esse rem per causam cognoscere"</note>—it must be established that this question is the key by which the doors of knowledge are opened.

<summary>This page provides a systematic theological defense of Christ's title as "Shepherd" by applying Aristotle's four causes (efficient, material, formal, and final) to his ministry. It concludes that true knowledge of Christ's role requires understanding these underlying divine purposes.</summary>

<keywords>Christology, Good Shepherd, Aristotelian logic, Four Causes, Ezekiel 34, Divine Providence, Pastoral Care</keywords>

### Lecture VII.

## Concerning the question of "How Much."

->1. *The breadth of this question.* 2. *The species of the same.* 3. *The threefold nature of quantity.*<-

<margin>1</margin> The fifth question is *how much* <note>original: "quantum"</note>, which inquires about quantity, whether absolute or comparative, continuous or discrete. Its general rule is quantity—not only of a physical thing, but accepted so generally that it is able to comprehend any formality whatsoever, such as the finite and the infinite. For example: how great is Christ? He is infinite according to his divinity. How great is the faith of Christians? It is great, as seen in the Canaanite woman <note>A reference to Matthew 15:28, where Jesus commends the "great faith" of a non-Jewish woman</note>.

<margin>2</margin> And through this question of quantity, we inquire in various ways according to the variety of the types of quantity. For one inquires:
First, concerning magnitude according to length, width, and depth; then the question is made through *how much* <note>original: "quantum"</note>, the rule of which is *quantity*.
Second, one inquires concerning place, or time, or multitude; this is done through the sign *how many* <note>original: "quot"</note>, the rule of which is *number* <note>original: "quotitas," a technical term for "how-many-ness"</note>. This is done in different ways: namely, either according to order, as when we ask *which one in the series?* <note>original: "quotus"</note>; or according to multiplication, as when we ask *how many fold?* <note>original: "quotuplex"</note>; or according to distribution, as when we ask *how many times?* <note>original: "quoties"</note>. Furthermore, quantity is threefold: of *Essence*, of *Mass* <note>physical bulk</note>, and of *Virtue* <note>inherent power or efficacy</note>.

<summary>This lecture defines the philosophical category of Quantity, explaining how it applies to both physical dimensions and abstract concepts like divinity and faith. It distinguishes between various ways of asking "how much" or "how many" regarding size, order, and frequency.</summary>
<keywords>Quantity, Logic, Christology, Canaanite woman, Magnitude, Number, Essence, Mass, Virtue</keywords>

# ACADEMIC ART OF THE CABINET. SIXTH CLASS.

### LECTURE VIII.

### Concerning the question: Of what kind? <note>original: "De quæstione Quale." This refers to the Aristotelian and Lullian category of Quality.</note>

*1. The breadth of this question. 2. The common rule for answering this question.*

<margin>1</margin>The rule called Quality is applied to this question. Therefore, because there are four types of quality, when it is asked what kind of thing something is, the answer will be drawn from these four types. For example, if it is asked what kind of shepherd Christ is, one will answer according to his **disposition** <note>original: "habitus," referring to an acquired or ingrained character trait</note> that he is faithful, truthful, and prudent. According to his **natural powers or incapacities**, the answer will be that he is a shepherd in whom heroic impulses against wolves are eminent, and in whom affection toward the sheep is visible. According to **sensible qualities** <note>original: "passibiles qualitates," qualities that can be perceived by the senses or undergo change</note>, the answer will be that he is audible to his sheep, and will finally be visible and tangible to them, etc.

<margin>2</margin>Furthermore, the common rule for answering this question is the circular discourse, which is usually performed by changing abstract terms into concrete ones. For instance, when it is asked, "What kind of shepherd is Christ?" the answer is drawn from **Circle S** <note>In the Lullian system, Circle S typically represents the soul and its powers</note>: divine, ordained by God, heavenly, descending from heaven, spiritual, and human. From **Circle V** <note>Circle V usually pertains to the virtues and vices</note>: just, courageous, etc. From **Circle A** <note>Circle A represents the attributes of God, such as Goodness and Greatness</note>: Good, eternal, omnipotent. From **Circle T** <note>Circle T represents relational principles like difference, identity, and contrariety</note>: other than and different from the mercenaries, the same as God the Father, and opposed or contrary to the wolves.

<summary>This page explains how to answer the question "Of what kind?" (Quality) regarding Christ as the Good Shepherd using the four types of quality and the symbolic circles of the Lullian Art. It demonstrates how to apply abstract theological concepts to concrete descriptions of character and nature.</summary>

<keywords>Quality, Christ, Shepherd, Habit, Power, Sensible Qualities, Circle S, Circle V, Circle A, Circle T, Ramon Llull, Logic</keywords>

# ACADEMIC CABINET OF ARTS. CLASS VI.

### LECTURE IX.

### On the question "When."

*1. The breadth of this question. 2. Distinctions of time.*

<margin>1</margin> The rule of this question, adapted for providing an answer, is **Temporality** <note>Latin: *Temporalitas*; the quality of existing within time</note>, which advises that this question must be answered using words signifying time, either expressly or by consequence. "Expressly" means either by naming the whole—such as *always*, *perpetually*, *from eternity*, *unto eternity*—or a part, such as *a year*, *summer*, *winter*, or *an hour*. "By consequence," however, refers to what is understood from named circumstances; for example: "this happened under King Alexander the Great." For instance, if asked: "When did Christ suffer?" the answer is: "Always, throughout the whole course of his life." "When is the vintage?" the answer is: "In the time of autumn." "When was Christ born?" "In the time of Augustus, when the whole world was being taxed." But you will note here the distinctions of time: some words belong to the present, such as *today* or *now*; others to the past, such as *yesterday*, *a while ago*, or *long ago*; others to the future, such as *tomorrow*. To this category also belong common adverbs of various times, such as *at some time*, *sometimes*, or *once upon a time*.

<margin>2</margin>

# Art of the Academic Kabbalah: Sixth Class

<note>The "Sixth Class" likely refers to a specific division in the author's encyclopedic system of knowledge.</note>

...it is necessary to designate the place, or the subject, in which the matter under discussion exists when one asks the question: **Where is it?** <note>original: "Vbinam sit"</note>

<margin>2</margin> This question is not only the most general of all, but it is also "transcendent" <note>In medieval logic, a transcendent term is one that rises above and applies to all specific categories of being.</note>, for it is understood in several ways:

First, **physically**, or according to local differences: above, below, before, behind, right, left, inside, outside; or according to the named types of places, such as in heaven, on earth, in the air, and so forth.

Second, **civilly**: In the forum, in the Temple, in the Senate.

Third, **mathematically**: In such a number, in such a measure, weight, or degree.

Fourth, **logically**: In the genus, in the species.

Fifth, **metaphysically**: In goodness, wisdom, or harmony.

Finally, this question is so general that it embraces all the ways in which we can imagine or say that something is "within" another. Thus, the word is said to be in the mind, the mind in the word, the plant in the seed, the line in the point, and time in eternity.

From this, one can see that this Art <note>The "Art" refers to the author's specific method of reasoning or mnemonic system.</note> is more general than the common "organon" <term>Organon: The traditional collection of Aristotle's logical works used as a tool for scientific reasoning.</term> of the sciences, and even more general than metaphysics or the arts themselves; for it encompasses things that can be known, things that can be believed, things that can be done—whatever is practical, whatever is mechanical, and whatever is speculative. To this question, however, must be added the question **From where?** <note>original: "Vnde"</note> by which the principle or source of motion is sought.

<summary>This page analyzes the universal question "Where?" across physical, civil, mathematical, logical, and metaphysical contexts, arguing that the author's system is more comprehensive than traditional Aristotelian logic. It concludes by introducing the next fundamental inquiry regarding the "source" or "origin" of things.</summary>

<keywords>location, categories of being, metaphysics, logic, universal art, Aristotelian organon, spatial relations, theory of knowledge</keywords>

# Art Cabinet of the Academy. VI. Class.

### Lecture XI.
### On the questions "In what manner" and "With what."
*1. The subject matter of this question. 2. To this is added the question, "With what."*

1. The ninth question is *In what manner* <note>original: "Quomodo"</note>, the rule of which is modality. This shows that the order and the parts of that order must be explained whenever a question arises concerning actions or modes of acting. For in this question, one asks what was done first, what second, what was done together, and what was done separately; the answer is given by means of the gerund <note>A grammatical form in Latin used to express the means or manner of an action, often translated as "by doing" or "in doing."</note>. We know these things either through experience, which requires us to have been present at the action, or through instruction, which occurs either by the living voice or in writing.

2. To this question is added the question *With what* <note>original: "Cum quo"</note>, the rule of which is instrumentality. For this question asks about the instruments and means which things use in their operations. These may be essential, just as the rational soul is the instrument by which a human understands and wills; or they may be passions or properties, such as gravity in iron and lightness in fire; or they may be common accidents <note>In Aristotelian logic, "accidents" are qualities that a thing possesses but which are not essential to its nature, such as being tall or sitting down.</note> according to which substances operate, such as the nine categories of Accidents; or moral accidents, under which all the virtues are contained; or finally, the physical bodies which craftsmen use in various arts.

You have at last, Listener, the terms most necessary for this art, through which the discourse of the entire art can be conducted. We are able, however, to multiply these and take up other external terms through which we may discourse in the same way. Let it be so, then.

<meta>catchword: Let it be so, then.</meta>

<summary>This lecture defines the logical categories of "Manner" (modality) and "Means" (instrumentality), explaining how to analyze the order of actions and the tools—whether spiritual, physical, or moral—used to perform them.</summary>
<keywords>modality, instrumentality, logic, rational soul, Aristotelian accidents, methodology, pedagogy</keywords>

# THE SEVENTH CLASS.

### Concerning the multiplication of the terms of the Art, the investigation of external matters, the mixing and multiplication of figures, the discovery of propositions, and their defense and the formation of arguments.

-> LECTURE I. <-

*1. The method of multiplying subjects. 2. Examples of this multiplication.*

<margin>1</margin> We shall multiply the types of Subjects in this way: either by seeking a division under a genus <note>A "genus" is a general category in Aristotelian logic, which is then divided into specific "species."</note>, or by assigning various attributes to the single subject under discussion through a substantial or accidental combination, thereby making the subject complex in various ways. Therefore, we must frequently reduce the things themselves from general categories to species—whether essential or accidental—and often from species back to categories, from subjects to properties, and from parts to the whole and vice versa; in this manner, a great abundance of material will occur to the speaker.

Therefore, according to the present artifice, the principal subject should be placed in the center, and around it on the circumference—divided by the number of letters—should be placed those things which are essentially or

By a similar logic, if a speech is to be delivered in the demonstrative genre <note>The "demonstrative" or epideictic genre of rhetoric is used for praise or blame.</note>—for instance, in praising a person—let that person be placed as the subject in the center. Around these, we further place locations, times, and occasions, giving each of these specific determinations so that the order remains certain and easy to follow.

Likewise, in the deliberative genre <note>The "deliberative" genre is used for political or legal persuasion regarding future actions.</note>, let the type of action be placed in the center. Around this, circumscribe the good, the bad, the useful, the easy, the difficult, the possible, the impossible, the advantageous, the disadvantageous, as well as laws, decrees, statutes, privileges, customs, opinions, etc. Proceed with all of these through each of the principles, predicates, relations, questions, and rules, as it shall seem easiest and most convenient for the intended purpose.

# Academic Cabinet of the Kabbalah, Class VII.

### Lecture III.

->On the multiplication of relative predicates.<-

1. *The multiple ways of multiplying predicates of this kind.*

<margin>1</margin> From what has been said previously regarding the multiplication of subjects and absolute predicates <note>Absolute predicates refer to inherent qualities like Goodness or Greatness, as opposed to relative ones like Difference.</note>, you will be able to derive for yourself many ways by which you may multiply relative predicates: besides those, the specific methods for multiplying these must be explained.

These are therefore multiplied in two ways, namely in their own figure and in a foreign figure <note>In this system, a "figure" refers to a visual diagram or a specific set of categories used to organize thought.</note>. In their own figure, they are multiplied in two ways: first, by deriving one relative predicate through other relatives, as if we were to say the "difference of a difference," the "difference of a Concordance," and so on through the letters B to K <note>The author uses letters as algebraic placeholders for philosophical concepts, a hallmark of Lullian logic.</note>. Also by naming them differently, namely by saying "differing difference," "opposing difference," or "originating difference." And just as you have derived this for B (that is, Difference), so you will be able to derive it for C and the other elements. Secondly, by ascending from particulars to universals and vice versa. In a foreign figure, however, it is also done in two ways: first, by the application of this figure to the first figure—namely that of subjects; an application, I say, which is sometimes general, as when a difference or concordance is considered between God and man, and sometimes particular, as when it is considered between this Angel and

this man, this Heaven and that one, this and that virtue. Secondly, through the deduction of the terms of this Figure T <note>Figure T in this system represents relational concepts like Difference, Agreement, and Opposition</note> through the general terms of Figure A <note>Figure A represents absolute divine attributes or "Dignities"</note>; that is, of the absolute predicates. For example, when B (which represents Difference) is placed under B (which represents Goodness), it signifies the difference between one goodness and another goodness. Or, if placed under C, it signifies the difference between greatness and greatness, and so on through to K. Next, by placing the angle B of Figure T between B and C of Figure A, it signifies the difference between goodness and greatness; then between C and D, that is, between greatness and duration, and so on until the midpoint between I and K.

Finally, by placing angle B of the same Figure T between B and D of Figure A, one sees the difference between goodness and duration; then between C and E, that is, between greatness and power, and so on. Furthermore, what we say regarding angle B should be understood as applying to all other angles of the Triangles.

### LECTURE V.

### On the investigation of extraneous terms.

->*1. Which terms are extraneous. 2. Their discovery.*<-

<margin>1</margin> In this lecture, it must be taught how extraneous terms are to be received. Moreover, extraneous terms <term>extraneous terms: concepts or vocabulary words not explicitly listed in the primary symbolic alphabet of this Art</term> are any which are not openly noted in this art: a manifold abundance of these emerges.

First, from the multiplication of principles.

Second, through opposition to the terms expressed in the art.

Third, from similarity or proximity to the same, as is evident from the individual terms to which we have subjoined related and repugnant <note>meaning contradictory or opposing</note> things.

Fourth, from the proper and common principles of any thing, action, business, science, or art.

### Lecture VI.

#### On the mixture of figures.

*1. How this mixture is performed. 2. That it is done in three ways, and that in one or various figures. 3. The types of this mixture along with their conditions.*

<margin>1</margin>
This mixture <note>Latin: *mixtio*; in Lullian logic, this refers to the systematic combination of different categories or "principles" to explore all possible relationships between them.</note> is performed by leading individual principles <term>principles: the fundamental attributes of God or reality, such as Goodness or Greatness</term> with other principles: for example, let "goodness" be led through the principles.

Goodness by itself is the reason for a good being to act well or to produce; however, because goodness is great through **greatness**, it is therefore a double reason for a good being to produce a great good; and because it is lasting through **duration**, it is a triple reason for that same good being to produce a lasting good; and so it is with the other absolute principles with which it can be mixed in the same way.

Goodness through **difference** is clear and not confused, and thus it is the reason for a good being to act as a good clearly distinct from itself. The same goodness through **concordance** acts as a good similar to itself; through **contrariety** it is opposed to malice; through **beginning** <note>original: *principium*; here used in the sense of a starting point or origin</note> it has the nature of a beginning; through the **middle** the nature of a middle; through the **end** the nature of an end; through **majority** the nature of majority; through equal—

# Art of the Academic Cabinet, VII. Class.

<note>The text discusses how the divine attributes or "principles" interact through different logical relations.</note> through equality, the reason of equality; through minority, the reason of minority; these are the manifold reasons by which goodness performs a manifold good. Indeed, through the **beginning**, it performs a good having a cause or origin; through the **middle**, it performs a good with a medium; through the **end**, it performs a perfect good; through **majority**, a greater good; through **equality**, a good equal to another; through **minority**, a good less than another. All these are spoken of respectively or comparatively, and so it is with other principles derived through others with which they can be manifoldly combined. We exclude, however, contrariety and minority <note>Llull often excludes "minority" and "contrariety" when discussing the absolute nature of God, as God contains no deficiency or opposition.</note> with which the principles cannot possess a multitude.

This kind of mixture occurs in three ways:

| | |
| :--- | :--- |
| Either | { With terms explicitly stated in the Art among themselves, |
| | { With external terms among themselves, |
| | { With explicit terms combined with external terms. |

And all these occur in various ways: either in one figure only, as if it were said in **Figure S**: *God is man*;
In **Figure A**: *Goodness is great*;
In **Figure T**: *God is the beginning-maker*;
In **Figure Q**: *The Angel is from God*;
Or in different figures, either double or manifold, as in **Figures S. A.**: *God is good*;
In **S. T.**: *God is the beginning*;
In **A. T.**: *Goodness is concordant*.

And in this way, a great variety of mixture will emerge when, with each letter of the **fixed circle**, all the letters in the **mobile circle** are varied. <note>This refers to Llull’s "combinatorial wheels," paper instruments where rotating circles of letters (representing concepts like Goodness, Greatness, etc.) were used to generate all possible philosophical arguments.</note> Where it must be noted in this double—

# Academic Cabinet of the Art of the Kabbalah. VII. Class.

<note>The header refers to the classification of this work within a specific academic collection or library system.</note>

...whether by a single or multiple figure, one of the Circles is often doubled or multiplied. For example, in the combination of **S** and **A**, by doubling circle **S**, as if one were to say: *God is better than an Angel*; or *An Angel is better than a human*. In the combination of **S** and **T**: *God differs from a human*. In **A** and **T**: *Goodness differs from eternity*; or by doubling circle **T**, saying: *Concordance agrees more with truth than difference does*.

<note>In Llull's system, letters represent fundamental concepts: A usually represents the attributes of God (Goodness, Greatness, etc.), S represents the powers of the soul, and T represents relational principles (Difference, Concordance, etc.).</note>

In a similar way, one must proceed with external terms; for we can take an external subject and discourse with it through these figures—whether the Circles are single, double, or multiple. We can act similarly with external predicates and relations, so that one circle always remains immobile as a base, to which all other mobile circles may be applied in various ways.

There are, moreover, common types of this mixing:

<margin>3</margin>

| | |
|---|---|
| Eight. | { **SS**. <note>The Soul acting upon itself.</note> |
| | { **AA**. <note>Divine attributes acting upon themselves.</note> |
| | { **TT**. <note>Relational principles acting upon themselves.</note> |
| | { **QQ**. <note>Questions or rules acting upon themselves.</note> |
| | { **AT**. |
| | { **AQ**. |
| | { **TQ**. |
| | { **ATQ**. |

However, Raymond <note>referring to Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1316), the Majorcan philosopher and mystic</note> in his Art posits only four types of mixing, which he calls the four general figures.

The first is Figure **A** applied to itself, as if one were to say: *Goodness is great*.

# Art Cabinet of the Academy: Class VII

The second is Figure T, likewise applied to itself, as if one were to say: *Difference is a beginning principle*. <note>Figure T in the Lullian Art represents relational principles like Difference, Concordance, and Contrariety.</note>

The third consists of the first and second combined, such as: *Goodness is different*.

The fourth is composed of all three previous ones joined together.

The first figure, namely **A.**, is concerned with the First Being <note>original: "primum ens"</note> in whom the subject and the predicate are interchangeable in the identity of their underlying nature and number. This First Being is God, to whom the principles of this figure are applied primarily and interchangeably; thereafter, they are applied to all other subjects according to their proportional share.

Furthermore, through this figure, we investigate the natural conjunction, arrangement, and proportion between the subject and the predicate, so that a "middle term" <term>middle term: the linking concept in a logical syllogism</term> can be found to reach a conclusion. Through this same figure, we investigate which things are interchangeable and which are not, as well as which are "proper passions" <note>essential characteristics</note> and which are merely "appropriated" <note>characteristics assigned by context rather than essence</note>.

The mixing of this figure occurs in three ways:
First, in the abstract, such as: *Goodness is greatness*.
Second, in the concrete, such as: *That which is good is great*.
Third, in the concrete and abstract combined, such as: *Goodness is great*.

And from this figure, eighty-one propositions are drawn out according to every mode of predication, by drawing one principle together both with itself—that is, with its own definition—and with another.

Finally, in this figure, the greatest care must be taken so that the interchangeability and proportion which exists between

->Artistic Cabinet of the Academy, VII. Class.<-
<margin>Signature: M iij</margin>

one principle and another is destroyed; just as duration is spoken of in terms of eternity, so let magnitude be spoken of in terms of infinity, and power in terms of omnipotence. <note>The author is explaining how the divine attributes (principles) relate to one another through absolute scales.</note>

The second figure is **T.**, namely the figure of triangles, which investigates desired objects. Through this figure, the Intellect <note>Latin: *Intellectus*; refers to the human capacity for rational understanding</note> begins to acquire knowledge by moving through it in a circle, in a line, both straight and oblique, changing angles with angles and terms with terms, both generally and specifically. It creates as many propositions in its own way as the first figure does.

The condition of this figure is that one triangle should assist another, and one should prove the other.

The third figure, which we have said consists of the first and second figures—namely **A.** and **T.**—mixes all principles with all others, so that the Intellect may know any principle through all the other principles. It finds the "middle term" <note>A logical term used to connect the subject and predicate in a syllogism</note> with which the subject and predicate are joined, so that it may find many reasons to prove and declare any proposition or conclusion.

This figure contains thirty-six chambers (in this art, "chambers" <note>Latin: *camerae*; the cells or boxes in a combinatory grid</note> are what we call any combinations or complications of letters or terms). Each of these chambers embraces two letters, and each letter stands for a subject and a predicate from each of the two preceding figures. The table for this figure follows below.

# Art Cabinet of the Academy: Seventh Class

->M iiij<-

Now, in any given chamber of the aforementioned Third Figure, there are twelve propositions, as seen in chamber **bc**:

**Goodness** is **great**,
**Goodness** is **different**,
**Goodness** is **concordant**.

**Greatness** is **good**,
**Greatness** is **different**,
**Greatness** is **concordant**.

**Difference** is **good**,
**Difference** is **great**,
**Difference** is **concordant**.

**Concordance** is **good**,
**Concordance** is **great**,
**Concordance** is **different**.

<note>The author is demonstrating how the "chambers" (cells in a combinatorial grid) function by pairing different principles—represented by letters—to create logical statements.</note>

By this same reasoning, all the remaining chambers can be fully worked out.

Furthermore, it should be known that every single proposition contains two implicit questions regarding the nature of the letters forming that chamber. Therefore, in any single chamber, there are twenty-four questions; thus, in the entire figure, there are four hundred and thirty-two propositions, and eight hundred and sixty-four questions. <note>The system uses these mathematical permutations to ensure that every possible logical relationship between concepts is explored.</note>

The condition of this figure is such that one chamber is never in opposition to another; rather, they mutually assist and prove one another.

<note>original: "Bonitas" (Goodness), "Magnitudo" (Greatness/Magnitude), "Differentia" (Difference), "Concordantia" (Concordance/Agreement), "camera" (chamber/cell), "propositiones" (propositions), "quæstiones" (questions), "figura tertia" (the third figure).</note>

<summary>This page explains the combinatorial logic of the Third Figure, detailing how twelve propositions and twenty-four questions are derived from each chamber to create a comprehensive system of proof.</summary>
<keywords>combinatorial logic, Ramon Llull, Third Figure, propositions, goodness, greatness, difference, concordance, mathematical philosophy</keywords>

# Art Cabinet of the Academy: Seventh Class

The **Fourth Figure** prepares the **Intellect** <note>Latin: *Intellectus*; in this context, it refers to the human capacity for reasoning and understanding</note> for investigating and discovering, for raising objections, for proving, and for determining truths through necessary conclusions. This figure consists of three circles, of which the uppermost is always stationary, while the two lower circles are movable.

Furthermore, this figure contains within itself the three preceding figures; therefore, it is more general in its application. In every **compartment** <note>Latin: *camera*; literally "room," used here to describe the individual cells or spaces within the mnemonic device</note> of this figure, there are three letters. The first letter represents those things placed in the position of the **subject** in a proposition; the third represents those in the position of the **predicate**; and finally, the middle letter demonstrates the **middle term** <note>The "middle term" is the crucial link in a syllogism that connects the subject and the predicate to reach a logical conclusion</note>.

There follows a **table** for this figure consisting of eighty-one columns. Each column contains nine compartments, which together make seven hundred and twenty-nine compartments. By moving through these principles and rules, the Intellect renders itself so universal and general that no **Sophist** <note>A person who uses clever but false arguments; a deceptive reasoner</note> can stand against it.

<summary>This page describes the Fourth Figure of a logical system, explaining its mechanical structure of rotating circles and its use in forming perfect syllogisms to defeat false reasoning.</summary>
<keywords>Fourth Figure, logic, syllogism, middle term, mnemonic circles, Art of Memory, reasoning, Sophistry</keywords>
