->Artistic Cabinet of the Academy, Class VII.<-

The condition of this figure is such that only those chambers should be selected which are most applicable to the purpose at hand. For here, one must not strive for quantity, but for weight <note>original: "non numero, sed pondere," a common rhetorical maxim suggesting quality over quantity</note>; nor should a hundred axioms be chosen if ninety of them are to be discarded as irrelevant.

This observation belongs to Fonseca <note>Pedro da Fonseca (1528–1599), a Portuguese Jesuit philosopher known as the "Portuguese Aristotle"</note>, who says in Book 7 of his *Institutions of Dialectic*: *“When a question has been proposed, then the subject, the predicate, and even the entire combination must be led through the dialectical 'places' <note>term: 'places' (loci) - these are mental 'locations' or categories, like 'cause' or 'effect,' used to find arguments</note>, so that all arguments pertaining to the matter may present themselves as if at a given signal. For it will not be necessary to knock on every door or search every hiding place, since those things which contain any truth will come forward of their own accord from their individual places at each summons. Indeed, such a crowd of useless arguments will often follow you, even as you run quickly through all the places, that it will be no small labor to free yourself from them. On the other hand, questions will sometimes be proposed for which only a very few places will supply arguments; in such cases, however much you knock at the doors of the rest, you should hope for no help from there. Therefore, by this art of 'places,' arguments are not to be compelled, but rather invited. Otherwise, it is to be feared that this path and method—which was devised for easily seeking out and finding arguments—will instead become a hindrance to genius. Arguments which reason itself, wandering freely, would find with little effort, might otherwise never be attained when bound by these laws as if by shackles. For this art was not sought out to bind nature in chains, but to lead it back to the path when it wanders in certain steps.”* It must be noted, moreover, that the handed-down 'places' supply not only the material for every...

of proof, but also of *amplification* <note>Latin: amplificationis. In rhetoric, this refers to the expansion of an argument to emphasize its importance or to clarify its meaning.</note>. For a matter is *amplified* by a massing together of definitions, or rather descriptions; by the enumeration of its parts; by its antecedents, consequences, and the abundance of circumstances; and by the comparison of many similar, greater, smaller, equal, dissimilar, contrary things, and testimonies.

Finally, if you intend to achieve knowledge through this revolution <note>Latin: reuolutionem. Likely referring to the rotating combinatorial wheels used in Lullian logic to generate arguments.</note> of the fourth figure—that is, to defend and multiply a proposition—you must first find the proposition itself. Therefore, we must now deal with the discovery of propositions.

### Lecture VII.

#### On the Discovery of Propositions.

1. *Discovery of the subject.* 2. *Discovery of the predicate.* 3. *Combination of both.*

<margin>1</margin>
If you wish to find a proposition, first investigate the simple subject of the knowledge, then the predicate, by moving the circles <note>The "circles" refer to the "Volvelles" or rotating paper wheels used in the Art of Ramon Lull to combine different logical categories.</note>.

If, however, you already have the subject, first run through the circle of Subjects. If it is there, it will either be explicitly present—such as God and Angel—or implicitly contained under a general and explicit term, such as ox, donkey, fish, or bird being contained under *Sensitive Being* <note>Latin: Sensitiuo. Referring to the category of animals capable of sensation.</note>.

But if it does not occur in the circle of Subjects (S.), one must proceed to the circle of Accidents <note>In logic, "accidents" are attributes that are not essential to the nature of a thing, such as its color or size.</note>.

Finally, if it is found in neither, the circles of predicates must be traversed.

<summary>This page explains how to expand an argument through rhetorical amplification and introduces the method for discovering new logical propositions using rotating combinatorial circles. It details how to locate a subject within hierarchical categories, moving from explicit terms to general classifications or accidental properties.</summary>

<keywords>logic, rhetoric, amplification, fourth figure, discovery of propositions, subject and predicate, Lullian circles, classification of beings, accidents</keywords>

->Art Cabinet of the Academy, VII Class.<-

Often, however, we find a <term>subject</term><note>The "subject" is the primary topic or thing being studied.</note> without a circular discourse when we encounter a sensible object through the service of the senses—both external, such as when we see, hear, or touch something, and internal, such as when we remember something.

Once the subject of a <term>science</term><note>In this context, "science" refers to a systematic body of knowledge or a specific field of study.</note> has been found, it is also necessary to investigate those things which can be said and known about that subject; you achieve this by also moving the circles.

<margin>3</margin>Once the subject and the <term>predicate</term><note>The "predicate" is the quality or action attributed to the subject in a statement.</note> have been found, you will establish an affirming or denying proposition, the defense of which follows.

### Lecture VIII.

#### Concerning the defense of the discovered proposition.

1. *What the defense of a proposition is. 2. The laws of confirmation. 3. The easiest method of arguing, and also of defining and demonstrating. 4. The division of Lullian terms into three circles. 5. The question to be proved or defended. 6. The art of memory and how to read and listen with profit.*

<margin>1 2</margin>We say that propositions are defended by the confirmation of the truth and the refutation of the false. These, moreover, are the laws of confirmation:

If you wish to confirm a proposition, resolve it into its own terms, and lead each one of them through the circles, because confirmation is nothing other than the coherence of several propositions.

->### ACADEMIC KABBALISTIC ART: SEVENTH CLASS<-

II. Know that it is greatly beneficial for finding means and arguments to paraphrase a proposition in various ways. For example, if someone wishes to explain the essence of the Gospel found in John 3 <note>referring to John 3:16</note>, they might fashion a paraphrase such as this:

> Thus **God**, to whom we are nothing by comparison, **loved the world** which He could have hated, **that He might give** a Mediator whom He not only could have denied us, but could have even appointed as a judge to condemn us; a **Son**, not a servant; not one from among many, but the **only begotten**; **so that whoever believes in him**—who died—and indeed believes not by his own strength but by the grace of God, **should not perish, but have life**, not temporary for a month or a few years, but **eternal**. And thus, when opposites are placed side by side, they shine forth more clearly.

<margin>3</margin>III. The preceding table of the fourth figure will show you the easiest method of all for constructing arguments; concerning this, there are these practical rules:

**First.** If you have a certain proposition to be confirmed, inquire from which circle the <term>subject</term> <note>the topic being discussed</note> and the <term>predicate</term> <note>what is being said about the topic</note> are taken, so that you may find the letter for each.

**Second.** After the letters of both the subject and the predicate are ready in the circles, look at the <term>combination</term> <note>the pairing of symbolic letters in the system</note> shown by the letter of the subject. For example, if the subject is C, the second combination (whose mark is also C) should be applied.

**Third.** Examine the columns; the column in which you find the last letter to be the characteristic of the predicate contains the <term>chambers of arguments</term> <note>specific logical cells or categories</note> by which the present proposition can be confirmed.

->### ACADEMIC KABBALISTIC ART: SEVENTH CLASS<-

**Fourth.** After you are certain about the column of combination, you will first consider the middle letter of each individual chamber in that column, and you will find nine of them. Then, as you begin the confirmation, examine each middle letter according to the nature of the four circles. For example, if the middle letter is **B**, the argument is drawn from circle **S** (from Deity), from circle **A** (from Goodness), from circle **T** (from Difference), and from circle **Q** (from Possibility). For this middle letter shows the fountain from which the material of the argument flows, and therefore it must be sought there. <note>The author is describing the combinatorial logic of Ramon Llull, where letters represent divine attributes or logical categories that can be rotated on paper wheels (circles) to generate philosophical truths.</note>

**Fifth.** Because the middle letter belongs not only to the subject but also to the predicate, the discourse through the four circles **S, A, T, Q** must be performed twice—namely, for the sake of the former and the latter.

**Sixth.** Through the fourth figure, which is completed by three circles, we are able to define and demonstrate, or argue. We define when we take the genus along with the difference, as when we say, *man is a rational animal* <note>original: "homo est animal rationale"</note>, which terms are signified by **E** and **G** of circle **S**. We demonstrate or argue when we assume various means between the subject and the predicate to demonstrate that the meaning signified by the element of the smallest circle is predicated of the meaning signified by the element of the largest circle; thus, the middle circle always signifies the cause or the medium because of which the predicate is said of the subject. To understand this more clearly, you will note that all Lullian terms <note><term>Lullian terms</term>: The specific set of philosophical concepts—like Goodness, Greatness, and Power—defined by the 13th-century mystic Ramon Llull.</note> are divided into three circles: the largest, which is assigned to the subject; the middle, from which the cause or me-

the medium <note>The "middle term" in a syllogism, which connects the subject and the predicate.</note> because of which the predicate is present in the subject, and into the smallest or lowest circle which is owed to the predicates; all of these circles are received by the individual figures. For example, in this proposition *God is good*, the first term *God* is under the letter B of circle S. The second, namely *good*, is under the letter B of circle A. Therefore, B of circle S takes on the nature of the largest circle: B of circle A, however, takes on the nature of the smallest circle. Under these letters—that is, under the terms *God* and *good*—the middle circle must be rotated, which first takes on the nature of the absolute predicates of Figure A, and secondly the relative predicates of Figure T. Finally, it takes on the nature of the questions and rules of Figure Q. I provide examples below.

### Question to be proven or defended: Whether goodness is great: *The affirmative is demonstrated first through Figure A.*

In this question, you have *goodness* as the subject and *great* as the predicate. Therefore, arrange the element B in the upper circle signifying the subject, which is *goodness*, and C of the lowest circle signifying the predicate, which is *great*. To prove this, a medium or cause must be found. Therefore, while the highest and lowest circles remain immobile, let the other elements or terms be brought between those two elements—namely B and C, or the terms *goodness* and *great*—brought, I say, in order by revolving the middle circle, and

<meta>This page continues the logical demonstrations of the Art of Ramon Llull, specifically applying the "Art of the Kabbalah" to the attributes of the Academy. It follows the systematic combination of terms to prove the greatness of divine or transcendental qualities.</meta>

<header>THE KABBALISTIC ART OF THE ACADEMY. SEVENTH CLASS.</header>

First, regarding the B. of Figure A <note>In Lullian logic, 'B' usually represents Goodness and 'Figure A' represents the circle of Divine Attributes</note>, by applying this combination Bbc <note>A formulaic combination of terms: Goodness (B), Greatness (b), and Concordance (c)</note>. That which diffuses itself into everything is great: and yet goodness diffuses itself into all things, therefore it is great.

2. Through bcc <note>Greatness, Concordance, Concordance</note>. That which can be extended is great.

3. Through bdc <note>Greatness, Duration, Concordance</note>. That which is durable is great.

4. Through bec <note>Greatness, Power, Concordance</note>. That which has power over another, etc.

5. Through bfc <note>Greatness, Wisdom, Concordance</note>. Because it effects the knowledge of the highest things.

6. Through bgc <note>Greatness, Will, Concordance</note>. Because it conquers all things.

7. Through bhc <note>Greatness, Virtue, Concordance</note>. Because it gives virtue <note>or strength/power</note> to all things.

8. Through bik <note>Greatness, Truth, Glory</note>. Because it preserves the truth.

9. Through bkc <note>Greatness, Glory, Concordance</note>. Because it produces glory.

### Secondly, it is demonstrated through Figure T.

1. Through the chamber <term>camera</term> <note>A 'chamber' refers to the intersection of two or more principles in the Lullian combinatorial wheel</note> bbc. That which attributes to each its own is great: but such is goodness.

2. Through bcc. Because it unites all things.

3. Through bdc. Because it resists adversities.

## On the Formation of Arguments.

*1. How many forms of argument there are. 2. What an Example is. 3. What Induction is. 4. What an Enthymeme is. 5. What a Syllogism is. 6. The elements of a Syllogism.*

# ACADEMIC CABINET ARTICLE VII. CLASS.

...will be the cause of the loss of grace: whether that be of a fictional matter, such as parables, of which there are frequent examples in the sacred scriptures. <note>The author is likely concluding a previous point about how metaphors or parables function as a type of argument in theology.</note>

3 **Induction** <term>Induction: a method of reasoning that moves from specific instances to a general conclusion</term> is an argumentation in which, from a sufficient enumeration of singular instances, something universal is concluded in this manner: this man, and this one, and that one, and the rest all reason; therefore, every man reasons.

4 **Enthymeme** <term>Enthymeme: a "truncated" syllogism where one premise is unstated, often used in everyday speech or rhetoric</term> is an argumentation in which one of the two premises is kept silent, while the other is expressed; for which reason it can deservedly be called an imperfect Syllogism. Indeed, if that proposition which is withheld were added, it would become a complete Syllogism: for example, this is an Enthymeme:
> A human feels,
> Therefore, he is an animal. <note>The hidden premise here is "All things that feel are animals."</note>

5 **Syllogism** (which is the most noble species of argumentation) is defined by Aristotle <note>original: "Aristotele"</note> as a Discourse in which, certain things having been laid down, something else necessarily follows from those things which have been laid down: that is, a Syllogism is an argumentation in which, two propositions having been sent before in the antecedent, a third—which is called the Conclusion or the consequent—necessarily follows, being distinct from those two preceding ones, provided that the two preceding ones have been admitted or granted. In this, the Syllogism differs greatly from the Enthymeme; in the latter, even if the antecedent is granted, the consequent can be denied. The reason for this is that in a Syllogism, the formal consequence or inference is necessary because of the connection and arrangement of the "middle term" <term>Middle Term: the concept that links the two premises together to prove the conclusion</term> with the terms from which the Conclusion consists, which is not the case in an Enthymeme, in which...

<summary>This page defines and distinguishes between three primary forms of logical argument: induction, the enthymeme, and the syllogism, citing Aristotle's foundational definition of the latter.</summary>
<keywords>logic, induction, enthymeme, syllogism, Aristotle, argumentation, premises, conclusion</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on logical structures, specifically focusing on the formal components of a syllogism.</meta>

# Art Cabinet of the Academy, VII Class

such a connection is not found, just as it is not found in other types of logical consequences.

<margin>6</margin>From what has been said about the Syllogism <note>A form of reasoning where a conclusion is drawn from two given or assumed premises</note>, you may gather that the Elements or principles from which a Syllogism consists, and into which it is resolved, are propositions and terms.

The Proposition is threefold; the first two have the nature of the antecedent <note>the "if" or starting part of a logical statement</note>, and are commonly called premises, because they are placed before the Conclusion: the last one, however, has the nature of the Consequent, and is called the Conclusion, because it is necessarily inferred or concluded from the granted premises. Of the two premises, one is called the Major, which is orderly placed in the first position: the other is the Minor, or the Sumption or Assumption, which follows the Major.

The Term is called either Extreme or Middle.

The Extreme is that which is placed in the consequent after it has been taken once in the antecedent. It is, moreover, twofold: for one is called the major, which is taken with the middle term in the major proposition, and is the predicate <note>the part of a sentence that says something about the subject</note> in the Conclusion; the other is the minor, which is taken with the middle term in the minor proposition, and is the subject in the Conclusion—which is to be understood in the direct modes of arguing. The Middle term, or the argument, is the reason for proving the Conclusion; it is placed with both extremes in the antecedent, but it never enters the Conclusion, as is evident in this Syllogism:

->Every animal is a substance,<-
->Man is an animal,<-
->Therefore, man is a substance.<-

Consult, if you wish, the logicians regarding the Syllogistic figure.

<summary>This page defines the structural components of a syllogism, identifying the three types of propositions (Major, Minor, and Conclusion) and the three types of terms (Major, Minor, and Middle).</summary>
<keywords>Logic, Syllogism, Premises, Conclusion, Major Term, Minor Term, Middle Term, Aristotelian Logic</keywords>

# Academic Cabinet of the Art, Class VIII

Regarding solutions of this kind:

->Solutions are either { Direct, or Indirect.<-

Direct solutions occur when the response itself is gathered immediately from the terms of this Art.

Indirect solutions occur when that which is found as a response is not explicitly explained within the Art, but is discovered and thought out according to the Art's terms.

An example of both types of solution would be if, for instance, it is asked: *What is an Angel?* First, it is answered directly according to the rule of "Quiddity" <note>The "whatness" or essence of a thing</note> through Figure **A.** *An Angel is that which acts by its own power and knows by its own wisdom, with no sense commanding it or external object moving it.* Indirectly, however, according to the same rule of quiddity, the answer is not found expressly from those things placed in the Art, but is discovered within it—as from Figure **T.** through the angles **B. C.** For through angle **C.** something is found in the place of a "genus" <note>A general category or class</note> in which the *Angel* agrees with another thing, as if it were said: *An Angel is a spirit.* Then, through **B.** the "difference" <note>The specific characteristic that distinguishes a species from others in the same genus</note> is found by dividing that which was found in the place of the genus, or by separating the proposed subject from something else contained under that same genus; thus, a definition emerges such as: *An Angel is a spirit not joined to a body.* Alternatively, it is answered through the third species **C.** of Figure **Q.** and the principles **C. H.** of Figure **T.** *An Angel is a creature which is most like unto God;* or even through rule **D.** of the same Figure **Q.** *An Angel is a spirit consisting of intellect, memory, and will, having no appetite for* <insert>to</insert>

*What is the soul?* It is answered through G of Figure T. It is that essence which perfects the body in which it is joined.

*What is heaven?* It is answered through B and H of Figure T. It is that corporeal substance which is greater than all others.

*What is substance?* It is answered through the definition of E in Circle A. It is a being existing and acting naturally by itself.

*What is a body?* It is answered through the third species of C in Figure Q. It is a substance in a place, occupying space.

# Art Cabinet of the Academy, Class VIII.

## LECTURE II.

### On the Refutation of Arguments.

1. *In how many ways the Refutation of Arguments may be accomplished.*

The Refutation <note>original: "Dissolutio"</note> of arguments is achieved in three ways. For we either dissolve fallacies of words or of meanings by showing the falsehood of the argument due to a defect it possesses in its form or in its matter, or in its distinction, construction, conjunction, and other such things.

Or, we dissolve the argument by showing a diversity of subjects, principles, means, or causes; or that these things apply to one case in one way, and to another case in a different way.

Or, we dissolve the argument by disproving it directly, showing from other principles or necessary facts that what it concludes or says is false, or that this or that inconsistency follows. This is done by dissolving and disproving the reasoning of the argument, or by denying the author or the authority—either because the author is not to be trusted, or because they are rejected by another or greater authority—or by bringing forward another stronger or contrary reason, or by making a specific exception to a general rule.

However, when we are unable to dilute the reasons opposed to us, then "accidental topics" <term>accidental topics: rhetorical strategies used when a direct logical refutation is impossible</term> must be used, namely:

By an attack on a more serious matter, so that the listener believes the remaining points to be trivial.

<vocab>Refutation of Arguments, fallacy, form, matter, principles, authority</vocab>

<summary>This lecture outlines three primary methods for refuting logical arguments: identifying formal flaws, distinguishing between different contexts or causes, and challenging the underlying authority or truth of the claims.</summary>
<keywords>logic, rhetoric, refutation, fallacies, academic debate, authority, argumentation</keywords>

--- Page 242 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on rhetorical strategies for refuting an opponent's arguments, likely following a list of other techniques.</meta>

# ACADEMIC CABINET OF ARTS: SEVENTH CLASS

By **Interruption**, by showing that we are passing over a matter for the sake of brevity, and that we would satisfy the point more fully if time were granted.

By **Promise of a solution**, by saying many things afterward, with the multitude of which we so overwhelm and stun the listener that he becomes unmindful of the promised solution. <note>This describes a tactical distraction where the speaker buries a weak point under a mountain of subsequent words.</note>

By **Weakening**, by showing that what was presented as possible was actually impossible, and things of that kind.

By **Admonition**, by which we show that if the adversary persists in that argument, he will fall into various dangers and inconveniences.

By **Questioning** of two or more things, from which the adversary is likely to choose the wrong one, or to which he may be unable to respond.

By **Mockery**, by dissolving the arguments into laughter and turning them back against the adversaries.

By **Disdain**, by saying that the arguments are beside the point, unworthy of learned men, and ought to be passed over.

Finally, by **Suspicion**; for those things which we cannot disprove, we shall bespatter with suspicion.

<gloss>Interruption, Promise of a solution, Weakening, Admonition, Questioning, Mockery, Disdain, Suspicion, Rhetoric</gloss>

<keywords>rhetoric, argumentation, persuasion, debate tactics, logic, academic arts</keywords>

# ACADEMIC CABINET ARTICLE IX. CLASS.

The **Introduction** <note>original: "Exordium"</note> is a speech that prepares the mind of the listener for the rest of the discourse; this is achieved if we make the listener well-disposed, attentive, and receptive to learning.

We win over a **well-disposed** <note>original: "beneuolum"</note> listener by focusing on our own character, the character of the listeners, the character of our opponents, or the matter itself—specifically when we praise our own merits without arrogance and the merits of others without flattery. Likewise, we succeed when we stir up hatred, enmity, contempt, and envy toward our enemies, and when we present the matter in such a way that the minds of the listeners are led away from what they hate toward what they love.

We make the listener **attentive** if we promise that we are about to speak of great, new, and unusual things, using stirring words and encouraging the listeners to listen closely.

Furthermore, we make the listener **receptive to learning** <note>original: "docilem"</note> when we briefly embrace the main point of the matter we are discussing. In speeches, this part is called the **Theme** or **Thesis**, that is, the **Proposition**. We perform all these actions either openly or through **Insinuation**, by concealing our intent through the subtle insertion of words.

<margin>3</margin> Every Introduction must be drawn from the very heart of the matter under discussion, so that it properly coheres with the narrative of the facts and appears to be born from the subject itself. It should not be trivial or common, nor should it be too long-winded, nor extended with more words or sentences than is sufficient; nor should it be composed of overly elaborate or affected words, lest it diminish the speaker's credibility.

<margin>4</margin> Moreover, so that you may carry out everything correctly, it is necessary to consider the character of the listeners, and in whose presence [you speak].

<term>Exordium: The formal opening of a speech.</term>
<term>Auditor: The listener or member of the audience.</term>
<term>Beneuolum: A well-disposed or friendly state of mind in the audience.</term>
<term>Attentum: An attentive or focused state of mind.</term>
<term>Docilem: A state of being ready to be taught or receptive to information.</term>
<term>Thema / Thesis / Propositum: The central argument or subject of the discourse.</term>
<term>Insinuatio: A subtle or indirect approach used when the audience might be hostile to the speaker's topic.</term>

<summary>This page outlines the rhetorical requirements for a successful introduction, emphasizing the need to make the audience friendly, attentive, and ready to learn. It warns against using overly flowery language or generic openings that do not relate directly to the specific topic at hand.</summary>
<keywords>rhetoric, introduction, exordium, public speaking, persuasion, audience engagement, thesis statement</keywords>

# ACADEMIC CABINET ARTICLE IX. CLASS.

<note>original: "ART. KAB. ACAD. IX. CLASSIS." refers to the ninth category of the academic collection, likely dedicated to Rhetoric.</note>

...what, in the opinion of the listeners, in what place, or time, and according to their reasoning, the speech should be moderated and tempered: For in the **Introductions** <note>original: "Exordiis"</note> there is, as it were, the first acquaintance and recommendation of the **Orator** <term>Orator: a public speaker trained in the art of persuasion</term> which ought immediately to soothe and entice the one who hears.

<margin>5</margin> The **Statement of Facts** <note>original: "Narratio"</note> is the explanation of things and, as it were, a certain seat and foundation for establishing credibility. It ought, moreover, to be *brief, clear, and probable*, so that the listener may understand, remember, and believe.

The Statement of Facts will be brief if it consists of simple words, and each thing is said only once, and if we cut back everything which, if removed, would detract nothing from the understanding nor the truth.

It will be clear if it is told with common words, in order, and with the sequence of time preserved and not interrupted.

It will be probable if those things which are told are consistent with the persons, the times, and the places.

It greatly pleases **Cicero** <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero (106–43 BCE), the Roman statesman and preeminent authority on Latin rhetoric</note> that the statement of facts be pleasant and sweet; and he says that a statement is sweet which contains wonders, expectations, the conversations of persons, sorrows, anger, fear, and desires.

<margin>6</margin> But when should the Statement of Facts be used? It should be noted that there are three kinds of Causes or Controversies: **Judicial**, **Deliberative**, and **Ornamental** <note>original: "Iudicij, Deliberationis & Exornationis"</note>; the latter, because it is mostly applied to praises, takes its proper name from "praise" <note>often called Epideictic oratory</note>. In Judicial matters, however, it was a matter of judgment when the Statement of Facts should or should not be used; for if the matter were already known, or if there were no doubt as to what had been done, the statement was not employed; nor if the opponent had already told—

<summary>This page outlines the classical rhetorical requirements for the Introduction and the Statement of Facts, emphasizing Cicero's standards for brevity, clarity, and emotional engagement. It begins to categorize the three branches of oratory: judicial, deliberative, and demonstrative.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of rhetorical structure, specifically focusing on the transition from the introduction to the narrative and the division of arguments.</meta>

<header>Artistic Cabinet of the Academy. IX. Class. (Page 212)</header>

...unless it was being refuted. In the **Embellishment** <note>original: "Exornatione"; refers to the stylistic refinement of a speech</note>, there will be no **Narrative** <note>original: "Narratio"; the statement of facts in a case</note> that necessarily follows the **Introduction**, nor is there often much to be narrated in a **Deliberation** <note>original: "deliberatione"; a speech given to persuade an audience toward a course of action</note>. For Narrative concerns past or present matters, whereas **Persuasion** <note>original: "Suasio"</note> concerns the future. Certainly, a private Deliberation will never require a narrative of the matter upon which an opinion is to be given, because no one is ignorant of the subject they are consulting about; however, many external things pertaining to the Deliberation can be narrated. In public assemblies, that narrative which explains the order of the matter is also often necessary.

<margin>7</margin> **Division** <note>original: "Diuisio"</note> is a brief enumeration of the matters about which we are going to speak, so that the listeners may hold certain points in mind, and know that once these have been said, the speech is finished.

### Lecture III.

#### Concerning Deduction, the second part.

<margin>1.</margin> *1. What Deduction is. 2. The two-fold Deduction. 3. The three-fold process in deducing a subject. 4. The change of order.*

<margin>1</margin> **Deduction** <note>original: "Deductio"; here meaning the systematic development or "leading out" of an argument</note> is the part of the art by which it is shown how we ought to receive and derive speeches, arguments, or proofs. From this we
<margin>2</margin> elicit a two-fold course of deduction.

->Namely { Conversational,<-
->{ Argumentative.<-

<margin>3</margin> Therefore, in deducing any **Subject**, whether it be a saying, a speech, a proposition, or a question...

It also introduces the concept of Deduction as the systematic development of arguments in either a conversational or argumentative style.</summary>
<keywords>rhetoric, Narrative, Deliberation, Division, Deduction, public speaking, argumentation, classical education</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Art of the Academy," specifically focusing on the practical application and "deduction" of concepts within the Kabbalistic system.</meta>

# Art of the Academy: Ninth Class

<note>The header refers to the classification system of the academic cabinet or library where this manuscript was organized.</note>

We employ various methods.

First, by a straightforward process derived from the art itself, maintaining the established order of principles.

Second, by a divisive process <note>Latin: "processu divisivo," a method of breaking a subject down into its component parts</note>, whether it concerns a proposition, a physical thing, or both, through all the figures. Thus, if the subject is complex—such as a specific proposition or sentence—its parts, which have been distinguished through division, should first be briefly defined. Their essence <term>quiddity<note>The "what-ness" or essential nature of a thing.</note></term> should be explained, and each part should be treated through questions, principles, and subjects, as in the previous type of deduction.

Third and finally, we employ an external process. This occurs when one proposition or thing is deduced through another; for example, if **Justice** is the subject, it may be deduced through this verse:

> *I sing of arms and the man* <note>The famous opening line of Virgil’s Aeneid: "Arma virumque cano."</note>

by saying that through **Justice**, a man is *armed* against vices and temptations, and through the same, his soul is made *manly*, lest he succumb to the enticements of the senses and womanly <note>The author uses "muliebribus" (womanly/effeminate) in the historical context of weakness or lack of stoic resolve.</note> affections; finally, that on account of justice, he acquires for himself perpetual praise and glory, to be praised and sung of by all for posterity as an example of the good.

In a similar fashion, **Wisdom** may be the subject and be deduced through that verse:

> *O Queen, to whom Jupiter [granted] to found a new city.* <note>A quote from Virgil’s Aeneid (Book 1, Line 522), where Dido is addressed.</note>

by saying that **Wisdom** is a *queen* and *new* (never growing old), and that she *founds a city*, namely the **Church**. Regarding this, the sayings of Solomon occur to us, where he says that Wisdom has built a house for herself and has hewn out seven columns <note>A reference to Proverbs 9:1.</note>, and many more things in this manner.

From these things which have been said, the order of deduction is sufficiently clear—

<column-break/>

<summary>This page outlines three methods for deductive reasoning within a Kabbalistic academic framework, including a creative "external process" that uses classical poetry (Virgil) to explain moral and theological concepts like Justice and Wisdom.</summary>
<keywords>deduction, Justice, Wisdom, Virgil, Aeneid, Solomon, allegory, mnemonic methods, academic art</keywords>

Art Cabinet of the Academy. IX. Class.

of the action; but it must be observed that we should change the order by mixing the figures, and jumping from one place to another as decoration or necessity demands. This should be done in such a way that simple matters are treated first before arriving at complex ones, and then subsequently in a mixed fashion; sometimes discussing the whole matter at once, and sometimes the individual parts.

### Lecture IV.

->On the Conclusion.<-

->*1. What a Conclusion is. 2. In how many ways it may be made.*<-

The Conclusion is the final part of the entire speech and the boundary of the discourse, refreshing the listener's memory and moving their soul. <note>The author emphasizes that a conclusion serves both a cognitive function (memory) and an emotional one (moving the soul).</note>
It can, however, be performed in three ways.
Either, most importantly, by some argument in which the entire strength of the speech resides, and thus it is concluded. Or by enumeration, by gathering in order and reminding the audience of the things about which we have spoken; and this enumeration <term>enumeration: a brief recap of main points</term> ought to be brief so that it is committed to memory, not repeated in full, nor should its beginning be taken from the Introduction <note>original: "Exordium"</note>, but from the division of the topics.
Or, one must use Amplification <term>amplification: the art of expanding an argument to emphasize its importance</term> by stirring lukewarm minds, calming those who are agitated, and encouraging the listeners to retain these things; and by inciting hatred and envy against those by whom these things have been violated, and finally by moving the audience to pity. All these things are done by multiplying and amplifying through all parts of the art according to a double and manifold figure, and thus concluding the speech. Each of these becomes clear in the following example.

<vocab>Conclusion, speech, discourse, enumeration, Introduction, division, Amplification</vocab>

<summary>This page outlines the theory of the rhetorical conclusion, defining it as the final boundary of a speech intended to refresh the listener's memory and stir their emotions through three specific methods: strong argument, brief enumeration, or emotional amplification.</summary>
<keywords>rhetoric, conclusion, memory, emotion, enumeration, amplification, academic lecture, oratory</keywords>

# ACADEMIC CABINET OF ARTS. NINTH CLASS.
O iiij

## LECTURE V.

### *Concerning the fourth and final part of this last Class, namely, by the example of the aforementioned.*

The example that follows is that of Andreas Canter <note>original: "Andreæ Canterij"; likely the Frisian scholar known for his prodigious memory</note> which, although it may lack elegance and ornamentation, nevertheless demonstrates the method of arrangement and application according to the rules that have been handed down. This is done according to a plain and simple deduction, the subject of which is Fasting, which is first derived through Figure A.

### A Discourse on Fasting.
->*The Introduction.*<-

As I am about to deliver a discourse on Fasting, I shall begin from this word of the Gospel: *The days will come when the bridegroom shall be taken away from the children of the wedding, and then they shall fast.* <note>Matthew 9:15</note> By this word, we understand that Fasting itself was openly established and commanded by the Lord for the Apostles—who were then present as examples—and through them for the entire Christian Church. For so we read in another place: *What I say to you, I say to all: watch.* <note>Mark 13:37</note> Who, therefore, being warned by this commandment of the Lord, would not strive to fast, when he perceives that Fasting was instituted by Christ so that we might reach that eternal fullness promised to those who fast, as the Lord says: *Blessed are you who hunger now, etc.* <note>Luke 6:21</note> For Fasting is the abstinence from food and drink, drawing away those things which belong to the flesh so that it may preserve those things which belong to the spirit; it makes its observer more sublime than himself, and renders him spiritual instead of bodily. From this it follows—

<summary>This page introduces a practical rhetorical example by Andreas Canter regarding the spiritual discipline of fasting, framed as a divine command from the Gospels. It defines fasting as a means to elevate the spirit over the flesh in preparation for eternal fulfillment.</summary>
<keywords>Fasting, Andreas Canter, Rhetoric, Gospel of Matthew, Spiritual Discipline, Christian Church, Abstinence</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the theological discourse on the nature of fasting, transitioning from the spiritual definition of fasting to its practical and moral application.</meta>

ACADEMIC CABINET ARTICLE IX. CLASS.
Page 216
B

because Fasting unites man with God, for God is also a Spirit.

->*The Division*<-

Furthermore, the Fasting by which anyone is united to God ought to be one single thing, not divided, but composed of all its parts; namely, that just as the body fasts from food, so the soul fasts from sins. Thus the whole man should fast from all his <term>concupiscences</term> <note>Strong desires or lusts that lead away from spiritual health</note>: the hair from superfluous grooming, the eye from empty sights, the ears from hearing wicked words, the nostrils from voluptuous scents, and the tongue should fast not only from food and the delights of flavors, but also from every empty speech and idle word. Every ungodly fuel—Envy, Greed, and every evil lust—should depart from the heart, for this is the fasting of the heart. Let the hand not touch what is forbidden, let the feet not set out toward a cause for stumbling, and thus the feet and hands will fast. For all these things, in their integrity, constitute the unity and perfection of Fasting; if one of them should be lacking, it cannot be said that one has fasted rightly.

->*The Deduction*<-

->*And first through figure A.*<-

Therefore, Fasting of this kind is to be embraced by all as a heavenly and spiritual good. But Fasting is not called "good" unless it does good. For what is goodness if not communication? <note>In this context, "communication" refers to the sharing or imparting of goods to others</note> Therefore, he fasts well who bestows the food which he withdraws from himself upon the poor; not he who, like a tax collector, afflicts the poor at the very time he fasts, or is then most hostile to his debtors.

<vocab>Fasting, Division, Deduction, concupiscence, sin, the poor</vocab>

<summary>This page defines "true fasting" as a holistic discipline involving both the body and the soul, requiring every sense and limb to abstain from vice. It concludes by arguing that fasting is only virtuous when the food saved is given to the poor rather than used as an excuse for cruelty or greed.</summary>
<keywords>fasting, spiritual discipline, charity, asceticism, morality, social justice, unity of soul and body</keywords>

# ACADEMIC CABINET ARTICLE IX. CLASS.

As we read in Isaiah, *On the day of your fast, your own will appears in the troubling of your debtors.* <note>original: "In die Ieiunij apparet voluntas in molestandis debitoribus vestris" — a reference to Isaiah 58:3, criticizing those who fast while exploiting others.</note> Then he adds, *this is the fast that I have chosen: break your bread for the hungry, and receive the wandering and the poor into your home.* Unless this goodness is added to the Fast, it will not be a good Fast; and if the Fast is not good, it will not be pleasing to God, who commands nothing and accepts nothing unless it is good, since all good flows from Him.

<margin>C</margin> It is a great Fast to abstain from iniquities <note>sins or gross injustices</note>, because the multitude of iniquities is great, and their battle and warfare against man is also great, so that there may be greater praise in the Fast while so many enemies are clamoring on every side. And for this reason, that Fast is greater because it pertains to the soul, whereas the other pertains to the body and to food; for the soul is more than the body, and sin is more than food.

<margin>D</margin> In Fasting there must also be perseverance, according to that holy proverb, *He who perseveres until the end shall be saved, and he who strives lawfully shall be crowned.* <note>A synthesis of Matthew 24:13 and 2 Timothy 2:5.</note> Hence Moses, when he was about to receive the commandments, continued a fast of forty days, *neither eating bread nor drinking wine.* Therefore, if this perseverance is missing from the Fast, as in other virtues, it diminishes merit and loses the reward.

<margin>E</margin> Furthermore, Fasting makes its possessor powerful in dominating and commanding his own illicit desires <term>concupiscentiis: strong worldly desires or lusts</term>; on the contrary, the abandonment of the same makes him powerless. Hence the Savior says, *This kind of demon is not cast out except by Fasting and prayer.* And the Apostle <note>referring to St. Paul</note> commands, *Abstain from wine...*

<summary>This page argues that true fasting is not merely abstaining from food, but must include acts of charity and the rejection of sin. It emphasizes that spiritual discipline provides the strength necessary to overcome internal desires and external spiritual conflicts.</summary>
<keywords>fasting, Isaiah, Moses, charity, perseverance, spiritual warfare, virtue, soul vs body</keywords>

# ACADEMIC CABINET ARTICLE IX. CLASS.

in which there is excess <note>original: "luxuria"; here referring to unrestrained indulgence or lust</note>. And Jeremiah says, *A belly burning with unmixed wine foams over into lust*. This was shown in the case of Lot, who committed incest because of drunkenness; and in Holofernes, who burned with love for Judith, and thereby lost both his head and his life.

<margin>F</margin> Fasting bestows divine and human wisdom, or certainly disposes one toward it, hence that proverb: *A fat belly, a lean intellect*. This is evident first in Moses, who, by fasting, embraced divine knowledge and the arts. By fasting, Elijah was carried away in a fiery chariot. Likewise, Daniel and the other youths attained knowledge through fasting. And the Apostles, while fasting, received the Holy Spirit. John, by fasting on the island of Patmos, received the most secret revelations.

<margin>G</margin> Fasting must be voluntary, for the abstinence from food itself has no virtue in it unless it is done by the soul's choice. Hence we read that the Lord Jesus entered the desert by the Holy Spirit.

<margin>H</margin> Fasting gives birth to virtues if it is sustained bravely by a courageous person. Hence in Hebrews 11, we read how the saints manfully endured nakedness, misery, and fasting.

<margin>I</margin> Furthermore, one must fast in truth and not in pretense, as the Lord says in the Gospel: *Do not become gloomy like the hypocrites, etc.*

<margin>K</margin> Fasting provides no glory unless it is undertaken for an honorable cause, such as to avoid disgrace; for one must not fast for empty glory, hence that saying: *But you, when you fast, anoint your head and wash your face.*

<summary>This page outlines the moral benefits of fasting, contrasting it with the vices of drunkenness and lust while citing Biblical figures like Moses and Daniel as examples of spiritual wisdom gained through abstinence.</summary>

# Art Cabinet of the Academy, IX. Class.

### Secondly, Fasting is derived through the figure of T.

<margin>B</margin>
Fasting differs within itself. For one kind is spiritual, and the other is bodily; both are required for the perfection of Fasting, so that one may benefit from the support of the other. For bodily fasting without the spiritual is empty, and spiritual fasting without the bodily is often more difficult to observe. Fasting also distinguishes man from the beasts, which are led by their own appetite, so that they set no moderation for themselves in food or drink, nor in any other pleasure when delightful objects are present before them.

<margin>C</margin>
Just as man is distinguished from the beasts through Fasting, so too is he joined to the Angels—and even possesses something greater within himself than the Angels do. This is because abstinence from food and drink belongs to Angels by their very nature, since they are clothed in no body and use no bodily nourishment; man, however, leads a spiritual life through Fasting while dwelling in a corruptible body.

<margin>D</margin>
Just as Fasting makes the one who fasts harmonize with the good Angels, so too it conquers and puts to flight the evil demons, whom gluttony and lust—those wicked doorkeepers—have admitted into the moral courtyard of man. Whence the Savior says, *This kind of demon is not cast out except by fasting and prayer.* <note>original: "Hoc genus dæmoniorum non eiicitur niſi in ieiunio & oratione." A reference to Matthew 17:21.</note>

<margin>E</margin>
Furthermore, we can establish a manifold origin for Fasting. For it was instituted in the law of Justice before the first sin, so that there might be a law by prohibition, and merit through its observance. For the Lord commanded that they should not eat from the tree of the knowledge of good and evil— <note>The text breaks off here at the start of the word "eat" (comederent).</note>

<summary>This page explores the dual nature of fasting as both spiritual and physical, arguing that it elevates humans above animals and aligns them with angelic natures. It also identifies fasting as a spiritual weapon against demons and traces its origins back to the Garden of Eden.</summary>
<keywords>fasting, spiritual vs bodily, angels, demons, gluttony, Garden of Eden, law of justice, temperance</keywords>

# ACADEMIC ART OF THE KABBALAH. NINTH CLASS.

they might heal. It was instituted after sin as a punishment, with the Lord saying, *In the sweat of your face you shall eat your bread, of which you shall often be deprived, etc.* <note>original: "In sudore vultus tui vesceris pane tuo quo sæpe fraudaberis." A reference to Genesis 3:19, though the author adds the element of being "deprived" or "defrauded" of bread.</note> Similarly, we find the forty-day Fast instituted in Moses, in Elijah, in Ezra, in Jewish observance, and likewise in Christ, in the Apostles, in the Popes, and the holy fathers.

<margin>F</margin> Fasting is a medium that joins together those things in man which strive against each other in frequent battle: namely, the soul and the body. For the Apostle <note>Referring to St. Paul in Galatians 5:17.</note> says, *The flesh lusts against the spirit, etc.* Therefore, unless it is restrained by fasting, it will with difficulty be kept under the command of the spirit; a point which the Wise Man hints at in the Proverbs, saying: *He who delicately brings up his servant will find him stubborn.* <note>original: "Qui delicatè nutrit seruum suum, inueniet eum contumacem." From Proverbs 29:21.</note>

<margin>G</margin> The end <note>meaning the "purpose" or "goal"</note> of Fasting is so that the desires of the body may be suppressed, and so that the soul, unhindered by the delight of excessive eating <term>crapulæ: excessive indulgence in food or drink, often leading to a hangover or sluggishness</term>, may be freer to exercise its duty. Similarly, some things are found here concerning the final triangle.

->### Thirdly, Fasting is derived through Figure Q.<-

Whether Fasting is good, and whether it is good to fast.

<margin>D</margin> Regarding what must one fast?

And so, regarding similar matters, one receives the solutions to questions of this kind according to their own rules by mixing Figure A and Figure T, as we have taught above. <note>Figures A and T refer to the combinatorial wheels or diagrams used in this system to generate logical or mystical answers.</note>

### Epilogue of the entire Kabbalistic Art

You have at last, by the grace of the highest *Olympus*, the *Academy of the Kabbalistic Art*, or *Divine Wisdom*. If your soul is satisfied by this, what joy shall I feel?

*Olympus:* Truly, my dear *Morestellus*, I am satisfied. For you have so illuminated this Queen of Sciences that no one seems excluded from attaining this Art. No one of sound mind exists who ought not to love it, and apply themselves to its study with every impulse and all their strength, since it assists all other sciences, and shows the way and opens the door to many discoveries. But why do I linger in the praise of this Art? Success itself proves the matter. Therefore, my *Morestellus*, with what benefit have you graced me? Surely one that is distinguished, singular, and divine; wherefore, as long as I live, I shall hold a grateful memory of you.

->T H E  E N D.<-

<note>original: "Ieiunium" - Fasting; "Ars Kabbalistica" - Kabbalistic Art; "Sapientia diuina" - Divine Wisdom; "Olympus" and "Morestellus" are the interlocutors of the dialogue.</note>

### To the Reader.

I have noted in passing the following errors (Reader) which you shall correct as follows: by reading in line 12 of page 3, *great* <note>original: "magno"</note> instead of "of great"; in line 12 of page 118, *itself* <note>original: "ipsum"</note> instead of "of itself"; in line 7 of page 119, *another's* <note>original: "alieno"</note> instead of "the other"; in the same place, *destitute* <note>original: "destituta"</note> instead of "distinct." Correct other errors as well if they should occur.
